4 Data Storage

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why does storage become the invisible bottleneck that prevents accelerators from reaching their potential?

A training job can leave expensive accelerators idle even when its models fit in memory and its network has ample capacity, because the next batch must arrive before computation needs it and a saved checkpoint must remain usable when a worker fails. Storage becomes an invisible bottleneck whenever accelerator benchmarks begin with data already in place, excluding the work needed to deliver inputs and preserve results. A pipeline that keeps one machine busy can stall once many workers compete for the same data source. A burst of checkpoint writes from one job can delay input reads for others, which turns a local pause into lost progress across the fleet. Adding compute increases demand without expanding the shared system’s ability to serve it. How severely that contention hurts depends on the workload: compact training inputs may be easy to deliver even when saving model state dominates storage traffic, whereas larger samples can make continuous input delivery the limiting factor. Bringing data closer to the accelerator reduces those delivery delays, though it adds staging traffic, capacity cost, and the obligation to preserve complete versions, since a fast local save provides little protection if the only copy disappears with the failed machine. Storage decisions therefore govern both how quickly the fleet advances and how much of that progress survives disruption. In C³ terms, storage design coordinates data movement and durable state so that available compute produces recoverable progress.

Learning Objectives
  • Compare storage demands of full-corpus training, checkpoint writes, and model serving across local and shared tiers
  • Calculate input bandwidth and mean cache service time from workload demand and tier behavior
  • Design shard packaging, prefetching, locality, and direct-storage paths that keep fleet accelerators fed when small files or slow tiers would starve them
  • Select checkpoint staging and replication strategies that balance pause time, recovery risk, and storage cost
  • Specify storage commit and replay contracts that preserve correctness when distributed pipeline stages fail, including the provenance that generated training data must retain
  • Analyze serving-time storage workloads, from bulk model loads and rollout bursts to retrieval indexes bound by random-access IOPS
  • Evaluate staging and tiering choices by total delivery cost, including transfer fees and idle accelerators
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

4.1 The Fuel Line

The dense accelerator nodes of Compute Infrastructure pack eight GPUs delivering petaFLOP/s of aggregate compute, and the InfiniBand fabrics of Network Fabrics connect thousands of such nodes at hundreds of gigabits per second. Within the multi-tier fleet stack of The Fleet Stack, data storage completes that physical foundation, staging training data, model weights, optimizer state, and intermediate checkpoints at the right distance from the accelerator. An engine without fuel is expensive sculpture. The engineering question is how to deliver that fuel fast enough that the accelerators of a training cluster never starve. The running cluster for that question is 256 eight-GPU nodes, 2,048 accelerators in all, deliberately twice the 1,024-GPU (128-node) reference cluster used elsewhere in this book, because the larger fleet makes the pressure on shared storage easier to see. Every worked number for the 175B job uses this running cluster; a side scenario with a different workload or fleet size names its own scale where it appears.

Storage is the physical substrate spanning Compute (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_1\)), Communication (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_2\)), and Coordination (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_3\)).

Consider the running example for the storage analysis, an instance of Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3). A 175-billion parameter language model trains on 1.5 trillion tokens of text: roughly 3 TB in compressed source form, or 6 TB once represented as 4-byte token IDs. That token budget is set upstream of storage. Under the compute-optimal scaling results of AI Scaling Laws, the training-token count grows in proportion to parameter count, so each step up the model-size curve enlarges both the corpus the fleet must hold and the bytes each epoch must read. This corpus already sits below the compute-optimal allocation for a 175B-parameter model, so a compute-optimal run would raise both figures further. The run makes a single pass (one epoch) over this corpus, reading every selected token once in an order determined by the shuffle seed. No small “hot” subset dominates access. Meanwhile, each accelerator processes its local batch in roughly 200 ms, then waits for the next. If storage cannot deliver data within that 200 ms window, the accelerator sits idle, and the organization pays for silicon that produces heat instead of gradients.

The problem is deceptive because storage technology has improved substantially. Non-Volatile Memory Express (NVMe) drives achieve 7 GB/s of sequential throughput, a figure that would have seemed out of reach a decade ago. An H100 moves data internally through high-bandwidth memory (HBM) at 3.35 TB/s, roughly 479× the sequential bandwidth of a single NVMe drive. This ratio contrasts two tiers; it is not the rate at which storage must supply training inputs, because HBM bandwidth also carries repeated weight, activation, and tensor traffic during computation. The storage feed requirement is instead determined by the input-batch bytes each accelerator needs per iteration. Dividing those bytes by the compute window gives one worker’s required delivery rate; summing across workers gives the fleet demand. That rate must be evaluated at each tier using the representation stored there, since compressed inputs and decoded tensors occupy different numbers of bytes. A hierarchy becomes useful when the durable source cannot meet that rate or its latency varies enough to interrupt computation. Faster tiers stage upcoming batches while the durable tier supplies capacity and preserves the source data.

The storage problem joins physics with economics. Physics favors the memory closest to the accelerator, which offers the highest bandwidth in the smallest capacity; economics favors shared storage, which pools far more capacity at lower cost per byte behind additional interfaces and service queues. Device technology, interconnects, and contention then determine the rate a particular consumer actually receives. The engineering task is to place and move data so that the accelerator receives each batch before it is needed. The resulting design problem is quantitative. It asks how fast each tier must be, how deep the pipeline must run, and which bytes are worth keeping close to the accelerator. Improving arithmetic throughput alone does not improve input delivery or checkpoint persistence. Those paths require their own bandwidth and latency budgets.

Over the run, checkpoint writes exceed one epoch of training-data reads by more than 1,000 times.

The canonical training-data footprint for this example is roughly 9 TB across the hierarchy, combining the example’s compressed corpus and tokenized shards. Additional shuffled or packed variants can raise the staging footprint, but the per-epoch training read over 4-byte token IDs is 6 TB. This chapter adopts one resumable mixed-precision Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) checkpoint contract: FP16 model weights, FP32 master weights, and two FP32 Adam moments, for 14 bytes per parameter. This is the 16-byte training state of Node memory partitioning without its 2-byte FP16 gradients, which are recomputed after restore, while scheduler, random-number-generator, and data-loader metadata are negligible at this scale. The resulting checkpoint is 2,450 GB every 10 minutes. Over a 30-day training run on the 256-node cluster, the storage system must deliver 6 TB of tokenized training data in the run’s single epoch, absorb 10.6 PB of checkpoint writes, and stage model weights for evaluation runs. The sections that follow use this same contract for every checkpoint size, duration, retention total, and lifetime-write calculation.

Those numbers force the storage path. ML access patterns first invert the assumptions behind conventional storage systems. That inversion forces a hierarchy from HBM to cold archive. The hierarchy then requires a pipeline equation that sizes delivery and direct data paths that remove host copies. It also requires a checkpoint protocol for the dominant write traffic, commit boundaries that keep every versioned artifact reproducible (generated training data included), and economics that decide which bytes belong at each tier. Serving-time model loading and retrieval indexes are variations on the same fuel-line problem, and devices that move the boundaries between tiers close the chapter. In each workload, the storage system must place the right representation at the right distance before the accelerator asks for it.

Self-Check: Question

  1. An H100 accelerator consumes data from its HBM at roughly \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\), while a single local NVMe drive delivers sequential reads at roughly \(7\text{ GB/s}\). A team is designing the storage infrastructure for a 1,000-accelerator training fleet. Which conclusion follows directly from this bandwidth ratio?

    1. The gap is easily bridged by standard NVMe RAID arrays because sequential read throughput scales without bound across drives.
    2. The roughly \(479\times\) device-bandwidth gap motivates tiered staging, but required input bandwidth must be calculated from batch bytes and compute time rather than equated with HBM traffic.
    3. The bandwidth gap is negligible because large-scale training is entirely compute-bound and rarely exercises HBM bandwidth.
    4. The bandwidth gap disappears when high-speed InfiniBand fabrics deliver data directly into HBM from remote storage endpoints without local staging.

  2. A 175B-parameter model trains for 30 days on 256 nodes, writing resumable mixed-precision Adam checkpoints of roughly \(2.45\text{ TB}\) (\(350\text{ GB}\) FP16 weights, \(700\text{ GB}\) FP32 master weights, and \(1.4\text{ TB}\) of Adam moments) every 10 minutes. Explain why ML storage design is fundamentally a joint physics-and-economics optimization problem.

  3. True or False: A cluster benchmark demonstrating that compute hardware achieves advertised peak TFLOP/s and network fabric achieves advertised bisection bandwidth is sufficient evidence that end-to-end training throughput will scale linearly with accelerator count.

  4. An accelerator upgrade reduces compute time per batch while the input representation and storage delivery time remain fixed. What is the primary systems consequence for training cluster design?

    1. Training pipelines automatically increase data compression ratios to prevent accelerator starvation without requiring architectural adjustments.
    2. Storage hierarchies can eliminate intermediate DRAM and NVMe caching tiers because modern network fabrics directly absorb the difference.
    3. Checkpoint intervals must be decreased proportionally to the compute growth to keep local NVMe buffers from overflowing.
    4. The compute window available to deliver each batch shrinks, so input I/O can become the bottleneck unless the measured delivery path keeps pace.

  5. In large-scale distributed training, when hundreds of nodes simultaneously pause computation to write gigabytes of model weights and optimizer states to persistent storage, the resulting synchronized burst of write traffic is called a ____.

See Answers →

4.2 How ML Workloads Invert Storage Assumptions

A database administrator moving to an ML infrastructure team would find that the 175B running example’s multi-terabyte sequential scans and multi-terabyte checkpoint bursts invert many familiar storage design principles. Transactional databases emphasize small random accesses and strong consistency, issuing thousands of 4 KB reads per second to serve user queries. Storage designs for that regime combine caching algorithms that assume recently accessed records will soon be requested again, write-ahead logs that support point-in-time recovery, and redundant array of independent disks (RAID),1 which trades capacity and performance for fault tolerance. Full-corpus pretraining breaks the locality and access-size assumptions behind that stack while retaining the need to protect durable state; fine-tuning, curriculum learning, hard-example mining, recommendation features, and retrieval introduce their own skewed working sets, so each inversion below is a property of the workload rather than of ML as a whole.

1 RAID (redundant array of independent disks): A Berkeley taxonomy of drive-combining strategies that trade redundancy, capacity, and I/O performance (Patterson et al. 1988). RAID 0 stripes data without redundancy and can suit a disposable cache backed by a durable source. A drive failure then requires restaging; redundancy may still be worthwhile when that recovery cost or the required availability justifies it. Unreplicated checkpoints and sole dataset copies require a different durability contract.

Patterson, David A., Garth Gibson, and Randy H. Katz. 1988. “A Case for Redundant Arrays of Inexpensive Disks (RAID).” Proceedings of the 1988 ACM SIGMOD International Conference on Management of Data, 109–16. https://doi.org/10.1145/50202.50214.

Full-corpus pretraining inverts these assumptions when samples are packaged into sequential shards, allowing the pipeline to stream through datasets that span hundreds of terabytes. Individual accesses are large (megabytes rather than kilobytes) because models consume batches of images or text sequences, while immutable, versioned training shards avoid read-write conflicts during a run even though checkpoint publication and live feature updates still require explicit consistency guarantees. Image and video workloads can demand hundreds of gigabytes per second for days or weeks, whereas compact tokenized text can require much less input bandwidth even when checkpoint writes dominate total traffic. The mismatch between what storage was optimized for and what ML actually needs creates the I/O wall (principle 7): when storage throughput cannot deliver training data as fast as accelerators consume it, GPUs idle regardless of their computational power. For bandwidth-heavy image and video inputs, that wall arrives when accelerator count grows faster than provisioned storage throughput, making the data pipeline, not the model, the limiting factor whenever a fixed input service time cannot keep pace with faster compute.

↪ How storage stalls surface as communication or coordination bottlenecks in the C\(^3\) taxonomy is developed in Storage as the persistent substrate.

Systems Perspective 4.1: The moving I/O bottleneck
Hold the batch representation and storage path fixed while improving compute throughput. The batch now finishes sooner, leaving less time to deliver the next one. Prefetching can overlap delivery with execution, but it cannot overcome a sustained service-rate deficit. This compute-to-storage imbalance does not imply that every larger model is input bound: a model that performs more computation on the same batch can instead give storage more time. The design must follow workload service times, not advertised peak-throughput trends.

A normalized example isolates the effect. Suppose input delivery takes one quarter of the original compute time and overlaps perfectly with computation, so each batch takes the longer of the two service times. Doubling compute speed halves batch time, and quadrupling it halves batch time again, at which point compute and delivery take equal time. Beyond that point the pipeline is delivery bound. A sixteenfold faster accelerator still raises batch throughput only fourfold, and the remaining compute sits idle behind the input path. That plateau is an I/O wall for this workload and allocation. Faster storage, a more compact representation, or additional independent input paths can move it; deeper prefetching alone cannot.

The first inversion is access pattern. Where database workloads issue random access patterns that benefit from seek-time optimization, full-corpus pretraining performs large sequential scans. An epoch in the running example reads each selected sample once, in the order produced by shard and buffer shuffling, so the pattern resembles video streaming more than database queries. Storage systems optimized for random IOPS2 waste capabilities on this workload, whereas systems optimized for sequential throughput excel. The penalty for the wrong pattern is large. A Gen4/Gen5 NVMe drive delivers roughly 7–14 GB/s for sequential reads, while small 4 KB random reads can fall near 0.5 GB/s, an order-of-magnitude to 30\(\times\) drop. Hard drives pay an even steeper 100\(\times\) throughput reduction because mechanical seek times dominate random access.

2 IOPS (input/output operations per second): The metric that dominated storage procurement for decades because database workloads issue millions of small random reads. ML training inverts this priority: a pipeline streaming 256 MB shards sequentially needs sustained GB/s throughput, not per-operation speed. Provisioning storage by IOPS rating for an ML workload over-spends on random-access capability the pipeline never exercises.

The shuffling that ML training requires adds a complication rooted in the requirements of stochastic gradient descent. Stochastic gradient descent requires that each mini-batch be drawn approximately uniformly from the training distribution; presenting samples in a correlated order (all samples from the same document, all images from the same class) biases the gradient estimates and slows or destabilizes convergence. True global shuffling would satisfy this requirement perfectly, but it requires random access across the entire dataset, destroying the sequential access pattern that storage hardware demands. Even for a multi-terabyte corpus, random per-sample seeks are I/O-prohibitive. A dataset of 1 trillion 4-byte tokens (about 4 TB) stored as individual elements on NVMe and accessed at random would take hundreds to thousands of hours to read at the drive’s random-read IOPS rate, rather than about ten minutes of sequential streaming. The practical solution is therefore a compromise that shuffles the order of large data shards and then shuffles samples within each shard’s local buffer. This preserves sequential I/O, but adequate mixing depends on shard composition and buffer size, a dependence section 4.4.2 turns into a validation step for the loader.

The second inversion is working set size. Traditional applications rely on temporal locality: a web server repeatedly accesses popular pages, and a cache holding the top 10 percent of content serves 90 percent of requests. In the chapter’s full-corpus pretraining scenario, the 6 TB serialized corpus has no “popular” token range; each selected token is consumed once per epoch. A cache of any practical size holds only a small fraction of the corpus, and most samples are effectively “cold” when accessed. This access pattern makes traditional recency caching much less effective. A least recently used (LRU)3 cache can approach a 0 percent hit rate for a cyclic scan larger than the cache, because each sample is evicted before its next access. With independently reshuffled epochs, cross-epoch hits are low but nonzero and depend on the cache fraction and sample order.

3 LRU (least recently used): An eviction policy that discards the entry unused for the longest time, effective when recently accessed data is likely to be accessed again soon. Under independent reshuffling, some samples retained from the end of one epoch may be reused early in the next, so teams should measure that reuse before allocating DRAM that could otherwise serve as prefetch buffers.

Other training policies create different locality. Multi-task or curriculum learning, hard-example mining, and filtered sampling access some subsets more frequently during particular phases. This temporary working set lets the NVMe tier exploit locality. Large-scale pretraining jobs that repeatedly scan a fixed corpus remain the scenario for the hierarchy derived here; systems serving another sampling policy should measure its reuse distribution before adopting the same cache design.

The third inversion is write pattern. Transactional systems generate continuous streams of small writes, each immediately durable. ML systems generate occasional large writes when saving checkpoints. A 175B parameter model checkpoint, including optimizer state, occupies roughly 2,450 GB. Saving it every 10 minutes generates concentrated bursts that saturate bandwidth for seconds, followed by long idle periods. These synchronized bursts, called checkpoint storms and defined formally in definition 4.5, must be absorbed by the shared file system without disrupting ongoing training reads. The bursty write pattern is particularly challenging because all nodes in the cluster write their checkpoint shards simultaneously. If 256 nodes each write 10 GB, the parallel file system receives 2.4 TB of writes in a single burst, which blocks training if the job writes synchronously to that tier. Local staging and asynchronous replication can shorten this pause, as section 4.6 develops. When checkpoint and gradient traffic share one fabric, traffic isolation also keeps the burst from delaying the job’s collectives.

↩︎ Traffic isolation and quality-of-service mechanisms are established in Traffic isolation and quality of service.

The fourth inversion is subtler. The read/write ratio shifts by lifecycle phase. During the intervals between checkpoints, the input pipeline reads continuously. Across a complete run, however, the read/write ratio depends on input size, epoch count, checkpoint size, and save frequency. The running language-model example produces far more checkpoint bytes than training-input bytes despite spending most of its time between saves. During data preprocessing, both reads and writes are heavy, and the access pattern more closely resembles a MapReduce job than a training loop: tokenization, deduplication, and shuffling scan the raw corpus once (large sequential reads) and write intermediate artifacts (large sequential writes). The next stage then scans those artifacts again. A 3 TB compressed text corpus typically expands to 6 TB of tokenized shards before the first training epoch begins, loading each storage tier differently from the read-only training phase that follows. No single storage configuration optimizes for all phases, which is why ML systems require a multi-tier hierarchy rather than a single storage technology.

A fifth inversion emerges when comparing training and inference workloads. Training reads datasets sequentially and writes checkpoints in bursts. Weight-resident inference, by contrast, performs one large model read at startup and then serves requests from HBM. Its storage challenge is cold-start latency: the time required to load a model when scaling up or recovering from failure. The binding constraint flips from sustained dataset throughput to a bulk read whose duration depends on which tier supplies the weights. Retrieval, state offload, and models larger than aggregate HBM create additional serving-time I/O, so this weight-resident case is a baseline rather than a universal inference pattern. Section 4.9 derives the load times and connects that baseline to the retrieval workload that follows.

Serving at fleet scale also creates a fan-out read burst. A serving cluster with 100 replicas of the 175B running model holds 35 TB of FP16 weights, and a model rollout must deliver a new version to every replica within minutes while the old version keeps serving. The burst is the checkpoint storm in reverse. Instead of many nodes writing to shared storage at once, many nodes read the same data at once.

Together, the five inversions collected in table 1 reduce to one procurement rule, which is to optimize for streaming reads and bursty writes rather than database-style random IOPS. An organization provisioning storage for ML based on database-era heuristics will over-invest in random IOPS for sequential training shards, under-invest in the sustained bandwidth those shards require, and fail to account for the bursty write patterns that checkpointing creates.

Checkpoint 4.1: Storage workload analysis

Consider the storage subsystem for a new ML training cluster with 512 GPUs. The primary workload will train large language models on a 10 TB text dataset.

Translating these five inversions into a concrete architecture requires separating how a dataset is packaged from how fast each storage tier must serve it. The required delivery rate depends on workload demand rather than parameter count alone: it equals the aggregate input bytes consumed during a delivery window divided by that window, a rate that representation, batch size, parallelism, and compute time jointly set (section 4.4) and that prefetching alone cannot sustain when demand exceeds a tier’s allocated share. In the running example, the 1.5 trillion tokens of training data produce roughly 6 TB of sequential token-ID reads per epoch from a 3 TB compressed source corpus. Stored as one file per sample, that corpus would spend its storage budget on file opens rather than payload bytes, the metadata bottleneck that section 4.3.6 develops as the small file problem. Preprocessing the corpus into large sequential shards (Aizman et al. 2019) amortizes open, seek, and close costs across many tokens without changing the payload byte rate (section 4.3.5). Where those shards should live, and how close to the accelerator, is the question the storage hierarchy answers.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A storage procurement team is sizing storage hardware for a cluster dedicated to pretraining foundation models on a \(200\text{ TB}\) immutable text corpus. Which purchasing decision represents the most severe misallocation of budget based on ML workload characteristics?

    1. Paying a premium for enterprise SSDs optimized for high \(4\text{ KB}\) random-read IOPS while under-provisioning sustained sequential read bandwidth (GB/s).
    2. Aggregating raw text samples into \(256\text{ MB}\) to \(4\text{ GB}\) contiguous binary shards prior to training to amortize file-system metadata operations.
    3. Provisioning local NVMe drives on compute nodes to serve as a warm read cache for multi-epoch training passes.
    4. Sizing the parallel file system write tier to absorb synchronized multi-terabyte checkpoint write bursts from hundreds of nodes.

  2. True or False: An in-memory LRU cache sized to \(300\text{ GB}\) placed in front of a \(3\text{ TB}\) uniformly shuffled pretraining dataset will achieve roughly a \(90\%\) hit rate after the first epoch warms the cache because all samples are reused across epochs.

  3. Explain why ML data loaders implement shard-level shuffling combined with within-shard buffer mixing rather than true global per-sample random shuffling across petabyte-scale training corpora.

  4. When an inference platform deploys a new version of a 175B-parameter model (\(350\text{ GB}\) FP16 weights) across 100 serving replicas, how does the storage access profile contrast with a training checkpoint save?

    1. It generates continuous small random writes to update parameter journals on each replica node.
    2. It streams an incremental delta of only modified parameters over hours to minimize bandwidth utilization.
    3. It creates a synchronized fan-out read burst of roughly \(35\text{ TB}\) (\(100 \times 350\text{ GB}\)) that must complete within the deployment latency budget while serving traffic continues.
    4. It requires sustained multi-gigabyte-per-second sequential write bandwidth to central durable object storage.

  5. A team plans one storage configuration for the whole life of a language-model project: tokenizing and deduplicating a \(3\text{ TB}\) compressed corpus into \(6\text{ TB}\) of shards, then training on those shards with periodic checkpoints. Why does the chapter conclude that no single configuration optimizes every phase?

    1. Preprocessing reads and writes large sequential artifacts in MapReduce-like passes, while training reads immutable shards between bursty checkpoint writes, so the read/write balance and access pattern change by phase.
    2. Training reads are dominated by small random lookups that only a database-style random-IOPS tier can serve, while preprocessing is purely sequential.
    3. The tokenized corpus is smaller than the compressed source, so preprocessing frees capacity that the training phase cannot use.
    4. Checkpoint writes are continuous and small, so they require a different write-ahead log configuration than preprocessing.

See Answers →

4.3 The ML Storage Hierarchy

No single storage device can hold a petabyte-scale training corpus, absorb multi-terabyte checkpoints, and still feed an accelerator at terabytes per second. HBM supplies high local bandwidth at limited capacity, whereas object storage pools durable capacity behind a much slower service and client path. A multi-tier hierarchy combines these resources rather than requiring one technology to meet every demand. Each tier offers a different balance of bandwidth, latency, capacity, and cost. The hierarchy extends the familiar processor memory hierarchy across nodes. The new fleet-level decision is how local staging reduces demand on shared storage while preserving a durable, consistently versioned source. Comparing the tiers fairly requires stating each resource at its own scope, per accelerator, per node, or as an aggregate shared service (table 2).

HBM’s advantage over host DRAM in table 2 is bandwidth, not latency, because its lead comes from wide stacked interfaces rather than faster cells (HBM and the memory wall). The latency cliff between adjacent tiers opens only at NVMe and below, where the physical distance from the accelerator grows from millimeters to meters. The storage hierarchy model (principle 8) therefore governs storage tiering and pipeline design: the local tiers exchange bandwidth for capacity, while shared tiers aggregate many devices and expose a different client path. Because per-client bandwidth depends on allocation and contention rather than a fixed factor between tiers, data format choices, caching strategies, prefetch buffer sizing, and tiering policies all exist to manage the movement of data upward through the hierarchy so that the accelerator never starves.

The rest of the chapter numbers the six tiers by distance from the accelerator, from Tier 0 (HBM) through Tier 1 (host DRAM), Tier 2 (local NVMe), Tier 3 (parallel file system), and Tier 4 (object storage) to Tier 5 (archive). Distance alone hides an important distinction, because each tier’s bandwidth figure describes a different scope: per accelerator, per node, or a shared service divided among its clients (figure 1).

The physical tiers of figure 1 govern the trade-off among access distance, capacity, and cost. Shared services can aggregate more bandwidth than an individual local device, so the ordering is not a monotonic ranking of aggregate throughput. This trade-off is not arbitrary; it reflects the physics of data proximity. HBM sits on the same silicon interposer as the accelerator, connected by thousands of parallel traces measured in millimeters, whereas host DRAM connects to the CPU memory controller and reaches the accelerator across the host-device interconnect, and local NVMe reaches across the circuit board over PCIe lanes. Beyond the node, the parallel file system traverses meters of cable and network switches, while object storage may span kilometers of fiber between data centers. Distance, protocol overhead, and contention can increase access latency, while media technology and provisioning determine capacity cost. Distance is therefore a guide rather than a measurement; only the complete client path establishes all three properties for a given consumer.

The engineering challenge is to deliver each batch to HBM before the accelerator needs it. Return to the running example: the durable corpus lives in object storage (Tier 4) as a 3 TB compressed source copy plus 6 TB of tokenized training shards, but the accelerator needs each batch in HBM (Tier 0) within 200 ms. The chapter’s reference design fixes one path between them. At job start, each node copies its share of the tokenized shards from object storage to its local NVMe (Tier 2). The single training epoch then reads from that local copy, prepares batches in host DRAM, and transfers them to HBM. The parallel file system (Tier 3) holds checkpoints rather than training inputs. Streaming the shards directly from object storage, or serving them from a parallel file system, are the alternatives the chapter weighs against this design, and the hierarchy describes placement choices, not a requirement that every byte visit every level.

4.3.1 Bandwidth cliffs between tiers

Stepping down one level from a single accelerator’s HBM (3.35 TB/s) to the whole host’s DRAM (~200 GB/s) drops bandwidth by roughly 16.8×, and that comparison still understates the cliff on an eight-GPU node because all eight accelerators share the same host-memory bandwidth. Comparing adjacent tiers requires making the scope of each rate explicit—per accelerator, per node, or across a shared service—as bracketed in figure 1. Between host DRAM and NVMe, the ratio is roughly 7.1× (200 GB/s vs. ~28 GB/s from a 4-drive RAID-0). Between NVMe and a parallel file system, the ratio depends on the per-node allocation: if a 1 TB/s aggregate parallel file system (PFS) serves 256 nodes, each node receives roughly 4 GB/s, a 7× reduction from the local RAID-0 stripe. Object-storage throughput likewise depends on the client path and allocation; there is no fixed ratio to a parallel file system.

Per-client bandwidth falls roughly 7 to 17\(\times\) at each step from HBM to a node’s share of the parallel file system.

As section 4.1 established, a cliff is not a feed requirement; the feed rate remains input-batch bytes divided by iteration time, summed over distinct consumers. What the cliffs decide is where buffering must sit. Along the chosen path, host DRAM can buffer prepared batches and NVMe can cache shards from shared storage. Each buffer must cover the delay of the tier below it while that tier sustains the consumption rate; durable repositories need not act as intermediate buffers.

The same hierarchy can be analyzed as a cache rather than as six isolated devices. If a request hits the hot tier with probability \(\HotTierHitProbability\), taking \(\HotTierServiceTime\), and otherwise falls through to a colder tier, taking total end-to-end miss-path time \(\MissPathServiceTime\) (including the failed hot-tier lookup), its mean service time is

\[ \mathbb{E}[\TieredRequestServiceTime] = \HotTierHitProbability \HotTierServiceTime+ (1-\HotTierHitProbability)\MissPathServiceTime. \tag{1}\]

For fixed-size records served serially, the corresponding effective bandwidth is the record size divided by \(\mathbb{E}[\TieredRequestServiceTime]\). The arithmetic makes small miss rates consequential: with a 95 percent hit rate and a cold-tier access 100× slower than a hot-tier access, \(\mathbb{E}[\TieredRequestServiceTime]=5.95\HotTierServiceTime\), so the effective request rate is only about one-sixth of the all-hot rate. Concurrent requests and prefetching can overlap service, so this mean is a sizing baseline rather than a fleet-throughput or latency guarantee. Correlated misses or a heavy-tailed cold tier can drain the prefetch buffer even when the average satisfies the pipeline budget, so production designs must check a tail percentile and miss bursts as well as \(\mathbb{E}[\TieredRequestServiceTime]\).

The batch-volume arithmetic also explains why increasing cluster size creates storage pressure. Aggregate demand is each consumer’s batch bytes divided by its compute window, summed over consumers, so at a fixed per-node rate it grows linearly with node count. The per-node rate itself spans orders of magnitude across data modalities, as text and image training on the running cluster show.

Image training demands 1,800× the storage bandwidth of text.

Napkin Math 4.1: Text vs. image bandwidth
Problem: How much aggregate storage bandwidth does a 2,048-GPU cluster require for text training vs. image training?

Setup: Compare two declared batch representations at the same compute window and number of independent input consumers. The result depends on batch volume and rate, not modality alone. Both jobs are generic ones sized to the running cluster, not the 175B running job. Each GPU consumes one per-GPU batch every step, a pace that fits a model far smaller than 175B parameters.

For text training, the demand is low. With a per-GPU batch of one packed sequence of 4,096 tokens and a 200 ms step time, the aggregate bandwidth is:

\[2{,}048 \text{ GPUs} \times 4{,}096 \text{ tokens/GPU} \times 4 \text{ bytes/token} \div 0.2\text{s} \approx {167.8 MB/s}\]

This is easily served by a single network-attached storage node.

Image training demands far more. Using a common batch size of 256 per GPU (ImageNet at \(224{\times}224\), roughly 116 KB/image), the aggregate bandwidth is:

\[2{,}048 \text{ GPUs} \times 256 \text{ images/GPU} \times 115.7 \text{ KB/image} \div 0.2\text{s} \approx {303.2 GB/s}\]

Systems insight: Under the stated batch representations and compute times, image training requires 1,800× higher input bandwidth than text training. The text example is volume-heavy but bandwidth-light. One network-attached storage server can carry its input feed, so its storage design is shaped by total corpus volume and, as later sections show, by checkpoint traffic. The image example is bandwidth-heavy, with a feed that exceeds any single storage server and calls for the aggregate bandwidth of many servers or of local caches. Whether either demand becomes an actual bottleneck still depends on the measured delivery rate of the path that serves it.

The 175B running job sits lower still. Its 6 TB epoch spread over the 30-day window averages about 2.3 MB/s for the whole cluster, corpus bytes divided by run length, roughly 70× below the notebook’s generic text job. Each token of a 175B model carries so much computation that the corpus trickles in, which is why the running example’s storage design is shaped by checkpoint traffic rather than by its input feed.

The image result is a floor for visual data rather than a ceiling. At about 303.2 GB/s across 256 nodes, the image job already consumes nearly a third of a 1 TB/s parallel file system built from 100 data-serving nodes (the architecture section 4.3.6 develops). Higher-resolution images and video clips, at megabytes per sample rather than the roughly 116 KB of a compressed ImageNet image, raise the per-node demand by an order of magnitude or more, and a fleet several times larger multiplies it again. Demand at that scale exceeds what a shared file system of this size can serve, so the architecture must rely on local NVMe caching to take repeated reads off shared storage.

The bandwidth cliff between tiers also has implications for the data format at each level. Computation consumes float16 tensors, packed token IDs, or other supported representations, while NVMe and object storage can retain compressed records. Compression reduces bytes stored and transferred but adds decoding work. The chosen format must let CPU or GPU decoding keep pace with consumption; maximum compression can reduce end-to-end throughput when decoding becomes the limiting stage. A host-decoded path stages the resulting tensors in DRAM, while a GPU-decoded path moves the transformation and its resource demand to the accelerator.

The format challenge intensifies for multi-modal training, which combines text, images, audio, and video in a single model. Serialized sizes span orders of magnitude, from 4 bytes per text token to roughly 116 KB per compressed image to megabytes per short video clip, depending on representation, compression, resolution, and duration, and each modality requires its own decoding and augmentation work. A multi-modal training job must manage these parallel streams and provision for their combined service demand. For a corpus containing 3 TB of text, 50 TB of images, and 200 TB of video, video dominates retained capacity. Its bandwidth contribution also depends on how frequently training samples it and how many bytes each sampled batch reads. Corpus size alone determines neither the delivery rate nor the modality’s contribution to model quality.

Tracing how each tier meets these bandwidth and format constraints begins at the top of the pyramid and steps outward from the accelerator to the archive. That descent starts where computation actually consumes bytes: HBM, the tier fast enough to keep accelerator arithmetic fed but far too scarce to hold the full training corpus.

4.3.2 Tier 0 GPU HBM

The scarcest resource in the system sits closest to the arithmetic. On the H100 used here, HBM holds the active weights and activations served to the accelerator, while caches and on-chip buffers reuse portions of that state. Its stacked packaging supplies 3.35 TB/s of aggregate bandwidth, the top of the cliffs just measured. That bandwidth is what makes large-scale deep learning feasible, because a matrix multiplication involving billions of parameters reads those parameters from memory on every forward and backward pass. What HBM lacks is room.

↩︎ High-bandwidth memory packaging, the 3D stacking and through-silicon vias behind this bandwidth, is established in HBM and the memory wall.

For placing the unpartitioned running model, the immediate constraint is capacity. Once state fits, HBM bandwidth can still limit execution. The node-memory budget of Node memory partitioning already shows how far one 80 GB device falls short of the 175B running example: the FP16 weights alone, 350 GB, need at least five H100 accelerators. The mixed-precision contract this chapter checkpoints also retains 700 GB of FP32 master weights, so weights, gradients, master weights, and Adam moments occupy 2.80 TB before activations and communication buffers. State that does not fit in one HBM pool must be split across accelerators or kept outside HBM and fetched when needed. Splitting it across the cluster makes the run possible, but every shard becomes a separate piece of state that lower tiers must stage, save, and restore in step with the others. HBM scarcity is what forces the rest of the hierarchy to exist, and HBM is the destination that every lower tier exists to serve.

Because HBM capacity is so limited relative to both model size and dataset size, the accelerator processes data in batches. Each batch occupies a fraction of HBM for the duration of one forward-backward pass, then is discarded to make room for the next. The rate at which batches must be supplied sets the bandwidth requirement for all lower tiers.

The razor-thin margin is a defining feature of large model training, visible in the HBM budget that each 80 GB H100 carries while the fleet trains the 175B parameter model (table 3). The table treats partitioning as a storage layout, and the chapter keeps this one layout throughout. Tensor parallelism splits the model weights eight ways inside each node, so every node holds one complete copy of the weights spread across its GPUs. Optimizer, master-weight, and gradient state are additionally sharded across the 256 nodes, so each GPU holds only its slice of that state rather than a full replica. Sharding optimizer and gradient state across data-parallel replicas while each replica keeps its weights is the second stage of the Zero Redundancy Optimizer (ZeRO), a training technique in its own right; its third stage also partitions the weights. Here only the resulting per-GPU slices matter, because they set the storage pressure HBM sees.

↪ Sharded data parallelism (ZeRO and FSDP), which partitions optimizer, gradient, and weight state across workers, is developed in Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP.

Even with aggressive partitioning, the total memory footprint reaches 56.9–68.9 GB in decimal gigabytes, about 66.3–80.3 percent of the HBM pool (the device’s nominal 80 GB is a binary capacity, slightly larger than 80 decimal gigabytes). The remaining headroom shrinks further once fragmentation and framework workspaces are included, and that headroom is all the incoming data pipeline has to work with. An input batch and its activations occupy HBM for one training step, while model weights and optimizer state persist across steps. Prefetching the next batch therefore competes with the live training footprint for scarce capacity; a deeper input queue is useful only if the memory budget can accommodate it.

From the data pipeline’s perspective, Tier 0 is not a storage tier to be managed but a constraint to be satisfied. The pipeline’s purpose is to ensure that the batch the accelerator needs next is already resident in HBM before the current batch’s computation completes. If it arrives late, the accelerator stalls. If it arrives early, it consumes HBM that could hold activations. The tension between “just in time” and “just too late” defines the pipeline’s buffer management strategy, quantified in section 4.4.

4.3.3 Tier 1 host DRAM

One level below HBM, host DRAM serves as the staging area for the data pipeline. Every byte of training data that reaches the accelerator passes through host DRAM first (unless GPUDirect Storage bypasses it, as described in section 4.5). A typical training node contains 512 GB to 2 TB of system memory shared across the host CPU and its peripherals. While the bandwidth between host DRAM and the accelerator is limited to what PCIe Gen 5 (about 64 GB/s per direction, 128 GB/s bidirectional) can provide, host DRAM plays three roles in the ML storage hierarchy. It is the prefetch buffer, a home for embedding tables too large for HBM, and the augmentation workspace. Only CPU-accelerator superchips with a coherent NVLink-class host link raise that path toward the 900 GB/s that NVLink provides between accelerators. The data loader occupies two of these roles at once, because its augmentation stage and the prefetch buffer it fills both live in host DRAM.

The data loader pipeline that runs in host DRAM works as it does on a single node: reader threads fetch compressed samples, workers decode and augment them, and a collation stage assembles batches in pinned, or page-locked, memory for the host-to-accelerator direct memory access (DMA) transfer. At fleet scale, these stages compete with the node’s other demands for the same host DRAM.

↪ The single-node overlap techniques behind this loader (prefetching, pinned host buffers, and double buffering) are collected for reference in D·A·M coordination from sum to max.

Host DRAM’s most critical function is serving as a prefetch buffer. When the accelerator finishes processing batch \(\BatchIndex\), batch \(\BatchIndex+1\) should already be assembled in host memory, ready for transfer to HBM. The depth of this prefetch buffer determines how much I/O variance the pipeline can absorb without stalling.

Recommendation workloads, the regime of Archetype B (DLRM at Scale), place a different demand on host DRAM: hosting embedding tables that can exceed 100 GB, far too large for HBM. These tables reside in host DRAM and are accessed through lookups that fetch only the rows needed for the current batch. The bandwidth between host DRAM and HBM becomes the critical bottleneck for these workloads, and some systems go further, pooling embedding tables in the DRAM of dedicated CPU servers and returning only the requested rows over remote direct memory access (RDMA).

Host DRAM also provides the augmentation workspace that the CPU pipeline requires. Data augmentation operations (resizing images, tokenizing text, applying noise) execute on the CPU and require temporary memory for intermediate results. A training pipeline that applies five augmentations to a 256-image batch at 116 KB per image needs tens of megabytes of working space for each augmentation stage. Although modest per-batch, this memory accumulates when multiple data loader workers run in parallel. GPU-side decoding with libraries such as the NVIDIA Data Loading Library (DALI) moves decode and augmentation onto the accelerator, so smaller compressed bytes cross the host link and host DRAM bandwidth demand falls. The work then consumes HBM capacity and compute cycles instead, which helps an input-bound job and hurts a compute-bound one.

The three roles interact in subtle ways. The prefetch buffer and the augmentation workspace compete for the same physical DRAM, and embedding tables consume capacity that could otherwise serve as deeper prefetch queues. A node with 512 GB of DRAM hosting a 200 GB embedding table has only 312 GB remaining for prefetching and augmentation. If the data loader uses 8 workers, each maintaining a decode buffer of 1 GB, the effective prefetch capacity drops further. System architects must balance these competing demands by profiling the actual memory consumption of each pipeline stage and provisioning DRAM accordingly.

The physical layout of DRAM also matters. On a dual-socket node, each socket’s DRAM forms a non-uniform memory access (NUMA) domain local to the GPUs on that socket’s PCIe root complex, and reaching the other socket’s DRAM crosses the inter-socket link, with a penalty that depends on the processor, topology, and concurrent traffic (The PCIe hierarchy within a node). NUMA-aware allocation keeps each data loader worker and its prefetch buffer in the same NUMA domain as the GPUs it serves; the gain should be measured on the target node rather than assumed.

Host DRAM sits below the first cliff of section 4.3.1, and the host-to-GPU interconnect adds a separate cliff, with effective transfer bandwidth depending on whether the node uses PCIe or NVLink. In the conventional path that stages payloads through host memory, a depleted DRAM prefetch buffer leaves the accelerator waiting for lower tiers. The direct path of section 4.5 removes that staging copy when the data layout and devices support it. In the running example, the 256-node cluster requires each node’s host DRAM to sustain a continuous flow of decoded, augmented batches ready for PCIe transfer. If the NVMe-to-DRAM read pipeline falls behind by even a few hundred milliseconds, the prefetch buffer drains and the accelerator idles until the next batch arrives.

4.3.4 Tier 2 local NVMe

When the working set exceeds host DRAM, the system falls to Tier 2: the local NVMe drives attached directly to the compute node. NVMe4 provides a high-performance protocol designed specifically for solid-state drives, achieving 7 GB/s of sequential throughput per drive. NVMe’s deep queues and PCIe lanes, rather than faster flash, explain why it delivers 10\(\times\) to 50\(\times\) the throughput of SATA SSDs (capped near 550 MB/s by the link) with identical NAND flash, provided the loader keeps many asynchronous reads outstanding. With four drives in a RAID-0 configuration, a single node can sustain roughly 28 GB/s of sequential reads before overhead, sufficient to stream the 6 TB serialized dataset from local disk in about 3.6 minutes under ideal sequential conditions.

4 NVMe (Non-Volatile Memory Express): A storage protocol designed for SSDs, connecting directly to the CPU via PCIe lanes. Its many deep command queues, replacing the single 32-command queue of the older SATA interface, are what allow a multi-worker ML data loader to keep enough requests outstanding to saturate the drive’s bandwidth.

In ML training, local NVMe acts as a warm cache, storing data shards fetched from distributed storage. Workers can reread resident shards across epochs without fetching them over the network again. The savings depend on reuse, cache capacity, and whether remote reads cross a charged transfer boundary (section 4.8). Staging pays an initial transfer cost and provisions local capacity in exchange for avoiding later remote reads; it does not eliminate storage cost or read latency.

The warm cache pattern requires careful capacity planning. A training node with four 7.68 TB NVMe drives provides approximately 30.7 TB of local storage. For the running example, the 6 TB serialized dataset fits comfortably on a single node’s local storage, with room for checkpoint staging and temporary augmentation buffers. A multi-modal training job combining 20 TB of images, 10 TB of text, and 5 TB of audio totals 35 TB, exceeding local capacity by about 4.3 TB and forcing the pipeline to stream from the parallel file system for at least part of the dataset. The design trade-off is between provisioning more local NVMe (which increases node cost) and accepting network-dependent reads (which risks latency spikes).

The I/O wall takes a deceptive form at this tier. The HBM-to-NVMe ratio of section 4.3.1 does not set demand, so the question here is whether the stripe meets the input-batch rate, not whether it approaches HBM. Pipelining overlaps that delivery with compute (section 4.4), while GPUDirect Storage (section 4.5) can remove host payload copies when those copies limit throughput. The wall is easy to miss because NVMe performance is excellent by historical standards. A storage engineer accustomed to HDD-era throughput of 150 MB/s might view 28 GB/s from a local RAID-0 stripe as more than enough. Whether 28 GB/s suffices depends on the batch representation and consumption rate. Compact text may fit within this budget, while image or video inputs may require more bandwidth; overlap hides transfer time only when sustained supply keeps pace with demand.

Local NVMe also supports local checkpoint staging. Writing a consistent snapshot locally and replicating it to shared storage in the background can shorten \(\CheckpointWriteTime\), the time training pauses to save a checkpoint, when the shared write would otherwise be slower. Resuming after a local save accepts a period during which a node failure can destroy the new shard. The storage design must therefore specify both the exposed pause and the later durability boundary, while retaining a previous recoverable generation. Section 4.6 develops this trade-off and its timing model.

The same local NVMe tier also pays for itself by recovering utilization lost to remote-read latency. When congestion on a shared file system drains the host prefetch buffer and exposes I/O wait on every step, staging shards onto node-local flash converts that exposed remote wait into reclaimed accelerator compute time.

Napkin Math 4.2: Return on investment of local NVMe caching
Problem: A vision-model training pipeline, a separate workload from the 175B language model, runs each step in 800 ms on the 1,024-GPU reference cluster. It reads from a shared parallel file system, and even with prefetching, congestion bursts on that shared service drain the loader’s buffer and leave an average of 150 ms of exposed I/O wait per step. Assume that serving the same shards from local NVMe leaves only 10 ms of exposed wait per step, the residue of local tail latency that the prefetch queue does not cover. How much does adding local NVMe SSDs to each node improve GPU utilization?

Math: GPU utilization here means the fraction of step time spent in computation, an active-time fraction rather than model FLOPs utilization.

  1. Remote Only: 800 ms / (800 ms + 150 ms) \(\approx\) 84.2 percent.
  2. Local Cache: Prefetching from local NVMe removes the congestion bursts of the shared service, leaving the assumed local residue.
    • New Util: 800 ms / (800 ms + 10 ms) \(\approx\) 98.8 percent.

Systems insight: Local storage can act as a GPU utilization multiplier. In this scenario, adding a $500 NVMe drive to a node whose accelerators each cost $30,000 recovers 14.6 percent of the GPU’s capacity that was previously wasted on I/O wait. Across the 1,024-GPU cluster, that utilization gain is equivalent to adding 149 GPUs worth of useful work without buying that many more accelerators. The gain is exactly the exposed remote wait that local reads remove, so it is large only when shared-storage congestion leaves a wait of this size; where it does, local NVMe is not auxiliary but the physical buffer that decouples expensive compute from unpredictable shared storage.

A practical concern at this tier is SSD endurance. NAND flash memory can sustain a limited number of write-erase cycles before the cells degrade. Enterprise NVMe drives are rated for 1 to 3 drive writes per day (DWPD) over a 5-year lifespan. For a 7.68 TB drive at 1 DWPD, this means the drive can absorb 7.68 TB of writes per day, or roughly 14 PB total over its lifetime. ML training workloads are predominantly read-heavy (the dataset is written once and read many times), which is favorable for SSD endurance. However, checkpoint writes can be intensive: if each node saves its roughly 10 GB share of the running example’s checkpoint every 10 minutes, that is about 1.4 TB per day of checkpoint writes spread across the node’s drives, well within a four-drive node’s 1 DWPD budget. The risk emerges when local NVMe is used as a staging buffer for both checkpoint writes and dataset caching: the combined write volume from initial dataset staging plus repeated checkpoint saves must remain within the drive’s endurance rating.

4.3.5 Data formats for training

Local NVMe can stream 28 GB/s per node, but packaging determines how much of that bandwidth reaches the loader. The same multi-terabyte dataset stored in different formats can yield I/O throughput that differs by over 100\(\times\) on identical hardware, because each format charges a different access cost: per-file metadata, unnecessary feature reads, or sequential shard traversal. A directory containing one JPEG per sample pays a separate file open and metadata lookup for every image. At roughly 10 to 50 microseconds per open in the kernel’s file-system layer, a node reading 8,000 images per second spends 80 to 400 ms of CPU time per second on opens alone, before it decodes a single byte. On a shared file system the same operations also queue at a metadata service, the fleet-scale form of this cost that section 4.3.6 develops as the small file problem. A standard ImageNet training pipeline makes the cost of the wrong format visible.

Napkin Math 4.3: The ImageNet bottleneck analysis
Problem: A ResNet-50 training job on ImageNet (1.28M, ~116 KB average) targets 1000 images/s. Can individual JPEG files on an HDD sustain that rate, or does the job need NVMe?

Math:

  1. Raw Bandwidth: 1000 images/s \(\times\) 116 KB = 116 MB/s.
  2. HDD reality: A 7200 RPM hard disk drive (HDD) delivers 100 IOPS at random reads. Sustaining 1000 images/s requires 10× more IOPS than the disk provides.
  3. Result: When every image is a separate random read, shuffling individual files on an HDD will starve the GPU, capping utilization at about 10 percent before decode, seek, and file-system overheads.

Systems insight: Two fixes work, and they remove different limits. NVMe answers a random read in roughly 100 μs rather than the milliseconds of a mechanical seek, so even one outstanding request covers the 1000 images/s that the HDD’s 100 IOPS cannot. Alternatively, converting the dataset to a sequential format (TFRecord/WebDataset) removes the random reads altogether and turns the HDD problem into a bandwidth one, in which the job needs 116 MB/s, within the roughly 150 MB/s that one HDD streams sequentially.

The first format choice is the record layout. Row-oriented formats, such as CSV or JSON lines, store successive records together and are easy to inspect and debug. They can stream many records from one file, although parsing text and reading unused fields consume CPU cycles and bandwidth.

A second choice solves a different storage problem. Columnar formats like Apache Parquet and Apache Arrow avoid unnecessary reads when tabular features can be selected before transfer. They organize data by column rather than by row, which allows for excellent compression as data within a column is typically of the same type and has lower entropy. Column pruning lets the reader fetch only required features. Predicate pushdown applies filters at the storage reader and can skip groups of records using their metadata, further reducing unnecessary I/O. These formats are well-suited for tabular ML tasks and feature engineering but are less natural for unstructured data like images or audio, which are treated as atomic blobs.

A third choice addresses the training access pattern directly. Sequential streaming formats reduce per-sample metadata to the cost of opening a shard. TFRecord, Mosaic’s StreamingDataset format (Databricks 2026), and WebDataset5 group many samples into large, contiguous binary files called shards. WebDataset uses standard tar archives, in which each sample keeps its image, label, and metadata entries while the loader streams the archive in order. A data loader opens a single shard and streams its contents sequentially, amortizing the open over thousands of samples. The tar-based formats have the added advantage of being valid targets for HTTP byte-range requests, making them ideal for streaming data directly from object storage without downloading the entire file first.

5 WebDataset: A data format that repurposes standard POSIX tar archives for ML training. The design insight is that tar’s sequential header-then-data layout, invented in 1979 for tape backup, maps perfectly onto the streaming access pattern that ML data loaders need.

A key trade-off within streaming formats is the presence of an index. TFRecord readers often rely on a separate index file that records each sample’s byte offset within a shard, while HDF5 embeds its index in the file itself. This enables efficient random access within a shard but introduces a metadata dependency: the index must be read and held in memory. WebDataset is indexless, because each record in the tar archive contains its own header specifying its size, allowing for purely sequential streaming without any external metadata. The trade-off is that indexless formats cannot efficiently seek to an arbitrary sample within a shard, a capability that matters for curriculum learning or active learning workloads that require nonsequential data access.

Compression integration also varies across formats, and it trades storage bytes against CPU decoding work. Parquet integrates compression at the column level, exploiting type homogeneity for high ratios. WebDataset and TFRecord typically leave compression to the individual sample: a WebDataset of images stores already-compressed JPEG files, while a text dataset might apply zstd compression per shard, and the host CPU pays for that decompression. Consider two illustrative codecs on the same corpus: a lighter codec compresses 3:1 and produces 1.5 GB/s of decoded output per CPU core, while a denser codec compresses 4:1 and produces 0.5 GB/s per core. These are scenario assumptions, not fixed properties of named codec levels. At the 28 GB/s local NVMe rate, multiplying that read rate by each compression ratio yields potential decoded output rates of 84 GB/s and 112 GB/s, respectively, provided CPU decoding keeps pace. Dividing each uncompressed output rate by its per-core decoding speed requires 56 versus 224 cores, so the denser codec needs 4× as many cores because it produces one-third more uncompressed bytes from the same storage read while decoding each byte at one-third the per-core speed. Running each codec at the drives’ full output is the worst case, an upper bound on decode work; a node needs only enough cores to decode its own share of demand. The generic text job of notebook 4.1 asks the entire cluster for about 167.8 MB/s, which a single core of either codec covers, so these core counts bind only for image and video feeds whose decoded demand per node approaches the drives’ rate. With fewer cores than that demand requires, decoding limits delivery before storage saturates, so compression is chosen from the measured corpus, decoder, and available CPU budget rather than from the compression ratio alone.

For the running example, the 1.5 trillion token dataset is stored as roughly 2,000 shards of 3 GB each in a custom binary format. Each shard contains a small header (token count, vocabulary size, byte order) followed by a contiguous array of 4-byte token IDs. The number of open() calls per epoch is therefore the number of shards, not the number of tokens (trillions), an approximately 750,000,000× reduction in metadata operations that makes streaming from both local NVMe and remote storage feasible at training scale. When staged on POSIX storage such as local NVMe or a parallel file system, the data loader memory-maps the shard and indexes directly into the token array by position, achieving near-peak read bandwidth with zero per-sample metadata overhead. When the same shards are read directly from object storage, the loader uses HTTP ranged GET streaming and an explicit local buffer rather than memory mapping. The formats differ chiefly in the access cost each one imposes per sample on the storage path (table 4).

Packaging removes the per-file cost within one node, but no node-local format gives every node the same view of the data. That requirement belongs to the next tier.

4.3.6 Tier 3 parallel file systems

Local NVMe drives cannot give 256 training nodes a single namespace for shared datasets or a globally visible home for checkpoints that survive a node crash. A parallel file system provides that shared tier across the cluster.

Definition 4.1: Parallel file system

Parallel file system (PFS) is a distributed storage architecture for ML training clusters that stripes data across many storage servers to provide aggregate throughput exceeding the capacity of any single device.

  1. Significance: A PFS aggregates storage I/O bandwidth \((\StorageBandwidth)\) across object storage servers (OSS). Twenty OSS nodes delivering 10 GB/s each offer 200 GB/s aggregate. When the client and network sustain that full rate, a 10 GB striped shard loads in about 50 ms rather than the 1 second a single 10 GB/s server would need. The rate that enters the data-movement term \(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\) of the single-machine iron law recalled in The Fleet Law is the bandwidth that reaches the actual consumer, not the aggregate rating.
  2. Distinction: Unlike network-attached storage, where every I/O request routes through a single server, a PFS client receives stripe location metadata from a dedicated metadata server (MDS) and then reads data directly from multiple OSS nodes in parallel. The MDS and OSS paths are architecturally separated, so data bandwidth scales with OSS count while metadata operations scale with MDS count.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a PFS has unlimited throughput if enough OSS nodes are added. In reality, every open, stat, and create passes through the MDS, whose service rate is finite and does not grow with the number of OSS nodes; a one-time burst of 10,000 opens does not by itself establish sustained saturation. The MDS becomes a throughput bottleneck when the aggregate metadata-operation arrival rate exceeds its service rate, regardless of spare bandwidth at the OSS nodes.

A Lustre-style parallel file system decouples metadata lookups from bulk data movement (figure 2), separating two concerns that traditional file systems handle together (Schwan 2003). Metadata servers (MDS) manage the namespace (persisting file layouts and attributes on metadata targets, or MDTs): file creation, directory listings, permission checks, and lock management. Object storage servers (OSS)6 manage the actual data blocks, each serving a stripe of every large file. When a client opens a 10 GB training shard, the MDS tells the client which OSS nodes hold which stripes, and the client reads from all of them in parallel. A Lustre7 deployment with 100 OSS nodes, each providing 10 GB/s, delivers an aggregate 1 TB/s.

Schwan, P. 2003. “Lustre: Building a File System for 1,000-Node Clusters.” Proceedings of the 2003 Linux Symposium, 380–86.

6 OSS (object storage server): In parallel file system terminology, “object” means a chunk of striped file data managed by an object storage target, a usage that predates cloud object storage (S3, GCS) by over a decade. Confusing the two is a common source of miscommunication: when a Lustre administrator says “add more OSS nodes,” they mean adding data-serving capacity to the parallel file system, not provisioning cloud buckets.

7 Lustre: A portmanteau of “Linux” and “cluster,” developed at Carnegie Mellon University and first deployed in production in 2003. Lustre is common in HPC and ML infrastructure because its architecture scales aggregate bandwidth linearly with the number of OSS nodes, the same property that makes it a natural choice for training clusters where a single job may demand hundreds of GB/s of sustained read throughput.

This abstract architecture has concrete implications for performance tuning. When a training job creates a new dataset directory on Lustre, the administrator configures the stripe count (the number of object storage targets, or OSTs, a file is spread across) and stripe size (the chunk size written to each OST) for that directory (Lustre Wiki 2017). For large sequential-read training shards, administrators may choose wider striping and multi-megabyte stripe sizes so a single file can draw bandwidth from multiple OSS nodes. Lustre’s striping guide treats wide striping as useful for very large files or files accessed by many clients, while smaller files often use fewer stripes to reduce overhead (Lustre Wiki 2019). A 4 GB data shard striped across 100 OSTs with 4 MB stripes places roughly 10 stripes on each OST. When a data loader reads large contiguous ranges, the Lustre client can issue parallel read requests to the OSS nodes holding those stripes, aggregating their bandwidth. The client also maintains its own read-ahead buffer, prefetching the next several stripes while the application processes the current ones. This file-system-level read-ahead is distinct from the data loader’s application-level prefetch buffer; the two layers of prefetching compound to provide deep latency hiding, making the physical distance to the OSS nodes nearly transparent to the training process.

Lustre Wiki. 2017. lfs setstripe: Set Striping Pattern of a File. Lustre Wiki manual page.
Lustre Wiki. 2019. Configuring Lustre File Striping. Lustre Wiki.

8 GPFS (General Parallel File System): Developed by IBM Research starting in 1998, now marketed as IBM Spectrum Scale. For ML checkpoint writes, its token-based distributed coordination can reduce reliance on a single dedicated metadata path, but it also makes lock behavior part of the performance envelope.

The separation between metadata and data paths is what enables the aggregate bandwidth that ML workloads demand. In GPFS8 (IBM Spectrum Scale), the architecture takes a different approach: it stripes data and metadata across shared disks and coordinates concurrent access through token-based distributed locking (Schmuck and Haskin 2002). The result is not a simple MDS/OSS split; it is a shared-disk design whose metadata and data placement can be distributed while the lock protocol preserves consistency across clients. The trade-off is greater complexity in lock management, which must scale to thousands of nodes.

The data path scales with the number of object storage servers, but every file still begins with a request to the metadata service, and that service has a finite operation rate. That finite rate is the source of the small file problem. The per-open cost that section 4.3.5 charged to one node’s CPU now lands on a metadata service shared by the whole fleet. Each open() system call requires a metadata lookup, lock acquisition, and attribute fetch. When a dataset of millions of 10 KB images is stored as individual files and 10,000 workers, a fleet several times the running cluster, each open a new file every few samples, the aggregate open rate exceeds the few hundred thousand operations per second an MDS typically serves. The MDS becomes the serialization point, and delivery collapses to its operation rate long before the data links saturate.

Definition 4.2: Small file problem

Small file problem is an ML data-loading pathology in which per-file operations overwhelm a storage system’s metadata service.

  1. Significance: open, stat, and close requests from 10,000 workers can serialize at the metadata service, idle the cluster, and reduce effective I/O bandwidth \((\StorageBandwidth)\).
  2. Distinction: This is a metadata request-rate and latency \((\Latency)\) problem, not a bulk bit-rate problem.
  3. Common pitfall: Faster SSDs do not fix this format problem. Packing samples into sequential containers such as TFRecord or Parquet amortizes metadata operations across many samples.

At production scale, the metadata bottleneck can collapse an otherwise high-bandwidth file system. When a cluster migrates from aggregated containers back to individual sample files, metadata request queues saturate long before the underlying storage servers or network links reach their byte-rate limits.

Example 4.1: The metadata meltdown
Scenario: A large-scale training cluster migrates from a preprocessed sequential dataset to 200 million raw image files stored individually on a parallel file system provisioned for 500 GB/s aggregate bandwidth.

Diagnosis: The file system delivers less than 1 percent of rated throughput. Millions of per-second stat() and open() calls overwhelm the metadata servers, choking data loaders despite abundant network and storage bandwidth.

Systems lesson: Metadata contention limits delivery when operation demand exceeds metadata service capacity. Bundling 200 million raw images into 50,000 large tar shards (for example, WebDataset) reduces the file count by 4,000× and amortizes per-file operations across samples. Packaging alone does not prevent startup contention if loaders still enumerate files with per-file stat() calls; distributing a precomputed dataset manifest removes those repeated lookups from the hot path. Measure metadata operation rate alongside byte bandwidth to determine which service limits delivery.

Striping and aggregation solve different problems: striping distributes large files across data servers, whereas aggregation, the shard packaging of section 4.3.5, reduces the number of files the MDS must track. Realized bandwidth then depends on the reader. A read contained within one stripe uses a single server, while enough concurrent reads spanning stripes can draw from several servers at once. Wider striping therefore helps only when read sizes and concurrency exploit those paths, and small files usually need fewer stripes.

Parallel file systems provide a shared namespace and lock management that support coordinated checkpoints (Schmuck and Haskin 2002), but the two ML write patterns need different guarantees from them. Immutable training shards remove conflicts with concurrent writers once the dataset version has been published, so readers need only agree on which completed version belongs to the run. A checkpoint needs more: file-system locks coordinate individual writes, and a completed write to one file does not establish that the other shards are complete, so a multi-file generation needs an application-level publication protocol, developed in section 4.6.

Schmuck, Frank, and Roger Haskin. 2002. “GPFS: A Shared-Disk File System for Large Computing Clusters.” Proceedings of the USENIX Conference on File and Storage Technologies (FAST ’02), 231–44.

The performance of a parallel file system depends on the balance of load across OSS nodes as well as their number. If a shard uses 10 of 100 equally provisioned OSS nodes, those servers offer at most 10 percent of the system’s aggregate server bandwidth. Realized delivery can be lower because of client limits, request size, or insufficient concurrency. Conversely, when many concurrent readers draw on shards striped across all 100 servers, demand spreads over every server and realized bandwidth can approach the system’s rating, provided the clients and network keep pace. Placement and access scheduling therefore matter alongside the number of storage servers, and a simple shard-assignment calculation shows how an imbalance can arise even when aggregate storage capacity looks ample.

Napkin Math 4.4: The thundering herd: Shard contention
Problem: A dataset is split into 1,000 shards on a shared file system. If 32 workers each pick a shard at random to start their next epoch, what is the probability that at least two GPUs “collide” on the same shard, creating a potential read hotspot? A shard collision does not imply a server collision unless the placement maps each shard to a distinct server.

Math: This is a variant of the “Birthday Problem” in probability.

  1. Probability of No Collision: \(\approx e^{-\ShardReaderCount^2/(2\DatasetShardCount)} = e^{-32^2/2{,}000} \approx 0.60\).
  2. Probability of Contention: \(1 - 0.60 = 40\%\).

Systems insight: With 32 workers and 1,000 shards, the 40.1 percent collision probability identifies a potential storage-shard hotspot. Whether that collision delays workers depends on shard placement, caching, and available service capacity. Coordinating shard assignments can reduce this source of tail latency when assignments spread demand across independently serving devices while preserving the intended sampling policy.

At the scale of thousands of nodes, tail latency (Dean and Barroso 2013a) can dominate exposed input wait. If a training step requires data from 100 storage servers and each independently has a 1 percent chance of being slow, the probability that all 100 respond quickly is \(0.99^{100} = 0.366\), meaning over 63 percent of these request sets contain at least one slow response. Shared congestion can correlate delays, changing that probability. A slow response delays the step only when prefetching fails to hide it and the required input lies on the step’s critical path. When it does, the delay is not confined to one worker, because synchronous data-parallel training waits for every worker at the next collective, so one late fetch stretches the whole fleet’s step.

Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013a. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80. https://doi.org/10.1145/2408776.2408794.
Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013b. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80.

Systems can mitigate tail latency through hedged requests: after a chosen delay, the client issues a redundant read to another replica and uses the first valid response. The trigger delay trades faster tail completion against extra work (Dean and Barroso 2013b).
