An independence model can multiply the probabilities of two slow replicas, but shared congestion and the later start of the hedge invalidate a simple squared-probability latency guarantee. Issuing a hedge at the median response time would duplicate roughly half of requests before accounting for cancellation; that load can worsen the congestion the scheme aims to avoid. A hedge therefore pays off only when its trigger sits in the measured tail and the duplicate traffic it adds stays within the spare service capacity.

↪ Tail latency as a serving constraint, where routing and load balancing decide which requests miss the p99 target, is developed in Load Balancing and Request Routing.

The effectiveness of hedged requests depends on the replicated data layout. If both replicas reside on OSS nodes that share the same network switch, a switch failure makes both copies simultaneously unavailable. Effective hedging requires failure-domain-aware placement: replicas should reside in different racks, connected through different top-of-rack switches, so that the failure of any single component affects at most one replica.

In production clusters, the parallel file system is a shared utility, simultaneously servicing many concurrent training jobs with different I/O patterns. At any given moment, a large language model job might be streaming sequentially through text shards, a computer vision job might be reading random image shards, and a checkpoint storm from a third job could be saturating write bandwidth. This concurrency gives rise to the noisy neighbor problem, where one job’s I/O pattern severely degrades performance for all other jobs. A checkpoint save that consumes a disproportionate share of the PFS’s internal network bandwidth causes read latency for other jobs to spike, potentially stalling their training pipelines. PFS administrators mitigate this through I/O scheduling policies (quality-of-service tiers, bandwidth quotas per job), but these policies add administrative complexity and can reduce peak single-job throughput.

A related challenge is namespace isolation. Different teams and workloads require different storage configurations. A team training on millions of 100 KB images first needs to aggregate samples to reduce metadata operations; increasing stripe count does not remove per-file metadata work. A team working with large video shards can use wider striping when read sizes and concurrency exploit the additional data paths. Misconfigured striping for one team’s workload can create hotspots that degrade performance for the entire shared file system. The impact of sharing is easy to quantify. A PFS with 1 TB/s aggregate bandwidth, shared across 10 concurrent training jobs, provides only 100 GB/s per job on average. If one job’s checkpoint storm consumes 400 GB/s for 10 seconds, the remaining nine jobs share 600 GB/s, a 33 percent reduction that can trigger data stalls in their accelerator pipelines.

Checkpoint 4.2: Parallel file system design

Consider a training cluster with 512 nodes, each running 8 GPUs. The training job requires 400 GB/s of aggregate read bandwidth.

Parallel file systems solve the shared-namespace problem for active training clusters, but their cost per byte is too high for the organization’s complete data holdings. The petabytes of raw training data, historical checkpoints, and preprocessed datasets that an ML organization accumulates over years require a tier optimized for capacity and durability rather than bandwidth.

4.3.7 Tier 4 object storage

Keeping petabytes of raw corpora, preprocessed shards, and historical checkpoints on a parallel file system would pay high-bandwidth server rates for data that sits mostly at rest. At petabyte scale, object storage provides the durable, lower-cost foundation of the hierarchy. Services like Amazon S3 and Google Cloud Storage organize data in object namespaces where objects are retrieved by keys rather than by POSIX directory paths (Amazon Web Services 2026c; Google Cloud 2026). Unlike file systems, which organize data in hierarchical directories with metadata-rich operations (rename, link, permission inheritance), object storage treats each object as an opaque blob identified by a unique key. The simpler interface changes the namespace operations exposed to clients; the service still manages metadata for placement, versions, access control, and integrity as capacity grows.

Amazon Web Services. 2026c. Naming Amazon S3 Objects. Amazon S3 User Guide.
Google Cloud. 2026. About Cloud Storage Objects. Google Cloud Documentation.

9 Eleven nines (99.999999999 percent durability): A design target of 11 nines annual durability corresponds to an expected loss probability of about \(10^{-11}\) per object-year; for one million objects, that is about one expected object loss per 100,000 years.

10 Erasure coding: Built on Reed-Solomon codes (Reed and Solomon 1960), which use maximum-distance-separable coding to recover the original data from any sufficient subset of encoded fragments. In storage systems, Reed-Solomon-style codes and later local-reconstruction variants trade storage overhead against repair bandwidth and degraded-read cost (Plank 1997; Huang et al. 2012). Local-reconstruction codes, for example, read fewer surviving fragments to rebuild a lost one, cutting repair traffic at the price of a little extra capacity.

Reed, I. S., and G. Solomon. 1960. “Polynomial Codes over Certain Finite Fields.” Journal of the Society for Industrial and Applied Mathematics 8 (2): 300–304. https://doi.org/10.1137/0108018.
Plank, James S. 1997. “A Tutorial on Reed-Solomon Coding for Fault-Tolerance in RAID-Like Systems.” Software: Practice and Experience 27 (9): 995–1012. https://doi.org/10.1002/(SICI)1097-024X(199709)27:9<995::AID-SPE111>3.0.CO;2-6.
Huang, Cheng, Huseyin Simitci, Yikang Xu, Aaron Ogus, Brad Calder, Parikshit Gopalan, Jin Li, and Sergey Yekhanin. 2012. “Erasure Coding in Windows Azure Storage.” 2012 USENIX Annual Technical Conference (USENIX ATC 12), 15–26.

Services such as Amazon S3 publish an “eleven nines”9 annual durability target (99.999999999 percent). Erasure coding,10 replication, placement, and repair contribute to storage protection, but a coding scheme alone does not establish a service’s durability target. Selecting a source-of-truth tier for training data and checkpoint archives requires checking the chosen service’s failure model alongside versioning, lifecycle policy, access control, and recovery testing.

Three different guarantees govern whether that source of truth is usable. Durability asks whether acknowledged data survives failures; availability asks whether the storage service responds when a client needs it; and consistency asks which version a read is allowed to observe when writes overlap or replicas disagree. Replication can improve durability and availability without by itself guaranteeing that a reader sees the newest committed checkpoint. A recovery design must therefore state all three properties instead of treating a durability percentage as a complete reliability contract.

Definition 4.3: Erasure coding

Erasure coding protects stored data by adding redundant fragments, with the Reed-Solomon scheme considered here encoding \(\ErasureDataFragmentCount\) data and \(\ErasureParityFragmentCount\) parity blocks so that any \(\ErasureDataFragmentCount\) remaining fragments recover the original.

  1. Significance: Coding can protect against fragment loss with less capacity overhead than full replication. A 4+2 Reed-Solomon code stores four data fragments and two parity fragments, tolerating two unavailable fragments at 1.5\(\times\) storage overhead instead of the 3\(\times\) overhead of triple replication. Actual durability also depends on failure correlation, placement, detection, and repair.
  2. Distinction: Unlike replication (which stores complete copies), erasure coding uses mathematical encoding (for example, Reed-Solomon) to distribute redundant information across different failure domains (disks, racks, or sites).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that erasure coding is “free durability.” In reality, it is a latency-compute trade-off: reconstructing data after a failure requires additional CPU cycles and increases the p99 latency of the storage read.

Erasure coding lets the service recover data without requiring the training application to manage fragments. In the form considered here, the service retains the original \(\ErasureDataFragmentCount\) data fragments alongside parity, so healthy reads can use the data fragments directly. When a required fragment is unavailable, reconstruction uses surviving data and parity fragments. The object interface hides that recovery procedure, but additional I/O, network traffic, and decoding can remain visible as longer read latency. Protection against data loss does not imply unchanged service time during a failure.

Object storage also costs less per byte. At a representative $0.02/GB/month, its recurring capacity rate is 5× lower than the local-NVMe rate assumed here, and section 4.8 converts that ratio into annual costs for a 100 TB dataset. Comparing it with HBM acquisition cost additionally requires a hardware lifetime and utilization assumption. This cost advantage makes object storage a common home for the organization’s training data lake, where raw data, preprocessed datasets, and archived model artifacts can reside durably.

Request latency also matters. Design estimates use 50 to 100 ms for an object GET as a representative planning range rather than a universal service guarantee. Much of that delay is the price of the indirection that provides durability. The service looks up the object’s location in a distributed metadata index, determines which fragments to read, fetches them from potentially different storage nodes, and reconstructs data when a fragment is unavailable, all on top of the client network path and queueing. Large transfers amortize per-request overhead across more bytes, while small transfers can spend most of their elapsed time on that overhead. Request size and concurrency therefore enter the delivery budget alongside aggregate bandwidth.

The remedy is the shard packaging of section 4.3.5, applied here to request latency rather than metadata operations. Formats like WebDataset (Aizman et al. 2019) or Mosaic Streaming (Databricks 2026) bundle samples into multi-gigabyte shards that transform high-latency random access into high-bandwidth sequential streaming. Rather than issuing billions of small GET requests, the data loader issues hundreds of large reads in parallel, each fetching an entire shard that contains thousands of samples. With sufficient parallelism and colocated network capacity, object storage can sustain high aggregate throughput, enough to feed large training clusters for some workloads. The streaming pattern works by assigning each data loader worker a subset of shards and having it read them sequentially. Within each shard, samples are stored contiguously, so the worker decodes them in order and shuffles them in a local buffer. This approach achieves pseudo-random access across the dataset while maintaining sequential I/O at the storage level.

Aizman, Alex, Gavin Maltby, and Thomas Breuel. 2019. “High Performance I/O for Large Scale Deep Learning.” 2019 IEEE International Conference on Big Data (Big Data), 5965–67. https://doi.org/10.1109/bigdata47090.2019.9005703.
Databricks. 2026. Mosaic Streaming: Main Concepts. Databricks Mosaic AI Training Documentation.

The architecture of a streaming data loader for object storage differs from a local-storage loader in important ways. A local-storage loader can use memory-mapped I/O to treat the dataset as a virtual memory region, relying on the operating system’s page fault mechanism to load data on demand. This approach is elegant but incompatible with object storage, which does not support the POSIX file system interface that memory mapping requires. Instead, an object-storage loader must explicitly manage HTTP connections, issue ranged GET requests for specific byte ranges within shards, handle retries on transient failures, and manage a local buffer pool for decoded samples. Libraries like WebDataset and Mosaic Streaming encapsulate this complexity, presenting a simple iterator interface to the training loop while managing the HTTP transport and buffering internally.

Applying this streaming architecture to the running example makes the design concrete as an alternative to the staged path the chapter adopts. Instead of copying shards to local NVMe at job start, the cluster would read them from the object store throughout the run. The tokenized training set is stored in an S3-like object store as roughly 2,000 shards of 3 GB each. The streaming architecture assigns each of the 256 compute nodes a unique subset of roughly 8 shards. Within each node, 8 data loader worker processes issue concurrent HTTP Range requests to fetch 256 MB chunks from their assigned shards. Across the cluster, this results in 2,048 concurrent requests, allowing aggregate throughput in the 50–100 GB/s range when the object store and network path are provisioned for it, far exceeding the roughly 167.8 MB/s required by the generic text job in the text-versus-image comparison. The running example’s average input demand is lower still. At these provisioned service rates, the network between the compute virtual private cloud (VPC) and the object-storage endpoint can limit maximum streaming throughput without making input delivery the running job’s bottleneck. Organizations that co-locate compute and storage in the same cloud availability zone can reduce cross-network latency, directly reducing the required prefetch buffer depth and improving overall pipeline efficiency.

That request parallelism is what object storage rewards. Each of the 2,048 GET requests is independent and can be served by a different storage node within the cloud provider’s infrastructure, and the count grows with the fleet. With each request fetching a 256 MB chunk of a shard, the aggregate throughput depends on the cloud provider’s network capacity, internal bandwidth, and request-rate quotas. The practical limit is often the network bandwidth between the compute cluster and the object storage service, not the storage service’s internal media bandwidth.

Many object storage services provide strong read-after-write consistency, which means that a subsequent read of an acknowledged object sees the completed write. Amazon S3 announced strong read-after-write consistency for object writes and overwrites, together with strongly consistent listing, in December 2020 (Amazon Web Services 2020). The relevant contract is the documented behavior of the chosen backend, not a blanket assumption about object storage. Per-object consistency still does not make a checkpoint spanning many objects atomic, so the publication protocol of section 4.6 applies here as it does on a file system.

Amazon Web Services. 2020. Amazon S3 Update – Strong Read-After-Write Consistency. AWS News Blog.
Amazon Web Services. 2026a. Checking Object Integrity in Amazon S3. Amazon S3 User Guide.

The scale of ML datasets makes them vulnerable to silent data corruption: subtle bit flips in the storage medium or during network transfer that can introduce training artifacts nearly impossible to diagnose. Object storage services provide per-object checksums that verify integrity on upload, copy, or download; Amazon S3, for example, documents checksum options including CRC-family and cryptographic hashes (Amazon Web Services 2026a). Parallel file systems often rely on underlying RAID or erasure coding for physical integrity but may not provide end-to-end checksums visible to the application. A well-engineered large-scale ML pipeline computes and stores a checksum for each data shard during preprocessing and verifies it when the shard is first loaded by a training worker. The verification overhead is negligible compared to the cost of training on corrupted data, and checkpoints need the same check, because a flipped bit in restored state can make training diverge silently after recovery.

↪ Detecting and verifying silent data corruption across the fleet, including in restored checkpoints, is developed in Check-and-Verify Defenses Against Silent Data Corruption.

Training datasets can contain sensitive information, including personal data, proprietary text, and licensed images. Storage design must carry the organization’s access and retention policies through every copy. Object services expose identity policies and encryption options; parallel file systems provide POSIX permissions and access-control lists. Audit coverage and enforcement depend on the configured platform, not the tier’s name. Versioned manifests should identify derived shards and retained copies so that access reviews, retention decisions, and deletion workflows can locate the relevant artifacts; a deletion request, for example, must reach every immutable preprocessed shard derived from the deleted record. Storage controls protect stored bytes; evaluating what a trained model retains requires additional model-level analysis.

The transition from Tier 4 to Tier 5 is driven primarily by economics. Data that is accessed rarely should move to archive storage when retrieval latency and governance requirements allow it, because archive capacity is cheaper still.

4.3.8 Tier 5 archive and cold storage

Archive services such as S3 Glacier preserve rarely accessed lineage—superseded checkpoints, raw source corpora, and training logs—over multi-year horizons so that a deployed model can still be reproduced or audited years after its run ends. At a representative $0.004/GB/month, archive storage is 5× cheaper than the $0.02/GB/month object-storage assumption used in section 4.3.7, trading that lower capacity rate for retrieval latency measured in minutes to hours and making this tier unsuitable for operational access. The record at stake is training data provenance: the link from a deployed model to the exact data versions, hyperparameters, and preprocessing that produced it. The organization’s governance policy determines which records to retain and for how long, and the storage design must keep them identifiable and retrievable for that period; archive serves when its retrieval delay meets the requirement, while records needed during frequent investigations may justify a warmer tier.

Automated lifecycle policies govern the transition of data between tiers based on access recency. As one illustration of how such automation is expressed, a time-based rule for ML infrastructure might move checkpoints as follows. The most recent three checkpoints remain on the parallel file system for fast recovery. Checkpoints older than 72 hours are transitioned to object storage, and checkpoints older than 90 days are transitioned to archive storage. Checkpoints older than two years are deleted unless flagged for regulatory retention. S3 Lifecycle rules provide the same general transition/archive/delete mechanism for object lifetimes (Amazon Web Services 2026b). The running example’s checkpoint retention in section 4.6 adopts a count-based variant of the same idea, where the transition rule keys on checkpoint count rather than age. Without automation, teams either hoard data on expensive tiers (wasting budget) or delete data too aggressively (losing reproducibility). The lifecycle policy encodes the organization’s cost-recovery trade-off in a declarative rule that requires no manual intervention.

Amazon Web Services. 2026b. Managing the Lifecycle of Objects. Amazon S3 User Guide.

For the 175B model training run, the archive tier holds the complete training lineage: the raw 3 TB compressed source corpus, the 6 TB serialized shard set, all preprocessing scripts, the final checkpoint, and a sampled subset of intermediate checkpoints. Capacity must include every retained full checkpoint as well as both corpus representations; table 5 computes that total for the declared retention policy. A round dataset-only allowance would underprovision the archive. The asymmetry between archive cost and compute cost makes retention inexpensive relative to the run it documents; failures to archive usually reflect missing lifecycle ownership, unclear retention policy, or retrieval-process neglect rather than raw capacity cost.

Archive storage services offer multiple retrieval speed tiers, each with different pricing. In an S3 Glacier-style service, standard retrieval may complete within hours at moderate cost, expedited retrieval may complete within minutes at a higher cost, and bulk retrieval may trade still more latency for the lowest retrieval price (Amazon Web Services 2026d). The choice of retrieval tier depends on the urgency of the use case. Forensic analysis of a production incident warrants faster retrieval; annual compliance audits can use slower bulk retrieval. Designing the lifecycle policy requires understanding the storage cost, the expected retrieval frequency, and the urgency for each class of data.

Amazon Web Services. 2026d. Understanding Archive Retrieval Options. Amazon S3 User Guide.

The six tiers now have their roles, capacities, and prices, but a role does not say how fast bytes must climb the hierarchy. That rate is set by the accelerators consuming them, and it is the next quantity to derive.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Order the six tiers from Tier 0 through Tier 5 in the chapter’s placement hierarchy, moving outward from accelerator-resident memory through node-local resources to shared storage and archive: (1) Local NVMe SSD, (2) Object Storage, (3) Host DRAM, (4) GPU HBM, (5) Parallel File System, (6) Archive / Cold Storage.

  2. Host DRAM (Tier 1) executes a four-stage data loader pipeline: Read, Decode, Augment, and Collate. Why place the final collated batch in page-locked (‘pinned’) host memory for direct asynchronous DMA to GPU HBM without an intermediate staging copy?

    1. Pinned memory automatically compresses tensors using host CPU vector units before PCIe transmission.
    2. Pinned memory locks physical pages in host RAM so the PCIe DMA controller can copy data directly to GPU memory without an intermediate pinned staging copy.
    3. Pinned memory replicates the batch across all NUMA nodes to ensure uniform memory access for parallel data loader threads.
    4. Pinned memory guarantees transactional ACID durability on host storage in case the worker process crashes during transfer.

  3. A vision training cluster migrating to a high-performance parallel file system (PFS) with \(500\text{ GB/s}\) rated aggregate bandwidth reports that throughput during ImageNet training is capped at under \(5\text{ GB/s}\), with storage data disks showing negligible I/O activity. The dataset consists of millions of individual JPEG files. A worker trace shows repeated waits for file opens, and the MDS reports a sustained operation queue with rising lookup latency while its service rate is saturated. What bottleneck do these additional measurements establish, and which fix addresses it?

    1. The network fabric is dropping packets due to congestion; enable flow control on top-of-rack switches.
    2. The parallel file system is bottlenecked on drive endurance wear-leveling; replace SSDs with high-DWPD enterprise drives.
    3. The dataset is stored as millions of individual image files, saturating the Metadata Server (MDS) with per-file POSIX lookups; repackage images into large sequential shards (such as WebDataset tar archives).
    4. The GPUs are compute-bound and cannot consume data faster; decrease the batch size to increase I/O request frequency.

  4. A training node uses four local NVMe drives configured in RAID-0 as a warm cache for \(25\text{ TB}\) of pre-shuffled training shards. Explain why RAID-0 is an appropriate engineering trade-off for this specific tier, and identify what workload change would make RAID-0 catastrophic.

  5. A multi-modal training job combines \(3\text{ TB}\) of text, \(50\text{ TB}\) of images, and \(200\text{ TB}\) of video. The infrastructure team provisions storage bandwidth based exclusively on the video modality because video represents over \(75\%\) of total dataset capacity. What can be concluded about this sizing decision from the supplied information?

    1. Adequacy cannot be established from corpus capacity alone; sizing must include the combined sampled-batch byte rate and decoding, augmentation, and buffering demand of all active modalities.
    2. The video stream will starve the parallel file system because image and text files use incompatible POSIX stripe sizes.
    3. The model will fail to converge because object storage cannot maintain ACID consistency when serving multiple modalities simultaneously.
    4. The training job will exhaust GPU HBM because video tensors automatically overwrite text embeddings during collation.

  6. In a parallel file system architecture like Lustre, namespace management (file creation, directory paths, locks) is handled by a dedicated ____, while raw data blocks are striped across multiple Object Storage Servers (OSS).

See Answers →

4.4 The Data Pipeline Equation

Whether a training job streams from object storage or reads from local NVMe, each worker’s next batch must reach HBM before the current compute step finishes; falling short starves the fleet’s accelerators, while provisioning far above demand strands capital in idle storage bandwidth. Sizing the hierarchy therefore begins by turning the accelerator consumption rate into a required byte rate. The data pipeline throughput equation in equation 2 quantifies the bandwidth the pipeline must sustain for a target training rate:

\[\RequiredInputBandwidth = \InputConsumerAcceleratorCount \times \TargetComputeTimeFraction\times \frac{\BatchDataVolume}{\IronLawComputeTime} \tag{2}\]

where \(\InputConsumerAcceleratorCount\) counts accelerators consuming distinct local input batches, \(\TargetComputeTimeFraction\) is the target fraction of wall-clock time spent computing (typically 0.8 to 0.95), \(\BatchDataVolume\) is one local batch in bytes, and \(\IronLawComputeTime\) is its uninterrupted compute time. This utilization is an active-time fraction, not model FLOPs utilization. If iteration time already includes stalls, multiplying by utilization again would count the slowdown twice. Eliminating sustained input stalls requires supplying batches at the full compute rate; the target fraction sizes a lower accepted training rate. Devices that cooperate on one batch do not each require an independent storage read, so their shared input must be counted once at the tier being sized. The text-versus-image comparison of notebook 4.1 already applied this equation with \(\TargetComputeTimeFraction= 1\); the equation makes the target fraction explicit, and the service chain below adds the requirement that every stage, rather than the accelerator-facing endpoint alone, meet the resulting rate.

The demand equation sizes the accelerator-facing endpoint, but bytes must traverse the complete service chain. Let \(\InputStageServiceRate_{\InputPipelineStageIndex}\) denote the measured service rate of stage \(\InputPipelineStageIndex\), including object-store reads, network transfer, decoding and augmentation, host-memory copies, and host-to-device transfer. Express all rates in the same output-equivalent bytes per second, using each stage’s expansion or compression ratio; raw compressed-read bandwidth cannot be compared directly with decoded-tensor bandwidth. With bounded buffers and no sustained backlog, the pipeline can deliver at most

\[\InputPipelineServiceRate= \min_{\InputPipelineStageIndex} \InputStageServiceRate_{\InputPipelineStageIndex}, \qquad \InputPipelineServiceRate\geq \RequiredInputBandwidth. \tag{3}\]

Capacity at one tier cannot compensate for a slower downstream stage. Compression may raise the storage-read rate while lowering the decode rate; additional loader workers may raise decoding throughput while saturating the host-memory or PCIe stage. The end-to-end inequality in equation 3 therefore turns the chapter’s hierarchy into a testable contract. Measure every stage under representative concurrency, then provision or overlap the stage whose service rate is the minimum.

The throughput equation in equation 2 exposes four levers on storage demand, and none of them is free. Reducing the accelerator count (\(\InputConsumerAcceleratorCount\)) lowers aggregate input demand by reducing the number of distinct consumers, but it lowers training throughput with it, whereas lowering target utilization (\(\TargetComputeTimeFraction\)) relaxes the delivery rate only by accepting idle accelerators. Decreasing the per-batch data volume (\(\BatchDataVolume\)) through a more compact representation of the same samples helps only while decoding keeps pace. Shrinking the batch itself changes the training configuration and its statistical efficiency. Conversely, increasing compute time (\(\IronLawComputeTime\)) gives storage longer to deliver each batch, at the cost of proportionally longer iterations. This is also why a faster accelerator can expose an input bottleneck that a slower one hid. The equation therefore sizes the pipeline for a chosen training configuration; deliberately slowing useful computation does not improve delivery.

The consequences of falling short are captured by the data stall ratio, defined in equation 4 as the fraction of each training step where the accelerator waits for data:

\[\text{Data Stall \%} = \frac{\TrainingStepTime- \IronLawComputeTime}{\TrainingStepTime} \times 100 \tag{4}\]

The data stall ratio covers one worker’s input path with communication excluded, so its \(\TrainingStepTime\) is compute plus exposed input wait. The Fleet Law of The Fleet Law carries no input-wait term, so it implicitly treats the next batch as already resident. Whatever input wait the loader fails to hide adds to the distributed step on top of \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) and \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\), as compute (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_1\)) capacity that sits idle while storage catches up. In a real distributed job, the gap \(\TrainingStepTime- \IronLawComputeTime\) also contains exposed communication and synchronization, so the ratio implicates storage only when that gap is measured as time spent waiting on the input queue, separately from collectives and barriers. Without overlap, step time adds compute and I/O time. Pipelining reduces step time to the longer of the two durations and stall time to the positive difference between I/O and compute.

Napkin Math 4.5: The data stall ratio
Problem: A GPU processes a batch in 200 ms, but storage takes 250 ms to deliver the next batch. What fraction of each training step is the accelerator idle, and how much does pipelining I/O with compute reduce that stall?

Without pipelining (sequential I/O then compute):

\[T_{\text{step}} = T_{\text{IO}} + T_{\text{compute}} = 250 + 200 = 450 \text{ ms}\] \[\text{Stall \%} = \frac{250}{450} = 55.6\%\]

With pipelining (I/O overlapped with compute):

\[T_{\text{step}} = \max(200, 250) = 250 \text{ ms}\] \[\text{Stall \%} = \frac{250 - 200}{250} = {20\%}\]

Systems insight: Even with pipelining, the accelerator is idle 20 percent of the time. To eliminate the stall entirely, the next batch must arrive within the 200 ms compute window, either by increasing effective storage bandwidth or by adding parallel I/O streams. Deeper prefetch buffers cannot help here, because the delivery time exceeds the compute window on every step rather than merely in the tail.

The data stall ratio measures time lost waiting for inputs relative to the chosen iteration window. Acceptable stall time depends on the fleet’s cost and throughput objectives; a threshold is a policy choice, not a universal storage-health boundary. Instrument batch-ready times and accelerator execution in the same trace to distinguish input starvation from synchronization, CPU preprocessing, and other idle intervals. General profiling tools can expose these events, but an idle accelerator alone does not identify storage as the cause.

↪ An I/O-wait efficiency target for the fleet Data axis is developed in Fleet diagnostic scorecard.

A storage pipeline that met its budget at launch can drift out of it as datasets grow, jobs contend, and drives age, so it requires continuous monitoring to keep silent bottlenecks from eroding training efficiency. Key metrics to track in real time include per-tier bandwidth utilization (NVMe, network, object storage), prefetch buffer depth (how many batches are queued and ready), the data stall ratio per GPU, the parallel file system’s metadata operation rate, and NVMe drive health indicators (wear level, temperature, error counts). Alert thresholds should trigger before stalls become visible in training loss curves. An undetected 5 percent increase in data stall ratio across the 1,024-GPU reference cluster (half the running cluster) for just one week wastes about $17,203 in idle compute (1,024 GPUs \(\times\) $2/GPU-hour \(\times\) 168 hours \(\times\) 5 percent). Comprehensive storage monitoring is therefore a first-order economic concern rather than an operational nicety.

Diagnose a storage stall at three levels. The application timeline identifies when the accelerator waits; per-worker rates and queue depth identify the pipeline stage that falls behind; device and shared-server counters show which resource limits that stage. Correlating those observations distinguishes a byte-bandwidth shortage from metadata contention or insufficient decoding capacity. Because a small development dataset may hide the contention and accumulated state of a production run, a pipeline stress test should use representative data volume and concurrency and run long enough to expose queue depletion, resource leaks, and memory pressure before the loader is coupled to an expensive training job.

The terms in equation 2 explain why the chapter’s workloads place such different demands on the same cluster. In the bandwidth comparison of notebook 4.1, image classification with ResNet-50 on ImageNet combines large compressed samples (116 KB per image on average) with short iteration times (100 to 200 ms), pushing \(\BatchDataVolume / \IronLawComputeTime\) high, whereas language-model pretraining packs 4,096 4-byte token IDs into 16 KB per sequence even though total volume over the run still reaches trillions of tokens. Recommendation model training, the Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) workload, combines both access modes: embedding lookups require high-IOPS random access to the embedding table while the dense layers stream sequential training batches. Which storage tier binds therefore follows the workload’s access profile rather than cluster size alone. Archetype C (Federated MobileNet) falls outside this shared-storage analysis, because its raw training data stays on the devices that produce it; there is no central corpus to stage, and its storage constraints become per-device capacity and upload limits.

↪ On-device training data and federated updates are developed in Edge Intelligence.

Devarajan, Hariharan, Huihuo Zheng, Anthony Kougkas, Xian-He Sun, and Venkatram Vishwanath. 2021. “DLIO: A Data-Centric Benchmark for Scientific Deep Learning Applications.” 2021 IEEE/ACM 21st International Symposium on Cluster, Cloud and Internet Computing (CCGrid), 81–91. https://doi.org/10.1109/CCGrid51090.2021.00018.

MLPerf Storage measures these profiles on production storage systems. Each submission emulates a set of accelerators that consume samples with a fixed per-sample compute time, so the storage system, not a real GPU, sets the pace, and reports the read throughput the system sustained while keeping the emulated accelerators at least 90 percent busy (70 percent for CosmoFlow) (MLCommons 2024; Devarajan et al. 2021). Across the 124 passing results of the closed division in the 2024 round, sustained throughput grows in proportion to the number of emulated accelerators, as the \(\InputConsumerAcceleratorCount\) term of equation 2 predicts (figure 3, panel a). Per accelerator, the median demand differs about 15× across workloads, and ResNet-50, although demanding among image-classification jobs, is the lightest of the three because its samples are small 2D images while 3D U-Net reads whole medical volumes. An emulated H100 needs about 2.8 GB/s for 3D U-Net on medical volumes, 0.55 GB/s for CosmoFlow, and 0.18 GB/s for ResNet-50 (figure 3, panel b). Emulating H100s instead of A100s raises every workload’s demand by 1.6–2×, because the faster accelerator shortens \(\IronLawComputeTime\) while the bytes per batch stay the same. A storage tier sized for one accelerator generation is therefore undersized for the next, even when the dataset and the model do not change.

In an ideal overlapped pipeline, the bandwidth-to-demand ratio bounds the compute active-time fraction (figure 4). With input demand defined as batch bytes divided by uninterrupted compute time, the frontier ramps linearly through bandwidth-starved operating points before flattening at the supply-equals-demand saturation knee (ratio = 1). The active fraction is the smaller of one and available bandwidth divided by that demand, assuming no other bottleneck.

Below matching supply, bandwidth increases raise the ideal active fraction proportionally. Above it, more bandwidth does not improve this ideal compute stage, although headroom can help absorb contention and variable delivery. The markers show how far apart the chapter’s workloads sit. Served by the whole 1 TB/s parallel file system, the text job of notebook 4.1 has about 6,000× more bandwidth than it consumes, the image job only 3.3×, and the pipeline of notebook 4.5 sits left of the knee at 80 percent active time. Real systems can fall below the curve because of preprocessing, latency tails, or synchronization. The graph identifies a workload service-rate boundary; it does not establish that every job needs every storage tier.

Because \(\RequiredInputBandwidth\) grows linearly with \(\InputConsumerAcceleratorCount\), adding compute nodes against a fixed shared file system moves any workload leftward along figure 4 until supply equals demand; past that saturation knee, additional GPUs add cost without raising training throughput, and equation 2 locates that limit from the storage tier’s measured bandwidth. Even to the right of the knee, however, the frontier in figure 4 assumes that storage reads and CPU preparation overlap computation completely and that latency spikes never empty the input queue. Achieving that overlap in practice requires pipelining the loader stages and sizing a prefetch buffer deep enough to absorb tail delays.

4.4.1 Pipelining and prefetching

When a data loader waits until the GPU finishes batch \(\BatchIndex\) before reading and decoding batch \(\BatchIndex+1\), the accelerator sits idle across the entire storage read and CPU preparation window on every step, as panel (a) of figure 5 shows. Pipelining removes that synchronous stall by staging and preparing batch \(\BatchIndex+1\) on host resources while the GPU trains on batch \(\BatchIndex\), turning the sum of input and compute time into their maximum (D·A·M coordination from sum to max). When delivery keeps pace and enough prepared work sits in host memory to absorb latency variation, four batches finish in about the time sequential loading needs for two (panel (b)); at fleet scale, the open question is how deep that buffer of prepared work must be.

Pipelining converts a sequential bottleneck into overlapped execution (figure 5), but continuous saturation requires a prefetch buffer deep enough to absorb variance in I/O latency. Stage durations need not be deterministic if ready batches cover the delays. Storage systems experience I/O jitter: variation in read latency caused by contention and background work. NVMe drives suffer occasional latency spikes during internal garbage collection (when the controller reorganizes NAND flash blocks) or wear-leveling operations. Parallel file systems incur spikes when multiple training jobs contend for the same OSS nodes, when the MDS processes a burst of metadata operations, or when background scrubbing detects and repairs bit errors. Object storage likewise exposes delays from shared-service contention and maintenance.

A single slow I/O creates a “bubble” when it exhausts the pipeline’s ready work. If the CPU is preparing batch \(\BatchIndex+1\) with no additional batches ready, a delayed read can leave batch \(\BatchIndex+1\) unavailable when the GPU finishes batch \(\BatchIndex\). The GPU then idles until the batch is ready. A longer queue tolerates a longer interruption, and delivery faster than consumption can refill it afterward. Rather than preparing just one batch ahead, the CPU maintains a queue of \(\PrefetchBufferCount\) preloaded batches.

A buffer sized to the measured p99 I/O delay needs enough ready batches to cover that delay without draining:

\[\MinimumPrefetchBufferCount= \left\lceil \frac{\TailStepIoTime}{\IronLawComputeTime} \right\rceil \tag{5}\]

The prefetch depth equation in equation 5 estimates how many ready batches cover the selected latency window. Outstanding reads contribute to replenishing that buffer but do not count as ready batches until they complete. The equation assumes that the long-run data supply rate is at least as high as the accelerator’s consumption rate; prefetching absorbs latency variance, not a sustained bandwidth deficit. When average throughput is sufficient but p99 latency exceeds compute time, deeper prefetching absorbs delays within the provisioned window; rarer or correlated delays can still cause stalls. When sustained delivery is lower than demand, identify the limiting stage before choosing more storage bandwidth, additional decoding capacity, a different representation, or local staging. The equation uses the p99 delay rather than the mean because a buffer sized for the mean drains whenever a slow read arrives, and the tail-latency arithmetic of section 4.3.6 shows that when a step depends on many servers, some read is slow on most steps. Even the p99 window leaves rarer events and correlated interruptions to test.

If object-storage reads take 500 ms at the 99th percentile and compute takes 200 ms, then \(\MinimumPrefetchBufferCount= 3\) batches; provisioning five ready batches adds a margin. A data loader’s configured work-ahead count (for example, a per-worker prefetch factor) is not a count of ready batches, because some of that work may still be loading or preprocessing. The quantity that protects the accelerator is completed-queue occupancy and its refill rate, measured against the delay window on whichever tier serves the reads.

A 500 ms p99 read spans 2.5 steps of 200 ms, so three ready batches must cover it.

To illustrate the memory cost of prefetch buffer depth, return to the image job of notebook 4.1. Each GPU processes its batch in roughly 200 ms, and once its 256 images are decoded into \(224{\times}224{\times}3\)-byte tensors, the collated batch occupies about 40 MB per GPU. Reading from local NVMe, the p99 I/O latency for that batch is approximately 50 ms. Under the p99-window model, the minimum ready-buffer depth is \(\lceil 50/200 \rceil = 1\) batch, and this example provisions a depth of 2. Reading from a parallel file system, the p99 I/O latency rises to roughly 200 ms due to network jitter and contention, requiring a minimum depth of \(\lceil 200/200 \rceil = 1\), with an illustrative provisioned depth of 3. Reading from object storage, the p99 latency can exceed 500 ms, requiring a depth of at least 3, with an illustrative provisioned depth of 5 or more. These numbers translate directly into host DRAM consumption. At 40 MB per batch, a depth-5 prefetch buffer per GPU consumes 200 MB, and 8 GPUs per node consume 1.6 GB. At 40 MB per batch with a depth of 1, the same node needs only 320 MB. Measured delivery delays, batch size, and the chosen protection window determine this memory budget. Queue occupancy and refill rate must still be tested under correlated delays.

The representation decides whether that depth is cheap. Text barely registers, because a per-GPU batch of a few packed 4,096-token sequences occupies tens of kilobytes, so even a deep text queue is negligible. Decoded images are where depth costs memory. The 256-image batch above is roughly 39 MB before rounding, and a buffer of 8 such batches consumes about 308 MB per GPU, modest against a node’s 512 GB of DRAM even after multiplying by the GPUs on the node. Higher-resolution images and video raise the per-batch size by an order of magnitude or more, at which point the prefetch buffer competes with embedding tables and other DRAM consumers.

Prefetching and the storage hierarchy together form a layered defense against stalls. The prefetch buffer in host DRAM absorbs I/O variance from NVMe reads. The local NVMe warm cache absorbs network variance from shared storage (whether a parallel file system or an object store). The parallel file system read-ahead cache absorbs variance from disk seeks across the OSS cluster. Because each layer adds latency tolerance at the cost of memory or storage capacity, the system designer must check each active path against the interruption window it is intended to absorb and verify that sustained delivery replenishes the consumed batches. The running example’s shards come from object storage, whose 500 ms p99 against a 200 ms step would call for 3 ready batches if every read went to that tier. Once the shards are staged on local NVMe, every training read is a local hit, and the prefetch buffer needs to absorb only the 50 ms local p99, which a single ready batch covers.

Checkpoint 4.3: Data pipeline design

A training cluster the size of the book’s reference cluster runs 1,024 GPUs with 128-image batches (116 KB per image after compression, 150 ms per iteration).

4.4.2 Multi-worker data loading

A single CPU core cannot keep a high-throughput accelerator fed, even when the storage hardware is fast enough, because of the CPU work required between the storage read and the GPU transfer. For image training, decoding a 116 KB JPEG image into a \(224{\times}224{\times}3\) raw tensor costs several milliseconds of CPU time. One measurement decoded full-resolution ImageNet images at about 530 per second across the four virtual CPUs of a cloud inference instance (Kang et al. 2020), and each augmentation, such as a random crop, flip, or color jitter, adds further work per image. The image job of notebook 4.1 feeds each GPU 256 images every 200 ms step, more than 1,000 images per second. At 1,000 images per second per GPU and 8 GPUs per node, a single core would need to decode and augment 8,000 images per second, tens of times what a single core can sustain. The solution is multi-worker data loading, where \(\DataLoaderWorkerCount\) worker processes each read from storage, decode, augment, and enqueue batches independently. The effective I/O throughput scales approximately linearly with \(\DataLoaderWorkerCount\) until one of three bottlenecks is reached: the storage device’s bandwidth saturates, the PCIe bus between host and device saturates, or the workers exhaust host CPU cycles.

Kang, Daniel, Ankit Mathur, Teja Veeramacheneni, Peter Bailis, and Matei Zaharia. 2020. “Jointly Optimizing Preprocessing and Inference for DNN-Based Visual Analytics.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 14 (2): 87–100. https://doi.org/10.14778/3425879.3425881.

The worker count \(\DataLoaderWorkerCount\) is tuned as on a single node, by profiling the data stall ratio, since too few workers starve the GPU and too many waste CPU on contention. At fleet scale, every core given to loading is a core the training runtime, communication threads, and operating system cannot use, so dividing host CPU between data loading and training orchestration is itself a capacity planning exercise. How those workers exploit concurrency then depends on which tier serves their reads. On local NVMe, workers issue concurrent reads to the same RAID array and rely on NVMe’s deep command queues to expose device parallelism until shared drive or controller capacity saturates. On a parallel file system, they distribute reads across different OSS nodes to aggregate server bandwidth. On object storage, they issue concurrent HTTP GET requests to distinct shards.

Multi-worker loading must also preserve shuffle quality without giving up I/O efficiency. Under the shard-level and within-shard shuffling of section 4.2, the loader assigns contiguous groups of the shuffled shard list to each worker, and the adequacy of that mixing depends on how the shards were built. A corpus whose shards are internally homogeneous, such as one source or one class per shard, needs finer shards or a larger shuffle buffer, so the layout should be validated against the loss curve of a fully shuffled baseline on a subset before the run commits to it.

The shuffle buffer size creates a memory-randomness trade-off. A larger buffer provides better randomness (because samples are drawn from a larger pool) but consumes more host DRAM. For the running example with text data, a shuffle buffer of 10,000 sequences at 2,048 tokens each, with each token represented as a 4-byte integer, consumes roughly 82 MB. This is negligible relative to the 512 GB of host DRAM. For image data, where each decoded image occupies 151 KB, a shuffle buffer of 10,000 images consumes 1.5 GB, still manageable but a nontrivial fraction of the prefetch budget. The data loader designer must balance shuffle buffer size against prefetch depth, since both compete for the same host DRAM capacity.

The data loading pipeline, from storage read through decode, augmentation, and transfer to accelerator, is the complete fuel delivery system for the training engine, and when any stage becomes the bottleneck the accelerator starves. Local reads can reduce network and shared-storage wait, which makes shard placement across nodes the next decision when that path limits delivery.

4.4.3 Data locality and placement

Even when a node has enough CPU workers to decode batches at full speed, fetching every shard across a shared storage network exposes the training loop to fabric congestion and remote service queues. Placing shards on node-local NVMe avoids that shared-network contention, whereas keeping them in a remote parallel file system or object store preserves a single shared pool that any node can read without copying data ahead of time. Because local NVMe’s advantage depends on both the node’s drive stripe and the per-client bandwidth the remote tier can sustain, placement decisions must compare measured client rates rather than assume a fixed local-to-remote ratio.

The staging decision determines where the job pays for data movement, whether before training starts, during the first epoch, or on every epoch. The simplest strategy is static placement, or prestaging. At the beginning of a training job, the orchestrator assigns a fixed subset of data shards to each node, and the node copies its shards from shared storage (the object store in the running example, a parallel file system in many on-premises clusters) to local NVMe drives before the first iteration. Reads remain local while the assigned shards fit and the sampling policy uses that assignment, which gives the highest steady-state throughput at the cost of the longest startup delay. Staging a 10 TB dataset across 256 nodes at 500 MB/s per node takes roughly 80 seconds under ideal conditions, and contention on shared bandwidth can extend that stall to several minutes. A changed dataset or assignment requires restaging, so the placement policy must preserve the intended sample distribution as well as local throughput.

The alternative is dynamic placement, or on-demand staging, where nodes fetch shards from shared storage when the data loader first accesses them and retain them locally for reuse. This removes the upfront delay and supports datasets larger than aggregate local NVMe capacity and sampling policies that change the active subset over time, but the first epoch pays the full network-read cost. Cache misses consume shared-storage bandwidth and expose remote latency unless prefetching hides it; cache hits retain the benefit of local reads, so the pattern pays off when later epochs reread the cached shards.

Streaming keeps data off local storage entirely, reading records from the parallel file system or object store on every epoch. It has no startup delay and needs no local NVMe capacity, but every epoch absorbs the network-read cost again. On transfer cost alone, streaming suits single-epoch pretraining, which reads each byte once, and datasets that exceed the cluster’s aggregate local capacity. The single-epoch running example stages anyway, for reasons of latency tails rather than transfer fees, as section 4.8 shows.

The cache view of equation 1 puts a number on that trade. Take the multi-modal job of section 4.3.4, whose 35 TB corpus overflows a node’s 30.7 TB of local NVMe. If the job samples uniformly and the cache holds that fraction of the corpus, about 88 percent of reads hit the 28 GB/s local stripe and the remaining 12 percent fall through to the node’s 4 GB/s share of the parallel file system. Taking the time to deliver one byte as the service time, a serial loader averages the time per byte across hits and misses rather than averaging the two bandwidths directly, so the harmonic mean delivered rate is about 16 GB/s, only 58 percent of the all-local rate. A miss rate near one in eight costs about two-fifths of the bandwidth because each missed byte takes 7× as long as a hit, and that mean is the optimistic case; misses that cluster in time drain the prefetch buffer faster than the average suggests.

A more advanced approach is locality-aware scheduling. The scheduler keeps track of which data shards are cached on which nodes’ local NVMe drives. When it starts a new job or replaces a failed node, it prefers a node that already has the required data cached, rather than treating all nodes as interchangeable. This uses the principle of data gravity,11 co-locating compute with data to minimize transfer times. Scheduling avoids a staging copy only when an available node holds the required, validated shard versions. The next requirement is therefore to identify the cached contents precisely enough to reuse them safely.

11 Data gravity: A metaphor treating large datasets as massive objects that attract compute. The gravitational analogy is apt: moving a 100 TB dataset out of one cloud provider to another costs roughly $9,000 in egress fees and takes hours, while launching compute next to the data is near-instantaneous. In ML fleet design, data gravity means the storage location of the training dataset often dictates the physical placement of the entire training cluster.

For the 6 TB serialized dataset distributed across 256 nodes, each node is responsible for approximately 23.4 GB of data. If a node fails, its replacement must restage that shard subset from shared storage. Drawn at the uncontended 4 GB/s per-node share of the chapter’s parallel file system, the copy takes about 5.9 seconds. At an effective 400 MB/s per-node rate, which recovery contention can impose and which is also the order of the per-node share of the 50–100 GB/s object-store streaming path described in section 4.3.7, the same copy stretches to about 58.6 seconds. If the orchestrator can instead schedule the replacement workload on an idle node that already has the data cached from a previous run, staging time is zero and training resumes as soon as the replacement joins. The saving is modest per event but recurs. At a per-GPU mean time between failures of 50,000 hours, the 256-node cluster sees about 1 node failure per day, and each contended restage idles the whole synchronous job for the copy time above. Larger fleets fail proportionally more often, although each node’s share of the corpus shrinks as the node count grows.

The pipeline equation, the prefetch queue, the loader workers, and shard placement together determine which bytes reach host memory and when. On the conventional path, however, every one of those bytes is also copied by the CPU through host DRAM before the accelerator’s DMA can start, and at high request rates that copy competes with decoding for the same cores. No amount of orchestration removes that copy; only a direct data path between storage and accelerator does.

Self-Check: Question

  1. The data pipeline throughput equation is \(\RequiredInputBandwidth = \InputConsumerAcceleratorCount \times \TargetComputeTimeFraction \times \frac{\BatchDataVolume}{\IronLawComputeTime}\). If a cluster doubles its number of GPUs consuming distinct local batches from 1,024 to 2,048 while keeping per-GPU batch size, uninterrupted compute time per batch, and target active-time fraction constant, how does the required aggregate storage bandwidth scale?

    1. It remains constant because data-parallel workers divide the fixed total dataset into smaller partitions.
    2. It quadruples (\(4\times\)) because collective communication overhead scales quadratically with worker count.
    3. It decreases by \(50\%\) because each node requires fewer samples per epoch.
    4. It doubles (\(2\times\)) because aggregate byte consumption scales strictly linearly with the number of accelerators independently executing forward-backward iterations.

  2. A training iteration has a GPU compute time of \(T_{\text{compute}} = 200\text{ ms}\) and an average I/O fetch time of \(T_{\text{I/O}} = 250\text{ ms}\) per batch. Explain why enabling double-buffering (pipelining batch \(i+1\) during batch \(i\) compute) reduces but cannot eliminate the data stall, and quantify the remaining stall time per step.

  3. A data loader pipeline has an accelerator compute window of \(T_{\text{compute}} = 200\text{ ms}\) per batch. The storage tier exhibits median I/O latency of \(180\text{ ms}\) but experiences P99 tail-latency spikes of \(500\text{ ms}\) due to network jitter. What is the minimum recommended prefetch buffer depth (\(Q_{\text{prefetch}}\)) suggested by the P99 sizing rule, before allowing for longer or correlated stalls?

    1. At least 3 batches (\(Q_{\text{prefetch}} = \lceil 500 / 200 \rceil = 3\)) plus a safety margin, covering the stated P99 delay when the queue is full.
    2. Exactly 1 batch, because median I/O latency (\(180\text{ ms}\)) is strictly less than compute time (\(200\text{ ms}\)).
    3. Zero batches, because multi-worker asynchronous threading eliminates the need for host-memory queue buffering.
    4. At least 10 batches, because queue depth must equal the total number of CPU worker threads allocated to the data loader.

  4. True or False: If a cluster monitoring dashboard indicates that average storage read bandwidth matches the aggregate throughput predicted by the pipeline equation, storage performance issues can be conclusively ruled out as the cause of low GPU utilization.

  5. Explain how locality-aware scheduling reduces cluster startup latency and relieves bandwidth pressure on the shared parallel file system when a node fails during a 1,024-GPU training run.

  6. Compare the three primary data staging patterns (prestaging, on-demand staging, and pure streaming) in terms of job startup latency and network bandwidth consumption during multi-epoch training.

4.5 GPUDirect Storage and the CPU Data-Copy Bypass

Even when the storage tier has enough raw bandwidth, the traditional data path for loading training data routes every shard read through three hops: storage \(\to\) host DRAM \(\to\) GPU HBM. Data is first read from NVMe into a kernel buffer in host DRAM by a DMA transfer, then copied by the CPU into the data loader’s user-space buffer, and finally transferred over PCIe to GPU memory by the GPU’s DMA engine. Each hop adds latency and consumes host resources because the CPU must orchestrate every transfer, manage buffer allocation, and handle interrupts from both the storage device and the GPU. For a single transfer, this overhead is negligible, but when eight GPUs on a node each demand thousands of small transfers per second, the aggregate CPU load for payload movement alone can saturate multiple cores and starve the data augmentation stage. GPUDirect Storage (GDS),12 which removes that host-memory bounce copy for local or RDMA-attached NVMe while the CPU still submits and coordinates I/O through the software stack, pays off whenever that host copy limits throughput.

12 GDS (GPUDirect Storage): Part of NVIDIA’s GPUDirect family, which also includes GPUDirect RDMA (network adapters writing directly to GPU memory) and GPUDirect Peer-to-Peer (inter-GPU memory access); GDS extends this data-copy-bypass approach to NVMe storage. With RDMA and GDS, payload data can traverse the path remote storage \(\to\) network \(\to\) GPU memory without a CPU-mediated bounce copy, while the CPU still issues and coordinates the operations.

Definition 4.4: GPUDirect Storage

GPUDirect storage (GDS) is a technology that enables a direct DMA path between NVMe storage devices and GPU memory, bypassing the system-DRAM bounce buffer and CPU payload copies while retaining CPU submission and control.

  1. Significance: It eliminates the “bounce buffer” through system memory. In the small-transfer model detailed in notebook 4.6, the assumed active CPU service per request falls by 75 percent. That model estimates CPU capacity savings, not an end-to-end latency guarantee. Whether the storage link saturates depends on the remaining bottlenecks.
  2. Distinction: Unlike traditional I/O, where payload data is staged through host buffers and copied between them, GDS provides direct memory access between the storage controller and the accelerator. CPU-issued I/O calls and kernel storage drivers remain in the control path.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that GDS makes all storage “faster.” The direct path depends on supported storage and interconnect configurations; it does not eliminate media latency, network latency, or queueing.

The software layers in the traditional I/O path contribute control overhead as well as payload-copy work. Direct I/O and pinned buffers can avoid some copies even without GDS. The correct baseline is therefore the actual data path rather than a fixed number of copies for every read. The direct path changes the payload route without eliminating the software control path. The application registers a GPU memory region for direct DMA (through the cuFile API in NVIDIA’s implementation), and later reads move payload from NVMe into GPU memory without an intermediate system-memory buffer. The file system and kernel storage drivers still handle submission and completion.

The benefit from GDS matters when host payload movement is a limiting stage after other pipeline work is accounted for. In figure 6, the conventional path takes two DMA hops through intermediate host-DRAM bounce buffers, while GDS takes a single direct DMA transfer. The direct path eliminates the CPU payload-copy stage, but the dashed control link shows that the host CPU still orchestrates transfer submission and completion.

GDS benefits depend on the bottleneck, request sizes, alignment, concurrency, and storage topology. Removing host bounce buffers can improve throughput when those copies limit the path; it does not imply that the smallest transfers benefit most. Small I/O may remain submission- or latency-limited and require concurrency to approach the available bandwidth. Benchmarks of the direct path therefore measure I/O size and per-device behavior as well as throughput. The benefit appears only in an end-to-end comparison of the pipeline with and without the direct path, and the clearest case is the one where host CPU time per request is the limiting stage.

Napkin Math 4.6: The CPU bypass dividend
Problem: A training node with 8 GPUs loads 116 KB images at 8,000 images/s per GPU (64,000 images/s total). That rate is deliberately aggressive, several times the per-GPU image rate of notebook 4.1, to isolate the case in which per-request CPU work is the limiting stage. Assume active CPU service of 120 μs per request on the buffered path and 30 μs on the direct path. These illustrative service times exclude time blocked on I/O; compare the implied CPU load rather than treating elapsed transfer latency as CPU time.

Traditional path: In the assumed buffered implementation, each image requires a DMA from NVMe to DRAM, a host copy from kernel to user space, and a PCIe transfer to GPU. At 64,000 images/s with 120 μs of CPU time per image, the CPU spends 7.68 seconds of CPU time per wall-clock second, consuming roughly 8 cores worth of processing just for data movement.

GDS path: Each image is transferred directly via DMA from NVMe to GPU. At 30 μs of CPU time per image (for initiating the DMA), the CPU spends 1.92 seconds of CPU time per wall-clock second, freeing roughly 6 cores for data augmentation.

Systems insight: Under the assumed CPU-service costs, bypassing payload copies releases processing capacity for preprocessing. The calculation does not predict end-to-end transfer latency, nor prove that preprocessing or storage throughput can sustain this request rate.

GDS has practical limitations: not all NVMe controllers support peer-to-peer PCIe transfers, and the data must already be in a format that the GPU can consume directly (raw pixel values, token IDs, or formats handled by GPU decoders, rather than arbitrary compressed JPEG or gzip streams). In practice, many pipelines use a hybrid approach: CPU-side decoding for compressed formats followed by a normal pinned-memory GPU transfer, while GDS is reserved for direct reads of GPU-consumable data from supported local or remote storage.

The architectural evolution from CPU-mediated copies to direct storage access reflects a broader trend of removing CPU payload copies from the critical data path wherever possible. RDMA allows network payloads to move without CPU copying, while GDS provides the same bypass for storage payloads. Together, RDMA and GDS let data flow from remote storage (such as NVMe over Fabrics, or NVMe-oF), through the network fabric, and into GPU memory without a host-memory bounce copy. The CPU still submits and coordinates the operation, while more of its capacity remains available for data augmentation, pipeline orchestration, and error handling.

↩︎ RDMA and kernel-bypass network paths are established in RDMA and GPUDirect.

The GDS design principle also extends to checkpoint writes: whereas a host-staged path transfers checkpoint bytes from GPU HBM through host DRAM to NVMe, a supported direct path can bypass that host staging for a 2,450 GB checkpoint. Removing the copy reduces \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) only when that movement contributes to the exposed pause; snapshot consistency, serialization, storage service, and durable publication remain separate requirements.

4.5.1 The complete data path

Combining GDS with the multi-tier hierarchy, the complete data path connects the tiers for the running example. At the beginning of the training job, the 6 TB serialized shard set, derived from the 3 TB compressed source corpus, is staged from object storage into the cluster’s local NVMe caches, with each node receiving its assigned shard subset during setup. The data loader workers read tokenized shards from NVMe, assemble packed sequences in pinned host DRAM, and transfer those batches to the accelerator. With GDS enabled, token IDs that are already in a GPU-consumable layout can be loaded directly into GPU memory without CPU payload copying; compressed formats still require CPU or GPU decompression before use.

During steady-state training on the host-staged path, CPU data loader workers read from local NVMe and fill a prefetch queue in host DRAM. The GPU receives batches from this queue via PCIe DMA; the supported GDS alternative bypasses that host payload staging. The compute phase (forward pass, backward pass, optimizer step) consumes the batch and updates model state in HBM. Every 10 minutes, the training framework captures a consistent checkpoint, writes it to local NVMe through the chosen path, and copies it to the parallel file system in the background. The checkpoint protocol must still establish when all required shards are safe to publish for recovery.

At the end of the training job, the final model checkpoint is promoted from the parallel file system to object storage for long-term retention. The local NVMe copies are deleted during job cleanup. If the model is deployed for inference, it is loaded from object storage into the serving cluster’s HBM, completing the lifecycle of data movement through the hierarchy.

The total number of data copies in this lifecycle is instructive. Each training sample traverses: object storage \(\to\) NVMe (staging), NVMe \(\to\) host DRAM (read), host DRAM \(\to\) GPU HBM (transfer). Each checkpoint traverses: GPU HBM \(\to\) NVMe (local save), NVMe \(\to\) parallel file system (async copy), parallel file system \(\to\) object storage (long-term retention). Every copy consumes bandwidth and contributes latency. The engineering goal is to minimize copies on the critical path (the training loop) and tolerate additional copies on noncritical paths (staging and archival). Both lifecycles cross the same tiers in opposite directions, with synchronous critical-path copies drawn solid and asynchronous background transfers drawn dashed in figure 7. For training inputs moving rightward, the active loop waits only on local promotion into HBM (or the optional GDS bypass); for checkpoints flowing leftward, the exposed pause is confined to the local NVMe save while parallel-file-system replication and archival retention proceed off the critical path.

Every copy that leaves the node also needs a network, and the choice of network decides whom a storage burst can disturb. Many training nodes carry a small number of storage-facing network adapters on a separate front-end network, in addition to one compute-fabric adapter per accelerator. Routing staging reads and checkpoint copies over that front-end network isolates them from gradient collectives, but it caps each node’s storage bandwidth at a few front-end ports. Routing storage over the compute fabric offers far more bandwidth, and remote GDS reads use exactly that RDMA path, but then a checkpoint burst shares switch buffers and spine links with the job’s collectives and must be separated by traffic classes. For the running example, each node’s fair share of a 1 TB/s file system shared by 256 nodes is roughly 4 GB/s, and copying its roughly 10 GB checkpoint shard within the 10-minute interval needs far less, so a front-end network can carry it. Image workloads that stream hundreds of GB/s across the cluster push designs toward the compute fabric. Through the C\(^3\) lens, storage traffic becomes communication (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_2\)) load the moment it leaves the node, and the network chosen to carry it decides which collectives a storage burst can delay.

Over the 30-day run, checkpointing writes roughly 10.6 PB of state against a single read of the 6 TB serialized dataset, so checkpoints, not training inputs, are the storage workload to design next. That design must take a multi-terabyte save off the training critical path without ever exposing a checkpoint that cannot be restored.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Which statement precisely describes the architectural data-path modification introduced by GPUDirect Storage (GDS)?

    1. GDS caches the entire training corpus in GPU HBM to eliminate all storage I/O operations during training epochs.
    2. GDS compresses training tensors directly inside the NVMe flash controller using hardware acceleration.
    3. GDS routes network packets through host DRAM bounce buffers to perform real-time data integrity checksumming.
    4. GDS establishes a direct DMA transfer path between NVMe storage (local or NVMe-oF) and GPU memory, eliminating the CPU-mediated bounce buffer in host DRAM while retaining CPU control-plane coordination.

  2. A computer vision training pipeline uses complex CPU-based JPEG decompression and multi-step data augmentations (color jitter, affine warping). Explain why adopting GPUDirect Storage (GDS) without moving decompression to the GPU yields minimal end-to-end throughput improvement.

  3. True or False: Enabling GPUDirect Storage (GDS) eliminates the physical round-trip network latency and P99 tail-latency spikes associated with fetching shards directly from remote cloud object storage endpoints.

  4. In a high-throughput vision training node with 8 GPUs processing a combined 64,000 images per second, traditional CPU-mediated I/O requires \(120\ \mu\text{s}\) of CPU time per image (consuming \(\approx 7.68\) CPU seconds per wall-clock second, or \(\approx 8\) dedicated CPU cores). GDS reduces CPU overhead to \(30\ \mu\text{s}\) per image (\(1.92\) CPU seconds per second, or \(\approx 2\) cores). What is the primary systems benefit of this ‘CPU bypass dividend’?

    1. It enables the cluster to eliminate all local NVMe SSDs in favor of remote tape archives.
    2. It frees approximately 6 CPU cores per node that can be reallocated to computationally intensive data augmentation and pipeline orchestration without adding hardware.
    3. It reduces GPU power consumption by \(75\%\) during tensor matrix multiplication.
    4. It doubles the physical PCIe bus bandwidth between the CPU socket and the GPU accelerators.

  5. Order the sequence of operations that occurs in the traditional (non-GDS) storage I/O path when an accelerator requests a batch from local NVMe: (1) The data loader process issues a POSIX read() system call traversing the VFS and filesystem drivers, (2) The NVMe controller initiates a DMA transfer of raw blocks into an OS kernel buffer in host DRAM, (3) The host CPU copies payload bytes from kernel space to user-space pinned memory, (4) The CPU programs a PCIe DMA transfer from host DRAM to GPU HBM, (5) The accelerator launches compute kernels on the newly arrived tensor resident in HBM.
