4.6 Checkpoint Storage

After the 2,048 accelerators of the running cluster have been training for six hours, a power supply failure turns checkpoint storage into the difference between resuming within minutes and repeating hours or days of lost computation. Whether the job recovers cleanly depends on three storage questions: how quickly the most recent checkpoint was saved, where its shards reside, and whether they can be read back intact. Because checkpoints are also the heaviest write workload in the hierarchy, the storage architecture must minimize \(\CheckpointWriteTime\), the time the training pipeline pauses to save a snapshot, while stating when that snapshot becomes durable and visible for restore. The save interval is an input to this storage analysis; selecting it from failure rates and recovery costs is a separate reliability decision derived in The Young-Daly checkpoint interval.

Under the resumable mixed-precision Adam contract of section 4.1, the 175B parameter model’s 2,450 GB checkpoint comprises 350 GB of FP16 model weights, 700 GB of FP32 master weights, and 1,400 GB for the two FP32 Adam moments, while learning-rate-scheduler state, random-number-generator state, and the data-loader cursor add negligible bytes and gradients are recomputed after restore. When a save triggers, every participating worker process, called a rank, writes its assigned shard at once, producing the checkpoint storm introduced in section 4.2. Keeping that burst tractable requires a coordinated save that writes each unique byte once, whatever the parallel layout. In the running layout of table 3, the Adam moments and master weights already exist as distinct per-node slices, while the FP16 weights repeat once per node, so a coordinated save assigns each of the 256 nodes a disjoint 1/256 slice of its local FP16 weight copy to write alongside its unique optimizer slice. Spreading those unique bytes evenly gives each of the 256 nodes about 1/256 of the checkpoint. The third ZeRO stage (ZeRO-3), which partitions the weights as well, makes that even split the natural one, because every rank already owns a distinct slice. Breaking that rule is a real risk even in the running layout, where a save in which every node writes the FP16 weight copy it holds would multiply the weight bytes by the node count. Its cost is clearest in a deliberately extreme thought experiment, a smaller model whose complete resumable state is written once per GPU of the book’s reference cluster.

Writing one copy per replica takes 1,024 times longer than writing each unique byte once.

Definition 4.5: Checkpoint storm

Checkpoint storm is a burst of synchronized network and storage traffic that occurs when all nodes in an ML training fleet save model state simultaneously.

  1. Significance: When each replica writes its own full copy, the naive storm takes \(\CheckpointWriteTime = \DataParallelDegree \times \text{per-replica state} / \StorageIngestBandwidth\), where \(\DataParallelDegree\) is the number of replicas writing at once and \(\StorageIngestBandwidth\) is the aggregate rate at which the target storage tier absorbs writes. A coordinated save that writes each unique byte once takes \(\text{unique state} / \StorageIngestBandwidth\). Under the chapter’s checkpoint contract, a 70B-parameter model, smaller than the 175B running example, stores 140 GB of FP16 weights, 280 GB of FP32 master weights, and 560 GB of Adam moments. With vanilla data parallelism across 1,024 replicas (one per GPU of the book’s reference cluster, rather than the chapter’s 256-node fleet), naively writing that complete state from every replica generates 1003.5 TB of simultaneous traffic. No 80 GB device can hold a 0.98 TB replica, so the case is a bound on write volume rather than a deployable layout. At 100 GB/s of aggregate storage ingest bandwidth, the write takes roughly 10,035.2 seconds, or over 167.3 minutes of training stall. A coordinated checkpoint protocol writes one logical copy. Replicated data parallelism can select one writer; ZeRO-3 instead distributes that copy across state-owning ranks. In the sharded example the total is roughly 0.98 TB, bringing \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) down to roughly 9.8 seconds. Gradients do not appear in either total because recovery recomputes them.
  2. Distinction: Unlike general I/O contention (which is stochastic and unpredictable), a checkpoint storm is synchronous and periodic: every node writes at the same moment because the training orchestrator triggers checkpoint after a fixed step count. Its predictability makes it both more damaging (all nodes compete simultaneously) and more tractable (it can be prevented by design through staggered scheduling or asynchronous serialization).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that checkpointing every 100 steps is “low overhead” whatever the save protocol. Under the naive protocol at 70B scale, if each training step takes 10 seconds and a checkpoint storm takes 10,035.2 seconds, checkpointing every 100 steps means spending most of the run on checkpoint I/O rather than useful training.

The severity of a checkpoint storm depends on two factors: the per-node shard size, determined by the model size and the sharding strategy, and the throughput of the first write tier, typically local NVMe. A concrete 175B example shows why the local-write phase, rather than the durable copy to the parallel file system, governs the exposed training pause.

Napkin Math 4.7: Tiered checkpoint staging
Problem: Assume sustained bandwidth, no snapshot-copy, serialization, or coordination overhead, and no interference from background copies. The resulting times are bandwidth lower bounds. A 256-node cluster saves a 175B-parameter checkpoint every 10 minutes. Each checkpoint totals 2,450 GB. Writing each unique byte once and spreading the bytes evenly across nodes, each node saves roughly 9.6 GB.

  1. Per-node write to local NVMe (4 drives at 7 GB/s each = 28 GB/s): 9.6 GB \(\div\) 28 GB/s \(\approx\) 0.34 seconds.
  2. Async copy to PFS: 256 \(\times\) 9.6 GB \(\approx\) 2.4 TB total. If the PFS provides 1 TB/s aggregate, the storm completes in roughly 2.4 seconds.
  3. Per-node PFS bandwidth: If all 256 write simultaneously, each gets 1000 GB/s \(\div\) 256 = 3.9 GB/s.
  4. Training pause: At least 0.34 seconds for the local NVMe write under this model. The PFS copy overlaps with the next training iteration.
  5. Overhead lower bound: 0.34 seconds pause every 600 seconds \(\approx\) 0.06 percent training time lost to checkpointing.

Systems insight: Under these 1 TB/s aggregate-PFS assumptions, tiered staging reduces checkpoint overhead from about a 2.4 seconds PFS-direct write to a sub-second local write. The training pause can end at the local snapshot boundary before durable publication, but actual pause includes snapshot creation, serialization, and coordination in addition to this byte-transfer lower bound.

A PFS-direct save of a few seconds every 10 minutes costs well under 1 percent of the run, which can make tiered staging look unnecessary for the running example. The checkpoint-dominance argument of section 4.5.1 concerns bytes moved, not this pause, and the notebook’s figure holds only while its assumptions do. The job has the whole 1 TB/s file system to itself; shared among the ten concurrent jobs of section 4.3.6, it receives a tenth of the bandwidth, the same save takes ten times as long, and its burst slows the other jobs’ reads. Realized ingest also falls below the rated aggregate once serialization, metadata operations, and stragglers enter. The save interval shrinks as the fleet grows, because a larger fleet fails more often and the reliability analysis of The Young-Daly checkpoint interval then calls for more frequent saves, and a larger model scales every save with it. Tiered staging removes the shared-tier write from the exposed pause when the background copy keeps pace, although local snapshot creation, serialization, writing, and coordination still determine that pause.

Tiered staging insulates the training loop from that shared-tier contention by splitting the save into two phases. In the first phase, as calculated in notebook 4.7, each of the 256 nodes writes its 9.6 GB share of the 2,450 GB checkpoint across 4 local NVMe drives at 28 GB/s, establishing a byte-transfer lower bound on \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) of roughly 0.34 seconds before snapshot-copy, serialization, and coordination overhead. Once every required local shard has completed and the captured state is no longer being modified, the training pipeline can resume.

In the second phase, a background process asynchronously copies the local checkpoint to the parallel file system for durability. The copy can overlap training, but it still competes for memory, network, and storage service and must drain before repeated saves create an unbounded backlog. The two phases therefore land differently in the Fleet Law (equation 1.3). The local write is a pause that every rank takes at the same step boundary, and training resumes only when the slowest writer has finished its shard. That is the shape of a barrier rather than of a transfer between workers, a coordination (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_3\)) cost, so the pause adds to the synchronization term \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\) of each saving step, the term that already carries barrier and straggler waiting. The background copy belongs to no step’s critical path. While it stays hidden and drains before the next save, it adds nothing to the step time; it reappears only as interference, in \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) when it shares links with collectives or in exposed input wait when it slows shared-storage reads. If a node fails before its copy completes, the new generation may lose a required shard. Replication to peer nodes’ NVMe drives can protect against a single-node loss, provided replicas occupy independent failure domains. Waiting for that replication extends the pause beyond the local-only calculation. A design that resumes earlier must retain the previous committed generation until the new one reaches its declared durability boundary.

Durability alone does not make a distributed checkpoint restorable. Each rank writes its checksummed shard under an immutable generation identifier. Only after every shard has persisted does a coordinator publish a manifest naming the complete shard set. That commit uses an atomic primitive of the target tier (a create or rename on a file system, a conditional create or versioned pointer on an object store). A crash can then leave garbage to collect but never an incomplete generation that appears valid.

↪ The commit rule behind this ordering, a two-phase commit over the shard set, and the consistency of the cut that a manifest names are developed in Checkpoint coordination patterns.

The exposed pause and the durability boundary therefore fall at different moments, as the four concurrent lanes of figure 8 align them. Training pauses only during the local NVMe shard write (\(\TimeInstant_0\) to \(\TimeInstant_1\)), resuming on live state while the parallel file system absorbs the asynchronous copy (\(\TimeInstant_1\) to \(\TimeInstant_2\)) and the coordinator validates checksums before publishing the manifest last (\(\TimeInstant_3\)).

4.6.1 Synchronous and asynchronous checkpoint writes

In the two-phase timeline of figure 8, the first phase is still synchronous with respect to local flash: across 256 nodes, every node must write and acknowledge its shard of the same completed training step before the completion barrier releases the next iteration, with the commit rules that make that cut consistent developed in Checkpoint coordination patterns. The slowest local writer therefore sets the exposed pause from \(\TimeInstant_0\) to \(\TimeInstant_1\).

Asynchronous checkpointing shrinks even that local-flash pause by staging the snapshot in host DRAM first. Nodes coordinate the training step to capture, copy each accelerator-resident shard into a dedicated pinned host-memory buffer, and resume training immediately while a background thread writes the buffer to local NVMe. The snapshot buffer must remain immutable until the background write finishes; separate live-state buffers allow training to advance without changing the captured generation. If a node fails during the asynchronous write, its local shard may be incomplete. The job must wait for the required writes or peer replication before declaring this generation durable, even if training has already resumed.

The choice between synchronous and asynchronous checkpointing depends on the pause budget and save frequency. A 1-second pause at 200 ms per iteration costs five iterations; whether that overhead is acceptable depends on how much useful training occurs between saves. Asynchronous checkpointing reduces exposed write time when snapshot buffers and background bandwidth are available, but its buffer capacity, contention, and durability delay also enter the decision.

Checkpoint formats implement the write-once rule of the checkpoint storm as rank-sharded saves, as in DeepSpeed’s zero-redundancy checkpointing and PyTorch’s Distributed Checkpoint (PyTorch Contributors 2026). The sharding is what keeps the pause short. Had one node saved the full 2,450 GB itself, its 28 GB/s local stripe would need more than a minute rather than the 0.34 seconds of an even shard. Making each individual save fast, however, creates a cumulative capacity problem once thousands of snapshots accumulate over a multi-week run.

PyTorch Contributors. 2026. Distributed Checkpoint: Torch.distributed.checkpoint.

4.6.2 Checkpoint retention and restore

Checkpointing a 175B-parameter model every 10 minutes over a 30-day training run generates roughly 4,320 snapshots of 2,450 GB each, totaling approximately 10.6 PB, enough to fill a typical parallel file system several times over if retained in full. The retention policy this run adopts keeps the three most recent checkpoints on the parallel file system for fast recovery, copies every 100th checkpoint and the final checkpoint to object storage for long-term auditability, and deletes the rest. This policy reduces the parallel file system checkpoint footprint from 10.6 PB to roughly 7.35 TB (three live full checkpoints) while preserving 43 periodic historical snapshots in object storage, plus the final checkpoint when it does not coincide with that schedule, for posttraining analysis.

Incremental checkpointing offers a further optimization for reducing \(\CheckpointWriteTime\). Rather than saving the entire training state every time, an incremental checkpoint records changed state relative to a declared base or preceding generation. The delta must include changed optimizer and continuation state as well as parameters. For models where a large fraction of parameters are frozen (such as fine-tuning scenarios where only the last few layers are updated), incremental checkpoints can be orders of magnitude smaller than full checkpoints. Restoring a generation requires its base and every intervening delta, so smaller saves add recovery work and dependencies. Periodic full checkpoints bound that chain, and the retention policy must preserve each dependency until no retained generation requires it. A shorter pause matters only if the checkpoint can be read back; the costlier failure is a checkpoint that was written but never verified as restorable.

War Story 4.1: The backup that had not been restored (2017)
Context: On January 31, 2017, a GitLab.com site reliability engineer working a database replication incident accidentally wiped the primary PostgreSQL data directory (GitLab 2017).

Mechanism: All five layered backup paths failed simultaneously: S3 dumps were silently empty due to a version mismatch, Azure disk snapshots were disabled, replication was broken, LVM snapshots were six hours stale, and WAL archiving was unimplemented.

Impact: Six hours of production database writes, user comments, and repository metadata were permanently lost, requiring an 18-hour emergency restoration from a staging snapshot.

Fix: GitLab instituted automated daily backup restoration testing, unified PostgreSQL versioning across environments, and implemented automated WAL archiving to S3.

Systems lesson: Storage reliability is a restore property, not a backup property. A checkpoint, replica, or dump only counts if the team regularly proves that it can be located, validated, and restored under incident conditions. ML systems face the same vulnerability. A model checkpoint written to object storage or a feature store snapshot is useless if silent version mismatches or missing restore tests prevent resuming a multi-day training run after node preemption.

GitLab. 2017. Postmortem of Database Outage of January 31. GitLab postmortem.

That incident turns checkpoint storage from a capacity question into a restore-design question. Before consolidating the running example’s full storage footprint, the following check tests the write rates and staging trade-offs that govern each save.

Checkpoint 4.4: Checkpoint storage design

A training cluster of 256 nodes saves a 175B-parameter model checkpoint every 10 minutes. Each checkpoint is 2,450 GB total, distributed across all nodes.

With the checkpoint accounting now derived in full, the running example’s storage demands can be consolidated against the same hierarchy. Placing the training dataset, live model state, checkpoints, and archive lineage side by side reveals which direction of traffic dominates the 30-day run.

Systems Perspective 4.2: The 175B model's storage footprint
The running example, a 30-day training run of a 175B-parameter model on 256 nodes, places data on every tier of the hierarchy (table 5).

The total data moved through the hierarchy is approximately 10.6 PB of checkpoint data plus 6 TB per 1 epoch of training data reads. For a single-epoch language model training run, checkpoint I/O dominates data loading I/O by a factor of over 1,764.

Checkpoints therefore set the run’s write budget, while the far smaller training corpus carries a different obligation. Because training quality depends on the exact samples delivered at every step, each byte of that corpus must remain traceable to the source version and transformation code that produced it.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A 256-node cluster saves a 175B-parameter resumable checkpoint (\(2.45\text{ TB}\) total: \(350\text{ GB}\) FP16 weights, \(700\text{ GB}\) FP32 master weights, and \(1.4\text{ TB}\) of Adam moments) every 10 minutes. Under tiered staging, each node writes its \(9.57\text{ GB}\) ZeRO-3 shard to local NVMe (\(28\text{ GB/s}\) local array) in \(\approx 0.34\text{ s}\), followed by an asynchronous background copy to a \(1\text{ TB/s}\) parallel file system taking \(\approx 2.45\text{ s}\). What is the exposed training pause time (\(T_{\text{write}}\)) per checkpoint?

    1. Roughly \(2.8\text{ s}\), because training must remain paused until both the local write and the durable PFS copy complete.
    2. Roughly \(0.34\text{ s}\), because the training barrier releases immediately after the fast local NVMe write completes, while the PFS replication proceeds asynchronously in the background.
    3. Zero seconds, because modern storage drivers execute zero-copy writes without interrupting GPU tensor execution.
    4. Roughly \(2.45\text{ s}\), because parallel file system latency determines the minimum barrier synchronization time across nodes.

  2. True or False: In distributed ML infrastructure, storage reliability is defined primarily as a write-completion property: once all nodes successfully execute and acknowledge their checkpoint write calls, the checkpoint can be guaranteed reliable for cluster recovery.

  3. A cluster of 1,024 accelerators trains a 70B-parameter model whose resumable checkpoint contains \(140\text{ GB}\) of FP16 weights, \(280\text{ GB}\) of FP32 master weights, and \(560\text{ GB}\) of Adam moments, or \(980\text{ GB}\) per full replica. Contrast naive full-replica checkpointing with ZeRO-3 sharded checkpointing across a \(100\text{ GB/s}\) shared storage fabric.

  4. A 30-day training run of a 175B model generates 4,320 resumable checkpoints totaling roughly \(10.6\text{ PB}\) of state data. Which retention policy balances rapid failure recovery, long-term auditability, and storage cost without exhausting the parallel file system budget?

    1. Retain all 4,320 checkpoints on the parallel file system to provide granular step-by-step rollback capabilities throughout the entire 30-day run.
    2. Delete all intermediate checkpoints immediately after each step and rely exclusively on live memory replication across peer nodes for fault tolerance.
    3. Retain the 3 most recent checkpoints on the parallel file system for fast local recovery, copy every 100th checkpoint to cloud object storage for long-term lineage, and purge older intermediate snapshots.
    4. Compress all checkpoints with gzip and write them directly to cold tape archive without maintaining any copies on NVMe or parallel file systems.

  5. Order the sequence of steps executed during a tiered asynchronous checkpointing operation: (1) Background daemons asynchronously transfer the local checkpoint shards from NVMe to the shared parallel file system, (2) The training orchestrator initiates a synchronized step barrier across all worker ranks, (3) Each rank snapshots its model weights and optimizer state shard into a local pinned DRAM buffer, (4) The training barrier releases and accelerators immediately resume forward-backward computation for the next step, (5) Each node flushes its pinned memory snapshot to local NVMe at full drive bandwidth.

4.7 Versioned Data and Commit Boundaries

If a multi-week training run suffers a sudden loss spike or a deployed model regresses, an engineering team that did not record which shard versions, preprocessing code, and shuffle seeds fed each step cannot tell a model bug from a silent change in the input data. A checkpoint manifest provides one commit boundary per save. The data pipeline that built the training corpus has many, one at every stage that turns raw inputs into processed shards. The storage system must record both the exact bytes read and the preprocessing code, shuffle seed, schema, and integrity checks that produced them before the run began.

Lighthouse 4.1: Archetype B (DLRM at Scale): Feature store latency
While Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) trains on static, versioned shards, Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) trains on live feature streams that change every second as users click and interact. Replaying an exact batch then becomes a point-in-time problem, which a feature store answers by keeping a retained, versioned feature log.

↪ Point-in-time correctness, the leakage it prevents, and the storage cost of retained feature history are developed in Point-in-time correctness.

13 Hash cost (SHA-256): Verifying the integrity of a 100 TB dataset using SHA-256 consumes approximately 55.6 CPU-core hours at a per-core hashing rate of 500 MB/s. The work parallelizes across shards, but it still competes with decoding for host cores and must read every byte, so the validation step is best pipelined with ingestion rather than run as a separate pass before the fleet starts.

Even when the underlying shard files are immutable, a distributed loader produces a stream of consumed samples whose versions and order can shift across epochs during a 30-day run. Dataset versioning provides a “Git for data” record by snapshotting the cryptographic hashes13 of every data shard, the exact preprocessing code used for tokenization, and the random seeds governing the global shuffle. With that snapshot in place, engineers investigating a mid-run divergence can replay the exact batch that triggered the loss spike and test whether the culprit was a corrupted sample or numerical instability in the optimizer.

At petabyte scale, the data pipeline has to make every transformation reproducible. Raw documents are cleaned, tokenized, packed into training examples, and sharded across storage tiers. Each stage writes an immutable artifact, and each artifact records the input version, code version, schema, and statistical checks it satisfied. Once those dependencies are explicit, the pipeline forms a directed acyclic graph: later artifacts depend on earlier artifacts, and no stage is allowed to rewrite its own history. When a training job starts, the cluster manager verifies that each worker’s local NVMe shards match the data snapshot declared for the run. Treating the data pipeline as a graph of versioned artifacts rather than a simple file transfer makes silent data drift detectable and attributable to a specific stage, code version, or input snapshot. Immutability also pays a storage dividend. Because an artifact never changes once written, a manifest can name it by content identifier, and many experiments can reference the same unchanged shards without copying entire datasets.

4.7.1 Replay and commit boundaries in data pipelines

When a tokenization worker crashes halfway through building a shard of the running example’s corpus, its replacement must decide which raw documents to process again without silently dropping records or writing duplicates. Versioned artifacts identify what a pipeline produced, while commit and replay rules determine whether mid-stage failures can alter that result. The storage system’s part in that decision is the commit boundary, the point at which a stage’s output becomes durable and visible downstream, the same contract that section 4.6 applied to checkpoint generations. That shared contract is why a storage chapter owns these semantics, because whether a replay loses or duplicates data is decided by what the store has committed. The restarted worker compares its input position with the last committed output and replays everything after it, so the work at risk is whatever accumulated past the last commit. Two service-rate effects govern how much accumulates. Records are assigned to workers by a partition key, such as a document’s source or a user identifier, and partition skew arises when one key owns disproportionate work and holds its stage open while the rest of the fleet idles. Backpressure propagates a slow downstream stage’s limit upstream by slowing admission or bounding in-flight data, a control response to overload rather than another name for slow processing.

Failures near that boundary raise a correctness question, and three guarantees answer it differently. At-most-once processing never replays uncertain work, so a document that was read but whose output was never confirmed is lost. At-least-once processing replays everything after the last confirmed commit, so a document whose output was written but not yet confirmed is written twice. An effectively exactly-once outcome combines replayable inputs with idempotent writes, writes that leave the same result however many times they repeat, or with transactional output keyed by a stable event or transaction identifier (Akidau et al. 2015). Reliable message delivery between workers cannot provide this guarantee on its own, because the duplicate arises from the worker’s own replay.

Example 4.2: The acknowledgment gap
Failure trace: A feature-pipeline worker writes the output row for click event e42, then crashes before it records that the write succeeded. Its replacement cannot tell whether e42 was committed. Skipping the event risks losing it. Replaying it with an append writes the feature row a second time, so that click counts twice in every training set built from the table. Writing the row under the stable key (feature_version, e42) turns the replay into an overwrite of the same row, so the uncertain event can be reprocessed safely.

Systems lesson: Exactly-once belongs at the output commit boundary, whose record must bind the input position, transformation version, stable identifier, and commit state.

The two replays differ only in how the output is addressed. An append gives every attempt its own row, while a write under a stable key sends every attempt to the same row, so reprocessing uncertain work leaves the stored result unchanged (figure 9).

Streaming adds a time boundary to the contract. Event time records when an event occurred; processing time records when a worker handled it. A pipeline that aggregates clicks per hour must decide when an hour is finished, even though events can arrive late. A watermark is its running estimate that every event up to some event time has arrived, which lets it close that window and emit the result; a late-data policy determines whether later arrivals update, retract, quarantine, or drop the result (Akidau et al. 2015). Watermarks therefore trade freshness against completeness rather than prove that no older event remains.

Akidau, Tyler, Robert Bradshaw, Craig Chambers, Slava Chernyak, Rafael J. Fernández-Moctezuma, Reuven Lax, Sam McVeety, et al. 2015. “The Dataflow Model: A Practical Approach to Balancing Correctness, Latency, and Cost in Massive-Scale, Unbounded, Out-of-Order Data Processing.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 8 (12): 1792–803. https://doi.org/10.14778/2824032.2824076.

Each of these mechanisms buys one property at the cost of another. Buffering absorbs finite bursts or temporary slowdowns but increases recovery state and freshness lag; sustained overload still requires backpressure or more service capacity. Aggressive watermarks reduce delay but exclude more late data, while retries help only when writes are safe to repeat. These choices change the stored result, because a feature table closed under an aggressive watermark holds different rows from one closed under a lenient watermark. A reproducible training snapshot must therefore record source and code versions alongside partitioning, replay policy, stable identifiers, watermark state, and late-data decisions. That record is what lets a later feature backfill, a replay of history under new code, rejoin features at the correct points in time without mixing old and new histories.

↪ Feature backfills that replay history under this snapshot contract are developed in Backfill procedures.

4.7.2 The synthetic fuel line

The versioned-artifact contract applies to generated training samples as well as collected ones. When a fleet generates candidate samples, filters them, and verifies the survivors, each stage adds storage traffic and records that must remain linked to the accepted payload. The pressure to generate comes from the compute-optimal allocation of AI Scaling Laws, which grows the token budget with the parameter count. As high-quality human-generated text becomes harder to expand, the fleet encounters the data wall: the point where additional training samples must increasingly come from synthesis, simulation, curation, or expensive human collection (Villalobos et al. 2024). This shift from simple collection to managed data generation sends more than payload bytes through the hierarchy. A “synthetic fuel line” must also store the provenance chain of every sample: which model generated it, which filters accepted or rejected it, which judge or verifier models scored it, and which downstream run consumed it. That lineage makes repeated reuse of generated material traceable. It supports investigation of model collapse, where recursive training on generated data can degrade the learned distribution (Shumailov et al. 2024); retaining provenance alone does not prevent the degradation. The storage design must separately budget retained artifacts and the repeated scans used to assess them.

Villalobos, Pablo, Anson Ho, Jaime Sevilla, Tamay Besiroglu, Lennart Heim, and Marius Hobbhahn. 2024. “Position: Will We Run Out of Data? Limits of LLM Scaling Based on Human-Generated Data.” Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 235: 49523–44.
Shumailov, Ilia, Zakhar Shumaylov, Yiren Zhao, Nicolas Papernot, Ross Anderson, and Yarin Gal. 2024. “AI Models Collapse When Trained on Recursively Generated Data.” Nature 631 (8022): 755–59. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-024-07566-y.

Napkin Math 4.8: The storage cost of synthetic data
Problem: A 1 TB synthetic dataset retains provenance metadata and undergoes three independent verification scans. How much persistent capacity and pipeline traffic does it require if judge outputs are not retained as full dataset copies?

  1. Raw Payload: 1 TB.
  2. Provenance Overhead: 40 percent extra for lineage hashes, generation logs, and reward-model scores.
  3. Verification Traffic: 3 judge passes each read the payload, producing 3 TB of read traffic. Their scores reside in the provenance metadata rather than in three retained payload copies.
  4. Capacity Result: Persistent capacity is 1 TB \(\times\) 1.4 = 1.4 TB, a 1.4× capacity factor.
  5. Traffic Result: Writing the payload and metadata once and reading the payload three times moves 4.4 TB, a 4.4× traffic factor before generation and judge-model compute are counted.

Systems insight: Verification primarily draws on service rate and compute, while retained provenance consumes capacity. The two costs coincide only when the pipeline materializes each judge output as another full dataset. Synthetic storage must therefore budget bytes at rest separately from bytes moved and record which generator, filters, judges, and downstream runs produced each accepted sample.

Generated data therefore makes lineage part of the payload, so the store must budget retained provenance and verification traffic alongside the samples themselves. Every tier that holds those samples, checkpoints, or provenance records also carries a distinct price tag, turning placement across the hierarchy into an economic optimization problem.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A feature-pipeline worker writes the output row for click event e42, then crashes before it records that the write succeeded. Its replacement cannot tell whether the row was committed. Which design lets the replacement reprocess e42 without losing the event or counting it twice?

    1. Write each output row under a stable key such as (feature_version, e42), so a replay overwrites the same row instead of appending a second one.
    2. Switch the stage to at-most-once processing, so uncertain events are never replayed.
    3. Deliver messages between workers over a reliable transport, so every message arrives exactly once.
    4. Lengthen the watermark delay, so late events have more time to arrive before the window closes.

  2. A team generates a \(1\text{ TB}\) synthetic text dataset. Retained provenance adds \(40\%\) metadata overhead, and 3 verifier models each read the payload once without materializing another full copy. What persistent capacity and total pipeline traffic are required?

    1. \(1.4\text{ TB}\) of persistent capacity and \(4.4\text{ TB}\) of traffic: \(1\text{ TB}\) payload write, \(0.4\text{ TB}\) metadata write, and \(3\text{ TB}\) of verifier reads.
    2. Exactly \(1.0\text{ TB}\), because verifier models run entirely in GPU registers without generating persistent storage artifacts.
    3. Roughly \(0.4\text{ TB}\), because synthetic data compresses more efficiently than natural language text.
    4. Roughly \(14\text{ TB}\), because every verifier model stores a full \(350\text{ GB}\) copy of its internal neural activations for every sample.

  3. Explain why synthetic training data pipelines must treat storage as an active ‘provenance engine’ by recording the generating model, filters, and verifier scores for each accepted sample, rather than storing plain text files.

  4. The degenerative failure mode where a machine learning model progressively degrades in quality and loses distributional diversity by recursively training on unverified synthetic data produced by earlier models is called model ____.

See Answers →

4.8 Storage Economics

Checkpoints, training shards, and the lineage that binds them now each have a place in the hierarchy, and every placement carries a price set by the tier that holds the bytes and by how often they cross a billing boundary. ML storage economics is therefore not simply about choosing the cheapest capacity tier; the design objective is to minimize total cost of data delivery, which combines recurring storage charges, cross-boundary transfer fees, and the opportunity cost of idle accelerators.

Applied to one 100 TB training dataset, the per-gigabyte rates quoted earlier for each tier diverge by orders of magnitude: object storage (S3 Standard) costs $24,000 per year, provisioned local NVMe costs $120,000 per year, and archival storage (such as Glacier) drops to $4,800 per year. By contrast, hypothetically provisioning the same capacity as GPU memory at $15/GB would require $1,500,000 in upfront acquisition cost alone, before factoring in amortization, power, or server housing.

The 5× cost difference between local NVMe and object storage motivates placing data on the least expensive tier that meets its delivery and recovery requirements. Device technology contributes to the gradient: HBM uses 3D-stacked silicon with through-silicon vias, and accelerator-adjacent capacity is constrained by packaging and node design. Shared storage pools capacity across clients but introduces network and service dependencies. The cost comparison must include those dependencies rather than infer the cheapest usable path from capacity price alone.

Repeated cross-boundary egress, not storage, dominates cloud cost.

Storage cost, however, is only part of the equation. Data transfer costs can dominate, especially in cloud environments. When compute runs in a different region or cloud from the bucket, as assumed here, reading the 100 TB dataset from object storage to compute instances incurs a transfer charge of $9,000. For multi-epoch training that reads the dataset 10 times, the egress cost alone reaches $90,000, more than the annual storage cost. This inversion, where reading data costs more than storing it, drives the architecture decision to cache data on local NVMe rather than streaming from object storage each epoch.

The economic analysis changes further when accounting for the opportunity cost of accelerator idle time. The book’s reference cluster of 1,024 GPUs, half the chapter’s running cluster, at $2/GPU-hour costs $49,152 per day. If input stalls reduce its compute-active fraction from 90 percent to 70 percent, the lost compute time is valued at $9,830 per day. Over a 30 days training run, this amounts to $294,912. That value sets a budget for evaluating a storage upgrade, but the recoverable benefit depends on how much exposed wait the upgrade removes. Capacity that does not relieve the limiting stage yields no corresponding compute-time saving.

Systems Perspective 4.3: The storage cost iceberg
The visible cost of storage ($/GB/month) is the tip of the iceberg. Below the surface lie costs that often exceed the storage cost itself:

  • Egress fees: The ten-epoch egress charge of $90,000 computed above already exceeds a year of storage.
  • IOPS charges: Object storage charges per-request fees. S3 Standard GET requests cost about $0.0004 per 1,000 requests, so a dataset of 100M individual files, read once, costs about $40 in GET request fees before data transfer or retrieval charges, even though the storage cost is $2,000/month.
  • Idle accelerator cost: The $9,830 per day of lost compute computed above dwarfs any storage saving.
  • Retrieval fees: Archive storage charges retrieval fees ($0.02/GB for Glacier) in addition to egress. Restoring a 100 TB dataset from Glacier costs $2,000 in retrieval fees plus $9,000 in egress.

The economically rational strategy often invests more in storage (local NVMe caching, parallel file system capacity) to reduce transfer costs and prevent accelerator stalls.

The cost iceberg reveals that raw storage capacity can account for less than half of the total expenditure; replication, metadata operations, API calls, and cross-region transfers can collectively dominate the bill at scale. Among those hidden line items, egress is the one that often flips the architecture decision. Unlike capacity charges, which scale with the volume of data stored, egress charges scale with the volume of data read, and ML training reads the full dataset on every epoch.

Napkin Math 4.9: The cost of egress
Problem: A team trains a vision model on a 50 TB image dataset stored in S3. Training runs for 20 epochs. The decision is whether to stream from S3 each epoch or stage to local NVMe.

Option A: Stream from S3

  • Storage: 50 TB \(\times\) $0.02/GB/month \(\times\) 12 months = $12,000/year
  • Egress: 50 TB \(\times\) 20 epochs \(\times\) $0.09/GB = $90,000 per training run
  • Total for 4 training runs/year: $12,000 + $360,000 = $372,000/year

Option B: Stage to local NVMe

  • Storage (S3 source of truth): $12,000/year
  • NVMe capacity (50 TB across cluster): 50 TB \(\times\) $0.10/GB/month \(\times\) 12 = $60,000/year
  • Egress (stage once per training run): 50 TB \(\times\) 4 runs \(\times\) $0.09/GB = $18,000/year
  • Total: $12,000 + $60,000 + $18,000 = $90,000/year

Systems insight: Local NVMe caching saves $282,000/year at 20 epochs per run, enough to fund 9 additional H100 GPUs at list price. Including the $60,000/year provisioned NVMe cost and the once-per-run staging egress, the break-even point is just over 4 epochs per run; by the fifth epoch, local caching is cheaper than streaming from S3 every epoch.

The notebook turns the iceberg into a decision rule: the cheapest storage tier is not always the cheapest delivery path. Object storage can remain the source of truth, but repeated reads make egress, request overhead, and idle-accelerator cost part of the storage design. Multi-epoch vision training may justify staging on local NVMe by the fifth epoch, while single-epoch language-model pretraining may leave the corpus in object storage and optimize the streaming path instead. The running example stages anyway, although it reads its corpus only once, because egress is not what decides it. Each node’s share of the shards fits easily on local NVMe, the staging copy happens once before the clock starts, and local reads keep object-store latency tails out of every training step. When compute and bucket share a region, transfer fees largely disappear and the same comparison turns on request charges, latency, and idle accelerators instead. The architecture is therefore chosen from access pattern, read count, and latency sensitivity rather than from $/GB/month alone.

The cost analysis extends to the parallel file system tier, which occupies a middle ground between NVMe and object storage in both performance and price. A parallel file system capable of delivering 1 TB/s aggregate bandwidth requires roughly 100 OSS nodes, each with multiple NVMe drives and high-bandwidth network connections. Because the throughput target, not the stored capacity, sets that server count, a bandwidth-sized system costs far more than its stored bytes alone would suggest. This expense is justified only if the alternative, streaming from object storage and paying egress fees, is even more expensive, or if the latency requirements of checkpoint writes cannot be met by object storage alone. The break-even analysis depends on the organization’s workload mix. A team running a single long training job may find that staging data on local NVMe and bypassing the parallel file system entirely is the cheapest option, while a team running many concurrent short jobs benefits from the shared namespace that a parallel file system provides.

The decision to build vs. buy storage infrastructure hinges on the trade-off between the high capital expenditure of an on-premises parallel file system and the recurring operational expenditure of cloud object storage. A 1-petabyte Lustre deployment, sized for capacity rather than for the 1 TB/s target above, costs roughly $3–5 million in hardware, plus an additional $500,000 per year in operational overhead for power, cooling, and engineering support. Storing 1 PB in a cloud object store at $0.02/GB/month costs $240,000 per year for storage alone. Egress fees for accessing the data, however, dominate the total cost. Reading that petabyte at 10 reads/year adds $900,000 in data transfer fees. Under these assumptions, annual cloud delivery costs $1,140,000; after subtracting $500,000 of on-premises operations, the net savings are $640,000 per year. The resulting hardware break-even is roughly 4.7–7.8 years, and a payback within two years would require a hotter workload with many more full-dataset reads per year, lower capital expense, or non-egress drivers such as checkpoint latency and shared-namespace requirements. For the running example, the 6 TB serialized corpus is much smaller than 1 PB, so the local-storage case is driven more by latency, repeated experiments, and checkpoint staging than by one-year egress savings alone. Read-heavy training fleets also rarely wear out their drives under the DWPD budget of section 4.3.4, so a storage refresh cycle is usually driven by density rather than wear, and its timing belongs to the lifecycle cost analysis of Depreciation and lifecycle.

4.8.1 Tiering strategies

The cost structure laid out above turns tiering into a placement policy: keep data only as close to the accelerator as its access frequency and latency requirement justify. A tiering strategy encodes that policy across the hierarchy. Its hot, warm, cold, and archive labels name placement roles for a dataset rather than devices, and they follow the scope brackets of figure 1. The node-local pair is the hot tier, and the three shared services become the warm, cold, and archive tiers. The tier sections called local NVMe a warm cache because it sat in front of shared storage; in this placement vocabulary the same drives belong to the hot tier, because they hold the data the running job consumes.

The fastest and most expensive layer is the hot tier (local NVMe + host DRAM), which holds data being actively consumed by the running training job. Datasets are staged from shared storage to local NVMe at job start. This tier is provisioned per-node and is not shared across the cluster, so data that multiple jobs or teams need simultaneously cannot live here alone.

That shared-access requirement is what justifies the warm tier (parallel file system), which holds datasets accessed by multiple jobs or teams, shared checkpoints for fault recovery, and model artifacts under active development. The parallel file system provides the shared namespace necessary for multi-tenant access with strong consistency.

Below the warm tier sits the cold tier (object storage), which serves as the canonical repository for all organizational training data. Datasets are authored and versioned in object storage, then promoted to warmer tiers when needed for training. Object storage also serves as the durable backup for checkpoints after they are staged from local NVMe through the parallel file system.

Data that must be retained for compliance or reproducibility but is not expected to be accessed during normal operations drops to the archive tier (Glacier or equivalent). Lifecycle policies of the kind shown for the archive tier in section 4.3.8 automate both directions of movement. They promote data from cold to warm when a job requests it, demote it when no job has read it for a configured period, and transition cold data to archive after 90 to 365 days since last access.

In the running example, the 3 TB source corpus and 6 TB serialized shards live permanently in object storage (cold tier), and the staging and cleanup lifecycle of section 4.5.1 moves each node’s shards through local NVMe (hot tier) for the life of the job. The parallel file system holds only the job’s recent checkpoints, which the retention policy of section 4.6.2 governs.

The economic benefit of automated tiering compounds over time. An organization retaining 50 training datasets of 10 TB each would need 500 TB of parallel file system capacity if all data were left in place. If ten datasets are active concurrently and the other forty can be demoted after their jobs finish, automated tiering reduces the dataset capacity required on the parallel file system to 100 TB, saving $144,000 per year at the parallel file system’s per-TB rate. These savings are invisible in any single experiment but substantial when accumulated across the fleet. Tiering and staging thus settle where training data and checkpoints live and what each placement costs; serving reuses the same hierarchy for a different objective, governed by the time to bring a model into HBM and the latency of serving-time state rather than epoch throughput.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team trains a vision model on a \(50\text{ TB}\) dataset stored in cloud object storage (\(\$0.02/\text{GB-month}\)) for 20 epochs across 4 training runs per year. Reading from object storage across a network boundary incurs a \(\$0.09/\text{GB}\) data egress fee. Staging the dataset onto local NVMe caches costs \(\$0.10/\text{GB-month}\). What does the economic analysis reveal about streaming vs. staging?

    1. Streaming directly from object storage every epoch is cheaper because local NVMe drives add unnecessary provisioned storage overhead.
    2. Egress fees are negligible compared to monthly storage costs, making tier selection purely a matter of operational convenience.
    3. Staging data once to local NVMe per run saves roughly \(\$282,000\) per year compared to streaming from object storage every epoch, breaking even after just 5 epochs per run.
    4. The team should migrate the dataset to cold tape archive to eliminate both egress fees and provisioned storage costs.

  2. In a 1,000-GPU cluster where compute time is billed at \(\$2.00\) per GPU-hour, an undersized storage system introduces an additional 20 percentage points of input stalls (reducing the active compute fraction from \(90\%\) to \(70\%\)). Calculate the daily cost of this lost compute capacity and explain why investing in high-performance storage is economically rational.

  3. An enterprise evaluates building an on-premises 1 PB parallel file system (\(\$3\text{M to } \$5\text{M}\) capex plus \(\$500\text{k/year}\) opex) versus storing the 1 PB corpus in cloud object storage (\(\$240\text{k/year}\) storage) and paying \(\$0.09/\text{GB}\) egress fees for 10 full-dataset reads per year (\(\$900\text{k/year}\) egress). What is the realistic financial payback horizon for building the on-premises system under these parameters?

    1. Between 4.7 and 7.8 years, because net annual cloud delivery savings (\(\$640\text{k/year}\)) require multiple years to amortize the multi-million dollar initial hardware expenditure.
    2. Less than 6 months, because cloud egress fees make any on-premises storage investment instantly profitable.
    3. Exactly 20 years, because on-premises operational overhead exceeds all cloud storage and egress fees combined.
    4. There is no payback horizon because cloud object storage is perpetually free for research and training workloads.

  4. In cloud-based ML infrastructure, the recurring fee charged by providers per gigabyte of data transferred out of an object storage bucket across region or internet boundaries is called an ____ fee.

See Answers →

4.9 Storage for Serving

Where full-corpus training streams datasets for weeks and writes checkpoints in bursts, the weight-resident serving baseline of section 4.2 reads the model weights once when a replica starts or recovers and then answers requests out of HBM, before KV-cache offload and retrieval indexes add serving-time I/O of their own.

That startup read makes model loading latency, often called cold-start time, the primary storage metric for weight-resident inference: the duration required to load a model from storage into the accelerator’s HBM. For the 175B parameter language model, the weights alone occupy 350 GB in FP16 format; loading that footprint sequentially from a single high-performance NVMe drive at 14 GB/s takes 25 seconds, an unacceptable delay for a user-facing application. Loading from a shared PFS at a per-node rate of 4 GB/s takes 87.5 seconds. Reading from object storage at 1 GB/s stretches the delay to 5.8 minutes.

To reduce cold-start time, the model is sharded and loaded in parallel. If the 350 GB model is striped across 8 NVMe drives, each loading a 43.75 GB shard at 7 GB/s, the total load time drops to roughly 6.2 seconds. When using tensor parallelism across 8 GPUs, each GPU loads only its 43.75 GB shard. If the weights already sit in host DRAM, each GPU’s own PCIe link could move its shard in about 0.7 seconds, but the 8 transfers share the node’s 200 GB/s of host-memory bandwidth, so the full load takes about 1.8 seconds. For the most latency-sensitive applications, organizations maintain warm replicas: one or more copies of the model weights kept preloaded in host DRAM, ready for that host-memory-bound transfer. This trades DRAM capacity for a load of about 1.8 seconds instead of the 6.2 seconds striped NVMe read.

↪ How cold-start time limits autoscaling, and how warm pools trade idle capacity for scaling response, is developed in Autoscaling and Capacity Control.

These load times describe one replica. The model rollout previewed in section 4.2 asks all 100 replicas to load a new version at once, 35 TB of weights in one burst. If every replica reads from object storage at the 1 GB/s single-client rate, the fleet asks the store for 100 GB/s. A store whose aggregate service matches that figure, the 100 GB/s of table 2, finishes the rollout in about 5.8 minutes, no slower than one replica’s cold start; any smaller aggregate share stretches the rollout in proportion. Reading from the 1 TB/s parallel file system instead would take about 35 seconds, but only by claiming the whole file system and starving every training job that shares it, the read-side mirror of a checkpoint storm. Serving fleets therefore stage the new version on local NVMe before switching traffic, roll the update across groups of replicas so that the old version keeps serving, or let replicas that have finished loading supply the rest.

During autoregressive generation, the server keeps a per-request record of the attention keys and values produced by earlier tokens so it can generate the next token without recomputing the entire prefix. This record, called the key-value cache (KV cache), grows with sequence length and can consume significant HBM. For long sequences (128K tokens or more), KV cache offloading to host DRAM or NVMe extends the effective context window at the cost of increased latency per generated token, because cache blocks must be moved back into HBM before attention can use them. The storage lesson is that inference maintains an active working set rather than presenting solely a model-loading demand. When serving must also retrieve external context to ground that generation, the storage pipeline faces an entirely different access pattern: low-latency random queries into high-dimensional vector indexes.

↪ KV-cache growth and the HBM capacity wall it creates for serving are developed in The KV cache wall and memory capacity.

4.9.1 Retrieval indexes

An inference request that needs supporting documents cannot wait for a sequential scan of the training corpus. A retrieval system first converts documents into vectors that represent semantic meaning, then finds nearby vectors and returns their source documents as context. The same storage analysis still applies, but the unit of demand becomes a query and its index accesses, and the deadline becomes the serving latency budget. With a graph-based approximate nearest-neighbor (ANN) index, the storage system traverses a high-dimensional vector graph instead of streaming sequential blobs via formats such as TFRecord or Parquet. Other index families shift the storage demand elsewhere. Exact flat search scans every stored vector and is bound by scan bandwidth, inverted-file (IVF) partitioning probes only the few clusters nearest the query, and product quantization (PQ) compresses each vector into short codes so that more of the index fits in memory, at some cost in accuracy.

Systems Perspective 4.4: The IOPS bottleneck in high-dimensional search
For graph-based ANN, this architectural pattern shifts the primary storage bottleneck from sequential read bandwidth to random-access IOPS. The search traverses a high-dimensional graph to find approximate nearest neighbors across billions of embeddings. Its performance is governed by the trade-off among recall, latency, and capacity, where recall is the fraction of the true nearest neighbors that the approximate search returns. Raising recall requires traversing a larger portion of the index, driving up IOPS and latency. The arithmetic of a disk-resident traversal shows why IOPS, not bytes, binds. Each hop that misses DRAM costs one dependent SSD read of roughly 100 μs (table 2), and the next hop cannot start until that read returns, so ten such hops already spend a millisecond of the serving budget in storage alone. Throughput is bounded separately. A drive whose 4 KB random reads run near 0.5 GB/s (section 4.2) completes on the order of \(10^5\) such reads per second, and every concurrent query draws on that same budget, while each read moves only a few kilobytes. A 1,536-dimension embedding in FP32 (4 bytes per value) consumes approximately 6.1 KB, so a billion-document corpus requires roughly 6.1 TB before graph adjacency lists. If those lists add 50 to 100 bytes per node, they add 50 GB to 100 GB, making raw vectors plus minimal graph metadata about 6.2 TB at the high end. Production indexes can be larger once identifiers, graph levels, allocator overhead, deleted-entry slack, and replication are included. In-memory hierarchical navigable small world graph indexes make graph navigation fast when the index fits in DRAM (Malkov and Yashunin 2020). Disk-backed graph indexes such as DiskANN trade DRAM footprint against SSD traversal while targeting high recall and low query latency (Subramanya et al. 2019).

Subramanya, Suhas Jayaram, Fnu Devvrit, Harsha Vardhan Simhadri, Ravishankar Krishnawamy, and Rohan Kadekodi. 2019. “DiskANN: Fast Accurate Billion-Point Nearest Neighbor Search on a Single Node.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 32.
Malkov, Yury A., and Dmitry A. Yashunin. 2020. “Efficient and Robust Approximate Nearest Neighbor Search Using Hierarchical Navigable Small World Graphs.” IEEE Transactions on Pattern Analysis and Machine Intelligence 42 (4): 824–36. https://doi.org/10.1109/TPAMI.2018.2889473.

Graph-based retrieval therefore spends storage budget on index levels that keep random traversal inside the serving latency service-level objective. A disk-backed graph is economical only if the extra SSD accesses do not erase the quality gain from searching a larger corpus. Flat, IVF, and PQ designs move that budget toward scan bandwidth, partition probes, or decompression and approximation error. The same hierarchy now supports bulk model loading, request-scoped KV state, and retrieval, each with a distinct service-rate contract. Every design so far also assumes today’s six tiers, and the boundaries between those tiers are themselves moving.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A serving node must load the \(350\text{ GB}\) of FP16 weights of a 175B-parameter model into accelerator memory. Which source gives the shortest cold start under the chapter’s stated rates?

    1. Eight Gen4 NVMe drives read in parallel, each supplying one \(43.75\text{ GB}\) shard at \(7\text{ GB/s}\), for about 6.2 seconds.
    2. A single Gen5 NVMe drive at \(14\text{ GB/s}\), for about 25 seconds.
    3. The node’s \(4\text{ GB/s}\) share of the parallel file system, for about 87.5 seconds.
    4. A single object-storage client at \(1\text{ GB/s}\), for about 5.8 minutes.

  2. A warm replica keeps the \(350\text{ GB}\) of weights in host DRAM. Each GPU’s own host link could move its \(43.75\text{ GB}\) shard in about 0.7 seconds, yet the full load takes about 1.8 seconds. Explain why.

  3. A serving fleet of 100 replicas of a 175B-parameter model deploys a new version, so all replicas must read \(35\text{ TB}\) of FP16 weights at once. Which statement correctly evaluates the shared sources?

    1. A \(100\text{ GB/s}\) object store finishes in about 5.8 minutes, while the \(1\text{ TB/s}\) parallel file system would take about 35 seconds but only by claiming the whole file system from co-tenant training jobs.
    2. The parallel file system is always the right source because 35 seconds is shorter, and serving reads cannot interfere with training jobs.
    3. The object store finishes in about 35 seconds because each replica reads only its own share of the weights.
    4. Rollout time is independent of the source tier because each replica’s load is limited by its own PCIe link.

  4. How does the storage performance bottleneck for a graph-based vector retrieval index at billion-vector scale contrast with the primary bottleneck of an ML training data loader?

    1. Vector retrieval is bottlenecked by sequential streaming bandwidth (GB/s), whereas training data loaders are bottlenecked by random \(4\text{ KB}\) read IOPS.
    2. Vector retrieval requires continuous multi-terabyte burst writes, whereas training data loaders perform purely read-only operations.
    3. Vector retrieval is strictly compute-bound on matrix multiplication units and generates zero storage device interaction.
    4. Vector retrieval traverses high-dimensional graph structures requiring high random-access IOPS and low point-lookup latency, whereas training data loaders require sustained high-bandwidth sequential streaming.

  5. True or False: In graph-based approximate nearest-neighbor search such as HNSW or DiskANN, increasing target recall from \(90\%\) to \(99\%\) generally requires exploring more graph nodes, increasing random storage accesses and query latency.

  6. A billion-entry vector index of 1536-dimensional FP32 embeddings consumes roughly \(6.1\text{ TB}\) of raw vector data plus graph adjacency overhead. Explain why systems migrate from pure in-memory indexes (HNSW) to disk-backed graph indexes (DiskANN), and identify the primary hardware trade-off involved.
