4.10 Storage Technologies That Move Tiers

Today’s six-tier hierarchy leaves a 1,000× latency cliff between host DRAM (~100 ns) and local NVMe (~100 μs), forces the host CPU to spend cycles decompressing bytes that GPUDirect Storage could otherwise DMA directly, and pauses training while synchronous checkpoints reach flash. Compute Express Link (CXL), the coherent capacity tier of Compute express link (CXL), targets the DRAM-to-NVMe gap first. Seen from storage, CXL-attached memory is a “Tier 1.5,” slower than host DRAM but byte-addressable, with access latencies of roughly 200 to 500 ns against the roughly 100 μs of an NVMe read. It offers a capacity pool for the structures that the Tier 1 discussion showed competing for host DRAM. For the running example, CXL-attached memory could expand the effective host memory tier from 512 GB to over 4 TB per node. That pool could hold deeper prefetch queues, embedding-table spillover, or a local shard cache as large as the 3 TB compressed source corpus without paying NVMe access latency on every read, though it still falls short of the full 6 TB tokenized training copy on one node.

A different bottleneck appears when CPU-side decoding, not storage bandwidth, throttles the pipeline. The computational storage drive closes that gap by embedding processing elements (field-programmable gate arrays (FPGAs) or simple CPU cores) directly on the SSD controller, enabling decompression, filtering, or format conversion to happen at the storage device rather than consuming host CPU cycles. For ML pipelines where CPU-side decoding is the bottleneck, computational storage could eliminate the CPU from the data path entirely, complementing GDS by offloading work that direct DMA cannot address.

Checkpoint writes impose the pause \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) while a consistent snapshot reaches the first storage tier, and shrinking it makes frequent saves less costly. Persistent memory, despite market shifts such as Intel’s 2022 wind-down of its Optane memory business (Intel Corporation 2022), remains the architectural idea that attacks that term directly. Byte-addressable non-volatile memory could lower the destination-device latency, but the checkpoint pause still includes snapshot creation, byte transfer, and coordination. A lower device-access latency reduces \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) only to the extent that this device lies on the pause’s critical path.

Intel Corporation. 2022. Form 10-q for the Quarterly Period Ended July 2, 2022.

These technologies are not yet broadly deployed at the scale of the largest ML clusters, but they illustrate the direction of the storage hierarchy’s evolution. The fundamental principle remains unchanged, with faster, more expensive storage closer to the accelerator and slower, cheaper storage at the periphery. What changes is the granularity of the tiers and the size of the gaps between them.

4.11 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Storage design errors are among the most expensive mistakes in ML infrastructure because they are discovered late, when training begins, and remediation requires either expensive hardware upgrades or time-consuming data reformatting. The following fallacies and pitfalls capture the most common errors that experienced storage engineers make when first encountering ML workloads.

Fallacy: NVMe is fast enough to feed GPUs directly without pipelining.

A single NVMe drive delivers 7 GB/s, while an H100 consumes 3.35 TB/s from HBM. The gap is roughly 479×. Even four drives in RAID-0 only close this gap to about 120× before overhead. Without pipelining and prefetching to hide the latency of loading from NVMe to host DRAM to HBM, every batch transfer introduces a stall equal to the transfer time. The HBM ratio measures tier disparity rather than the required input rate, because model state is reused many times for each batch loaded from storage. The relevant test compares measured input delivery with batch bytes divided by compute time, then checks whether buffering hides transfer latency and jitter. A device can have enough sustained bandwidth and still expose stalls when transfers are serialized with computation.

Pitfall: Relying on prefetching to hide object storage latency.

Prefetching hides latency only within the time covered by ready batches; it cannot hide an arbitrarily long interruption. With \(\PrefetchBufferCount\) batches ready and constant compute time, a complete interruption of supply exhausts the queue after \(\PrefetchBufferCount \times \IronLawComputeTime\). A slow request need not stall computation if other completed work remains available, but correlated delays can deplete many workers’ queues together. Provision buffer depth from measured delivery tails, test correlated interruptions, and verify that the sustained refill rate meets consumption. Local staging can reduce remote exposure when the required shards fit and remain valid.

Fallacy: Millions of small training files are harmless at scale.

Storing each training sample as an individual file (one JPEG per image, one JSON per text sample) creates repeated per-file work through operations such as open(), stat(), and close(). Metadata becomes the bottleneck when the resulting request rate exceeds the service’s capacity, even if byte bandwidth remains available. Aggregating samples into sequential shards amortizes that work (section 4.3.5). Packaging 10,000 to 100,000 samples per shard can reduce file-level operations by a corresponding 10,000 to 100,000\(\times\) when readers consume whole shards. The loader must still implement the intended sampling policy and avoid repeated enumeration.

Pitfall: Ignoring egress costs when designing the storage architecture.

Cloud egress costs are invisible during development but can dominate at scale. Under a representative $0.09/GB cross-boundary egress fee, a training job that reads a 100 TB dataset from object storage 10 times incurs $90,000 in egress fees, far exceeding the annual storage cost of $24,000 under the object-storage price assumption in section 4.3.7. These fees apply when reads cross a region, provider, or internet boundary; same-region reads may be free or discounted, which is exactly why the cost stays hidden until a cross-region or multi-cloud topology makes it unavoidable. Teams that prototype with small datasets on object storage and then scale up discover that their storage architecture is economically unsustainable. The fix is to budget egress costs explicitly and to design the data pipeline to minimize cross-tier transfers, typically by staging data on local NVMe at job start rather than streaming from object storage each epoch.

Fallacy: Average write bandwidth is enough for checkpoint design.

Checkpoints are written by all nodes simultaneously, creating bursts that can exceed steady-state bandwidth by 10\(\times\) or more. When the PFS write lies on the training critical path, sizing it only for average write load extends \(\CheckpointWriteTime\) during bursts and reduces effective training throughput. A synchronous PFS-direct design must provision enough burst bandwidth to meet its pause budget. With local staging, the first tier absorbs the burst and the PFS drains it in the background; the shared tier must then meet the durability deadline and keep up with repeated saves without an unbounded backlog.

Pitfall: Assuming faster accelerators automatically improve training throughput.

When an organization upgrades from A100 to H100 GPUs, they expect training time to decrease proportionally to the compute improvement. If the storage pipeline was already marginal on A100s, delivering data just fast enough to avoid stalls, the faster H100 compute hits the I/O wall sooner. The accelerator upgrade reduces \(\IronLawComputeTime\) without reducing \(\StepIoTime\), which means the data stall ratio increases. Every accelerator upgrade must be accompanied by a storage pipeline audit to ensure that the faster compute does not expose a previously hidden storage bottleneck.

Fallacy: RAID configurations optimized for databases are suitable for ML training.

RAID selection follows the tier’s failure contract. A disposable local cache can use RAID 0 to aggregate drives without parity, provided a durable source remains available and the fleet can tolerate restaging after a drive failure. Redundant layouts can still reduce disruption when restaging is slow or the cache supports an availability objective. A local checkpoint awaiting durable replication is not yet disposable merely because it lives on the same NVMe drives. Treating all ML storage as replaceable input data confuses capacity placement with recovery policy.

Pitfall: Creating full copies for each dataset snapshot.

Copying an entire dataset for every version duplicates unchanged content. For a 10 TB dataset with 5 percent of its bytes changed, a full copy retains 9.5 TB of redundant data. Content-addressable storage or delta encoding can reuse unchanged objects or blocks. If the storage granularity isolates the changed bytes, the new payload is roughly 500 GB, a 20× reduction before metadata and redundancy overhead. Rewriting large compressed shards can reduce that saving because a small sample change may replace a much larger stored object. Versioning must therefore preserve lineage while choosing a reuse granularity compatible with the dataset format.

Fallacy: Compression always helps because it reduces the amount of data to read.

Compression reduces stored and transferred bytes but adds decoding work. If decoding further saturates an already limiting host processor, it makes that bottleneck worse, not better. If transfer is limiting and decoding has headroom, compression can improve delivery until another stage becomes the limit. Compare candidate representations using output-equivalent stage rates, including CPU or GPU decoding, rather than select the strongest codec from an I/O-bound label alone. The same choice can reduce capacity cost without improving training throughput. These fallacies all return to the same accounting exercise: where each byte lives, how often it moves, and which tier pays the cost.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A team selects RAID 5 for a disposable local training-data cache whose immutable source is durably stored elsewhere. The fleet can tolerate restaging after drive failure. Why might RAID 0 be a better throughput-and-capacity trade-off under this failure contract?

    1. RAID 5 cannot be formatted with modern Linux filesystems such as ext4 or XFS.
    2. RAID 5 disables hardware DMA transfers, preventing the GPU from accessing host DRAM.
    3. The cache can be reconstructed from its durable source, so RAID 0 can aggregate drives without parity overhead when restaging disruption is acceptable.
    4. RAID 5 increases drive endurance wear by \(10\times\) compared to single-drive configurations.

  2. A training team upgrades its cluster from A100 to H100 GPUs, expecting a \(3\times\) end-to-end training speedup. However, wall-clock epoch time barely improves, and GPU utilization drops from \(85\%\) to \(35\%\). Traces show shorter uninterrupted compute time, unchanged measured input-delivery rate, and increased time waiting for ready input batches, while communication and checkpoint pauses remain unchanged. Diagnose the measured bottleneck and explain why faster compute exposed it.

  3. A team replaces a lighter codec with a denser codec on local-NVMe training shards. On this corpus, illustrative measurements give the lighter codec a 3:1 ratio and 1.5 GB/s decoded output per core, versus 4:1 and 0.5 GB/s for the denser codec. Before the change, worker profiles show decoding and augmentation saturating the host CPU and the ready-batch queue repeatedly draining. Afterward, decompression consumes more CPU time, the queue drains more often, and training step time increases although stored bytes decrease. What caused this measured regression?

    1. Compressed shards cannot be read by NVMe controllers because flash drives require raw uncompressed binary blocks.
    2. The pipeline was already CPU-bound on data decoding and augmentation, so adding heavy decompression work saturated the host CPU, starving the GPU of assembled batches.
    3. The denser codec introduces non-deterministic bit flips that cause stochastic gradient descent to diverge.
    4. The parallel file system rejects compressed shards larger than 1 GB due to POSIX file locking constraints.

  4. True or False: Sizing a data loader’s prefetch buffer depth based on average storage read latency ensures that training runs will not experience data stalls during multi-week training jobs on shared object storage.

  5. A team creates full \(10\text{ TB}\) copies of a pretraining dataset for every snapshot version, creating 20 versions over six months (\(200\text{ TB}\) total storage). Explain how content-addressable storage or delta encoding optimizes this workflow when successive versions differ by only \(5\%\).

See Answers →

4.12 Summary

Storage in ML systems is an active, multi-tiered pipeline that supplies accelerator inputs, preserves restorable state, and records the lineage of training and serving artifacts. The hierarchy exists because workloads need different combinations of bandwidth, capacity, latency, durability, and cost, and the demand on it grows with the scaling laws that drive the fleet. Larger models call for more training tokens and carry larger checkpoints, so storage pressure rises at every step up the model-size curve. The data pipeline throughput equation, \(\RequiredInputBandwidth = \InputConsumerAcceleratorCount \times \TargetComputeTimeFraction\times \BatchDataVolume / \IronLawComputeTime\), sizes delivery for a chosen training rate when applied to the representation and distinct input consumers at each tier. Measured against the Fleet Law, storage succeeds when it contributes no exposed time. Prefetching preserves the Fleet Law’s assumption that the next batch is already resident. Tiered checkpoint staging shrinks the save pause that surfaces in \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\) to the local write and takes the slow shared-storage copy off the step entirely, so that copy costs step time only when it contends with collectives or input reads. Both spend communication (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_2\)) and coordination (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_3\)) capacity to protect compute (\(\OperationalTaxonomyDimension_1\)).

Treating the hierarchy as a cache makes that sizing workload-specific. The hit rate and the service times of the hot and cold paths determine mean effective throughput, while the tail and correlation of misses determine whether a finite prefetch buffer drains. At the durable tiers, reliability also separates into three contracts: data survival, service response, and the version a reader may observe. These are durability, availability, and consistency, respectively; improving one does not automatically establish the others.

The diagnostic task is to identify which service contract fails before changing the storage tier. Low accelerator utilization can arise from metadata-intensive loading, a depleted prefetch queue, an exposed checkpoint write, or host-memory traffic crossing a contended NUMA link. Measure input wait separately from communication and synchronization before attributing it to storage. Full-corpus scans favor aggregated shards, while graph retrieval and live features require random access and explicit consistency. A depleted input queue can reflect slow storage, metadata contention, or insufficient decoding capacity; GDS helps when host payload copying is the limiting stage while retaining CPU control. Compare remedies by total delivery cost, including transfers and idle accelerators. For checkpoint writes, evaluate the shorter training pause together with the time until a complete generation becomes durable and publishable.

Key Takeaways: Feed the accelerators or waste them
  • Workload demand sizes the delivery path: Use \(\RequiredInputBandwidth = \InputConsumerAcceleratorCount \times \TargetComputeTimeFraction\times \BatchDataVolume / \IronLawComputeTime\) for the chosen training rate, then check each tier’s service rate and buffering (principle 8).
  • Workload determines the storage contract: Full-corpus scans favor sequential throughput and aggregated shards, and metadata operations can bind before byte bandwidth does (principle 7). Disk-backed graph retrieval must fit its random I/O within a serving deadline, and synthetic-data pipelines must budget verification traffic separately from retained capacity while keeping the versioned lineage of every accepted sample.
  • Tail latency sets the cache budget: A high hot-tier hit rate is insufficient when cold misses are orders of magnitude slower. Use equation 1 as a mean baseline, then size prefetch buffers from p99 I/O latency and test correlated miss bursts against the compute window.
  • Direct storage removes host payload copies: GDS retains CPU submission and coordination while bypassing host bounce buffers. The illustrative example assumes 75 percent less active CPU time per request; the benefit depends on whether payload copying limits the workload.
  • Checkpoint recovery is a storage protocol: Local NVMe staging can shorten pause time, but a restorable checkpoint also needs durable publication, a committed manifest, and verified shards; durability, availability, and consistency remain distinct contracts.
  • Pipeline correctness lives at commit boundaries: Replay preserves processing completeness only when stable identifiers and idempotent or transactional writes prevent duplicate effects. Event-time watermarks then make the separate freshness-completeness trade-off explicit.
  • Economics drive tiering: The 5× local-NVMe/object-storage price ratio illustrates the incentive to keep data on cheaper tiers under the stated pricing assumptions (principle 8). Compare total delivery cost, including transfer charges and idle accelerators, before deciding where to stage it.

Checkpoint staging shows the C\(^3\) trade-off in one design decision. Writing first to local NVMe shortens the compute pause, but asynchronous replication adds communication traffic and requires coordination to publish only complete generations. Once the shared-file-system write leaves the critical path, the bottleneck can move to local flash endurance, peer-replication bandwidth, or manifest commit latency. Measuring only the shorter training pause would miss the new recovery boundary.

What’s Next: From storage to communication
Compute silicon, network fabrics, and storage hierarchies establish the physical resources of the machine learning fleet. Coordinating those resources into one training computation remains a separate problem. This chapter’s 175B running model already exceeds, in its FP16 weights alone, the 80 GB device of the HBM placement example, so its state and work must span devices, and every division of that work obliges workers to exchange gradients, activations, or parameters across the fabric. Part II moves from physical infrastructure to distributed execution. It begins with the collective operations that carry those exchanges and the cost of each, then partitions neural networks across data, tensor, pipeline, and sequence dimensions, before turning to the hardware failures that a fleet must survive.

↪ The collective operations that carry gradient and activation exchanges, and the cost models that price them, are developed in Collective Communication.

4.13 Self-Check Answers

Self-Check: Answer

  1. An H100 accelerator consumes data from its HBM at roughly \(3.35\text{ TB/s}\), while a single local NVMe drive delivers sequential reads at roughly \(7\text{ GB/s}\). A team is designing the storage infrastructure for a 1,000-accelerator training fleet. Which conclusion follows directly from this bandwidth ratio?

    1. The gap is easily bridged by standard NVMe RAID arrays because sequential read throughput scales without bound across drives.
    2. The roughly \(479\times\) device-bandwidth gap motivates tiered staging, but required input bandwidth must be calculated from batch bytes and compute time rather than equated with HBM traffic.
    3. The bandwidth gap is negligible because large-scale training is entirely compute-bound and rarely exercises HBM bandwidth.
    4. The bandwidth gap disappears when high-speed InfiniBand fabrics deliver data directly into HBM from remote storage endpoints without local staging.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. HBM and NVMe serve different traffic: model state and intermediate tensors can be reused many times for each input batch. The roughly \(479\times\) ratio illustrates tier disparity, not a storage-rate requirement. Batch bytes, compute time, concurrency, and buffering determine whether inputs arrive in time; neither RAID aggregation nor a faster network alone establishes that condition.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish storage input demand from internal HBM traffic when interpreting the bandwidth gap

  2. A 175B-parameter model trains for 30 days on 256 nodes, writing resumable mixed-precision Adam checkpoints of roughly \(2.45\text{ TB}\) (\(350\text{ GB}\) FP16 weights, \(700\text{ GB}\) FP32 master weights, and \(1.4\text{ TB}\) of Adam moments) every 10 minutes. Explain why ML storage design is fundamentally a joint physics-and-economics optimization problem.

    Answer: HBM supplies high local bandwidth for active tensors but cannot economically retain the corpus and checkpoint history. Shared durable storage pools capacity at lower cost, while interfaces, contention, and client allocation determine the rate and latency a consumer receives. Tier placement must satisfy input-delivery and checkpoint-recovery budgets at the lowest total delivery cost. Remote input reads can suffice when their sustained rate and buffered latency meet demand; local staging is useful when they do not. Compare capacity cost, transfers, and exposed accelerator wait rather than infer a stall from remote placement alone.

    Learning Objective: Analyze why ML storage architecture requires tiered placement that simultaneously satisfies bandwidth physics and capacity economics

  3. True or False: A cluster benchmark demonstrating that compute hardware achieves advertised peak TFLOP/s and network fabric achieves advertised bisection bandwidth is sufficient evidence that end-to-end training throughput will scale linearly with accelerator count.

    Answer: False. Isolated compute and fabric benchmarks evaluate floating-point throughput and inter-node communication in synthetic conditions without exercising the end-to-end data pipeline. Real training throughput requires continuously streaming input batches, loading model weights, and absorbing periodic checkpoint write storms; if the storage hierarchy cannot deliver input samples within the compute window (\(\IronLawComputeTime\)), accelerators sit idle regardless of peak compute capability.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate why compute and network benchmarks in isolation fail to guarantee scalable end-to-end training throughput without storage pipeline validation

  4. An accelerator upgrade reduces compute time per batch while the input representation and storage delivery time remain fixed. What is the primary systems consequence for training cluster design?

    1. Training pipelines automatically increase data compression ratios to prevent accelerator starvation without requiring architectural adjustments.
    2. Storage hierarchies can eliminate intermediate DRAM and NVMe caching tiers because modern network fabrics directly absorb the difference.
    3. Checkpoint intervals must be decreased proportionally to the compute growth to keep local NVMe buffers from overflowing.
    4. The compute window available to deliver each batch shrinks, so input I/O can become the bottleneck unless the measured delivery path keeps pace.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. Faster computation leaves less time to fetch the next batch. Overlap and prefetching hide latency within their buffering window, but cannot fix a sustained service-rate deficit. Automatic compression is not guaranteed, removing staging does not improve a slow source, and checkpoint frequency does not set input delivery throughput.

    Learning Objective: Explain the systems consequences of compute throughput scaling significantly faster than persistent storage bandwidth

  5. In large-scale distributed training, when hundreds of nodes simultaneously pause computation to write gigabytes of model weights and optimizer states to persistent storage, the resulting synchronized burst of write traffic is called a ____.

    Answer: The correct term is checkpoint storm. A checkpoint storm occurs when all data-parallel or model-parallel workers simultaneously flush their memory shards to local or remote storage, creating extreme write contention that must be absorbed via tiered staging (such as writing to local NVMe before asynchronously copying to a parallel file system).

    Learning Objective: Explain the technical term for synchronized cluster-wide write bursts generated during distributed training state saves

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A storage procurement team is sizing storage hardware for a cluster dedicated to pretraining foundation models on a \(200\text{ TB}\) immutable text corpus. Which purchasing decision represents the most severe misallocation of budget based on ML workload characteristics?

    1. Paying a premium for enterprise SSDs optimized for high \(4\text{ KB}\) random-read IOPS while under-provisioning sustained sequential read bandwidth (GB/s).
    2. Aggregating raw text samples into \(256\text{ MB}\) to \(4\text{ GB}\) contiguous binary shards prior to training to amortize file-system metadata operations.
    3. Provisioning local NVMe drives on compute nodes to serve as a warm read cache for multi-epoch training passes.
    4. Sizing the parallel file system write tier to absorb synchronized multi-terabyte checkpoint write bursts from hundreds of nodes.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Paying a premium for enterprise SSDs optimized for high \(4\text{ KB}\) random-read IOPS while under-provisioning sustained sequential read bandwidth (GB/s). ML pretraining streams large contiguous batches sequentially, where a drive delivers \(7\text{-}14\text{ GB/s}\), whereas \(4\text{ KB}\) random reads drop throughput to \(\approx 0.5\text{ GB/s}\); optimizing for random IOPS buys capabilities the workload never utilizes while starving the pipeline of raw gigabytes-per-second throughput. Shard aggregation is the standard mitigation against metadata serialization, local NVMe caching prevents repeated network egress, and sizing for checkpoint bursts directly addresses the bursty write inversion.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate storage procurement decisions against the sequential-streaming and bursty-write inversions of ML workloads

  2. True or False: An in-memory LRU cache sized to \(300\text{ GB}\) placed in front of a \(3\text{ TB}\) uniformly shuffled pretraining dataset will achieve roughly a \(90\%\) hit rate after the first epoch warms the cache because all samples are reused across epochs.

    Answer: False. A cache holding one-tenth of a uniformly shuffled corpus does not gain a 90 percent hit rate merely because samples recur in later epochs. Most cached samples are evicted before reuse. A fixed cyclic scan larger than the cache can approach zero hits; independently reshuffled epochs allow low but nonzero cross-epoch hits that depend on cache fraction and sample order.

    Learning Objective: Analyze why temporal-locality caching strategies such as LRU fail on uniformly streamed ML pretraining datasets

  3. Explain why ML data loaders implement shard-level shuffling combined with within-shard buffer mixing rather than true global per-sample random shuffling across petabyte-scale training corpora.

    Answer: Shard-level shuffling and within-shard buffer mixing preserve sequential I/O while introducing sample-order variation. Adequate mixing depends on shard composition and buffer size: homogeneous shards may require finer shards or larger mixing buffers. Validate the layout against the loss curve of a fully shuffled baseline on a subset before committing the run.

    Learning Objective: Justify shard-level shuffling under explicit mixing and convergence checks

  4. When an inference platform deploys a new version of a 175B-parameter model (\(350\text{ GB}\) FP16 weights) across 100 serving replicas, how does the storage access profile contrast with a training checkpoint save?

    1. It generates continuous small random writes to update parameter journals on each replica node.
    2. It streams an incremental delta of only modified parameters over hours to minimize bandwidth utilization.
    3. It creates a synchronized fan-out read burst of roughly \(35\text{ TB}\) (\(100 \times 350\text{ GB}\)) that must complete within the deployment latency budget while serving traffic continues.
    4. It requires sustained multi-gigabyte-per-second sequential write bandwidth to central durable object storage.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. It creates a synchronized fan-out read burst of roughly \(35\text{ TB}\) (\(100 \times 350\text{ GB}\)) that must complete within the deployment latency budget while serving traffic continues. A model rollout is the architectural mirror image of a checkpoint storm: instead of many nodes simultaneously writing state to storage (fan-in burst), many replica nodes simultaneously read the same large model weights from storage (fan-out burst). The small-random-writes option describes OLTP database journaling. The delta-streaming option assumes parameter-level differential rollouts that production serving frameworks avoid in favor of immutable weight images. The central-write option confuses read distribution to serving nodes with training checkpoint writes.

    Learning Objective: Classify model rollout storage traffic as a synchronized fan-out read burst and quantify its cluster-wide volume

  5. A team plans one storage configuration for the whole life of a language-model project: tokenizing and deduplicating a \(3\text{ TB}\) compressed corpus into \(6\text{ TB}\) of shards, then training on those shards with periodic checkpoints. Why does the chapter conclude that no single configuration optimizes every phase?

    1. Preprocessing reads and writes large sequential artifacts in MapReduce-like passes, while training reads immutable shards between bursty checkpoint writes, so the read/write balance and access pattern change by phase.
    2. Training reads are dominated by small random lookups that only a database-style random-IOPS tier can serve, while preprocessing is purely sequential.
    3. The tokenized corpus is smaller than the compressed source, so preprocessing frees capacity that the training phase cannot use.
    4. Checkpoint writes are continuous and small, so they require a different write-ahead log configuration than preprocessing.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. The fourth inversion is that the read/write ratio shifts by lifecycle phase: preprocessing scans and rewrites large artifacts, while training streams read-only shards between synchronized checkpoint bursts. Each phase loads the tiers differently, which is why ML systems use a multi-tier hierarchy rather than one storage technology. Training reads are not small random lookups, the tokenized corpus is larger than the compressed source, and checkpoints are bursty large writes, not continuous small ones.

    Learning Objective: Explain why phase-dependent read/write behavior requires a multi-tier storage hierarchy

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Order the six tiers from Tier 0 through Tier 5 in the chapter’s placement hierarchy, moving outward from accelerator-resident memory through node-local resources to shared storage and archive: (1) Local NVMe SSD, (2) Object Storage, (3) Host DRAM, (4) GPU HBM, (5) Parallel File System, (6) Archive / Cold Storage.

    Answer: The correct order is: (4) GPU HBM, (3) Host DRAM, (1) Local NVMe SSD, (5) Parallel File System, (2) Object Storage, (6) Archive / Cold Storage. HBM is accelerator-resident; host DRAM and local NVMe serve the node; the parallel file system provides shared access; object storage retains durable organizational data; and archive preserves rarely accessed history. This is the chapter’s placement order, not a universal bandwidth ranking. HBM rates are per accelerator, DRAM and local NVMe rates are per node, and shared-service rates must be allocated among clients and checked against the actual delivery path.

    Learning Objective: Order the six storage tiers by placement and distinguish that order from a bandwidth comparison

  2. Host DRAM (Tier 1) executes a four-stage data loader pipeline: Read, Decode, Augment, and Collate. Why place the final collated batch in page-locked (‘pinned’) host memory for direct asynchronous DMA to GPU HBM without an intermediate staging copy?

    1. Pinned memory automatically compresses tensors using host CPU vector units before PCIe transmission.
    2. Pinned memory locks physical pages in host RAM so the PCIe DMA controller can copy data directly to GPU memory without an intermediate pinned staging copy.
    3. Pinned memory replicates the batch across all NUMA nodes to ensure uniform memory access for parallel data loader threads.
    4. Pinned memory guarantees transactional ACID durability on host storage in case the worker process crashes during transfer.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Pinned memory locks physical pages in host RAM so the PCIe DMA controller can copy data directly to GPU memory without an intermediate pinned staging copy. Standard virtual memory pages can be paged out or relocated by the operating system, forcing the GPU driver to copy data into an intermediate pinned staging buffer before initiating DMA; allocating pinned memory directly (pin_memory=True in PyTorch) eliminates this extra CPU memory copy and allows direct DMA across PCIe. The compression option is incorrect as pinned memory is an OS page-locking mechanism, not a compression algorithm. The NUMA replication claim is false because pinning prevents paging rather than replicating data or choosing NUMA placement. The ACID durability option confuses volatile host DRAM staging with persistent transaction logging.

    Learning Objective: Explain the architectural function of pinned host memory in accelerating CPU-to-GPU DMA data transfers

  3. A vision training cluster migrating to a high-performance parallel file system (PFS) with \(500\text{ GB/s}\) rated aggregate bandwidth reports that throughput during ImageNet training is capped at under \(5\text{ GB/s}\), with storage data disks showing negligible I/O activity. The dataset consists of millions of individual JPEG files. A worker trace shows repeated waits for file opens, and the MDS reports a sustained operation queue with rising lookup latency while its service rate is saturated. What bottleneck do these additional measurements establish, and which fix addresses it?

    1. The network fabric is dropping packets due to congestion; enable flow control on top-of-rack switches.
    2. The parallel file system is bottlenecked on drive endurance wear-leveling; replace SSDs with high-DWPD enterprise drives.
    3. The dataset is stored as millions of individual image files, saturating the Metadata Server (MDS) with per-file POSIX lookups; repackage images into large sequential shards (such as WebDataset tar archives).
    4. The GPUs are compute-bound and cannot consume data faster; decrease the batch size to increase I/O request frequency.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. The saturated MDS service rate, rising lookup latency, and worker file-open waits establish a metadata bottleneck in this case. Repackaging images into sequential shards amortizes per-file operations across many samples. Idle data disks and low throughput alone would not establish this cause; the supplied metadata measurements distinguish it from a slow decoder or another blocked pipeline stage.

    Learning Objective: Analyze parallel file system metadata saturation from telemetry signatures and prescribe shard-aggregation remediation

  4. A training node uses four local NVMe drives configured in RAID-0 as a warm cache for \(25\text{ TB}\) of pre-shuffled training shards. Explain why RAID-0 is an appropriate engineering trade-off for this specific tier, and identify what workload change would make RAID-0 catastrophic.

    Answer: RAID-0 aggregates drive bandwidth without parity capacity overhead, making it appropriate for a disposable warm cache when a durable source remains available and the fleet can tolerate restaging after a drive failure. It does not protect availability: losing one drive can invalidate the array. If the array holds the only copy of an unrecoverable artifact or a checkpoint not yet durably replicated, a drive failure can permanently lose that state; the cache placement policy cannot substitute for a recovery contract.

    Learning Objective: Justify RAID-0 storage configurations for warm cache tiers and identify failure boundaries where lack of redundancy causes unrecoverable data loss

  5. A multi-modal training job combines \(3\text{ TB}\) of text, \(50\text{ TB}\) of images, and \(200\text{ TB}\) of video. The infrastructure team provisions storage bandwidth based exclusively on the video modality because video represents over \(75\%\) of total dataset capacity. What can be concluded about this sizing decision from the supplied information?

    1. Adequacy cannot be established from corpus capacity alone; sizing must include the combined sampled-batch byte rate and decoding, augmentation, and buffering demand of all active modalities.
    2. The video stream will starve the parallel file system because image and text files use incompatible POSIX stripe sizes.
    3. The model will fail to converge because object storage cannot maintain ACID consistency when serving multiple modalities simultaneously.
    4. The training job will exhaust GPU HBM because video tensors automatically overwrite text embeddings during collation.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Retained corpus capacity does not determine per-step delivery demand. Sampling frequency, batch representation, and compute time set how many bytes each modality contributes to the feed, while transformations determine its decoding and augmentation work. Sum those demands across active streams and compare them with measured stage rates and buffer capacity. Video-only sizing omits that accounting, but the supplied corpus sizes do not prove a particular storage, host-DRAM, or decoding bottleneck.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish retained corpus capacity from combined sampled-batch and transformation demand when sizing multimodal delivery

  6. In a parallel file system architecture like Lustre, namespace management (file creation, directory paths, locks) is handled by a dedicated ____, while raw data blocks are striped across multiple Object Storage Servers (OSS).

    Answer: The correct term is Metadata Server (or MDS). The separation of the Metadata Server (MDS) from Object Storage Servers (OSS) allows data bandwidth to scale linearly with the number of OSS nodes, though the MDS can become a bottleneck if datasets contain millions of small individual files.

    Learning Objective: Explain the architectural role of the Metadata Server (MDS) in parallel file systems

Self-Check: Answer

  1. The data pipeline throughput equation is \(\RequiredInputBandwidth = \InputConsumerAcceleratorCount \times \TargetComputeTimeFraction \times \frac{\BatchDataVolume}{\IronLawComputeTime}\). If a cluster doubles its number of GPUs consuming distinct local batches from 1,024 to 2,048 while keeping per-GPU batch size, uninterrupted compute time per batch, and target active-time fraction constant, how does the required aggregate storage bandwidth scale?

    1. It remains constant because data-parallel workers divide the fixed total dataset into smaller partitions.
    2. It quadruples (\(4\times\)) because collective communication overhead scales quadratically with worker count.
    3. It decreases by \(50\%\) because each node requires fewer samples per epoch.
    4. It doubles (\(2\times\)) because aggregate byte consumption scales strictly linearly with the number of accelerators independently executing forward-backward iterations.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. It doubles (\(2\times\)) because aggregate byte consumption scales strictly linearly with the number of accelerators independently executing forward-backward iterations. In the pipeline throughput equation, each data-parallel accelerator requires an independent batch \(\BatchDataVolume\) every compute window \(\IronLawComputeTime\), so doubling \(\InputConsumerAcceleratorCount\) exactly doubles the total bytes per second that the storage system must deliver to the fleet. The constant-bandwidth option confuses dataset size with per-iteration ingestion rate. The quadratic scaling option confuses inter-GPU all-reduce communication with storage data loader ingestion. The halving option misinterprets worker count scaling.

    Learning Objective: Apply the pipeline throughput equation to predict aggregate storage bandwidth requirements as cluster compute scales

  2. A training iteration has a GPU compute time of \(T_{\text{compute}} = 200\text{ ms}\) and an average I/O fetch time of \(T_{\text{I/O}} = 250\text{ ms}\) per batch. Explain why enabling double-buffering (pipelining batch \(i+1\) during batch \(i\) compute) reduces but cannot eliminate the data stall, and quantify the remaining stall time per step.

    Answer: Pipelining overlaps I/O with compute such that step time becomes \(T_{\text{step}} = \max(T_{\text{compute}}, T_{\text{I/O}}) = T_{\text{compute}} + \max(0, T_{\text{I/O}} - T_{\text{compute}})\). Because \(T_{\text{I/O}} (250\text{ ms}) > T_{\text{compute}} (200\text{ ms})\), the pipeline remains fundamentally I/O-bound: when the GPU completes its 200 ms forward-backward pass, the next batch is still 50 ms away from completing its transfer. Pipelining reduces step time from sequential execution (\(200 + 250 = 450\text{ ms}\)) to \(250\text{ ms}\), but an exposed stall of \(50\text{ ms}\) (a \(20\%\) stall ratio, calculated as \((250 - 200) / 250 \times 100\)) persists until storage bandwidth is increased or I/O volume is reduced.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the exposed data stall time when I/O fetch duration exceeds accelerator computation time under pipelining

  3. A data loader pipeline has an accelerator compute window of \(T_{\text{compute}} = 200\text{ ms}\) per batch. The storage tier exhibits median I/O latency of \(180\text{ ms}\) but experiences P99 tail-latency spikes of \(500\text{ ms}\) due to network jitter. What is the minimum recommended prefetch buffer depth (\(Q_{\text{prefetch}}\)) suggested by the P99 sizing rule, before allowing for longer or correlated stalls?

    1. At least 3 batches (\(Q_{\text{prefetch}} = \lceil 500 / 200 \rceil = 3\)) plus a safety margin, covering the stated P99 delay when the queue is full.
    2. Exactly 1 batch, because median I/O latency (\(180\text{ ms}\)) is strictly less than compute time (\(200\text{ ms}\)).
    3. Zero batches, because multi-worker asynchronous threading eliminates the need for host-memory queue buffering.
    4. At least 10 batches, because queue depth must equal the total number of CPU worker threads allocated to the data loader.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Three ready batches cover \(600\text{ ms}\) of computation, exceeding the stated \(500\text{ ms}\) P99 delay. This is a buffer-sizing baseline, not a guarantee: a longer or correlated outage can still drain the queue, and insufficient sustained delivery bandwidth cannot be fixed by buffering. The median and worker count alone do not bound the required coverage.

    Learning Objective: Calculate minimum prefetch buffer depth from P99 I/O latency and iteration compute duration

  4. True or False: If a cluster monitoring dashboard indicates that average storage read bandwidth matches the aggregate throughput predicted by the pipeline equation, storage performance issues can be conclusively ruled out as the cause of low GPU utilization.

    Answer: False. Average bandwidth metrics mask transient tail-latency spikes, worker-side lock contention, metadata serialization, and prefetch buffer underruns. Even if aggregate gigabytes-per-second over a 10-minute window matches the theoretical target, microsecond-level and millisecond-level I/O jitter can cause individual GPUs to stall repeatedly at step boundaries, driving down utilization. Diagnostics must evaluate per-step wait-for-data distributions (P95/P99 stall times) rather than aggregate bandwidth averages alone.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate why average bandwidth metrics fail to rule out storage bottlenecks without fine-grained tail-latency and stall-ratio profiling

  5. Explain how locality-aware scheduling reduces cluster startup latency and relieves bandwidth pressure on the shared parallel file system when a node fails during a 1,024-GPU training run.

    Answer: A locality-aware scheduler can place a replacement worker where the required immutable input shards already reside in local NVMe, avoiding their transfer from the shared file system and reducing recovery traffic. This accelerates the data-staging part of recovery; it does not eliminate checkpoint restoration, coordination, or process startup. Locality is useful only when the cached version is correct and the placement also satisfies the job’s resource constraints.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how locality-aware scheduling optimizes node recovery times and mitigates shared storage contention at fleet scale

  6. Compare the three primary data staging patterns (prestaging, on-demand staging, and pure streaming) in terms of job startup latency and network bandwidth consumption during multi-epoch training.

    Answer: Prestaging copies the required working set before training, trading startup delay and cache capacity for fewer remote input reads during execution. On-demand staging fetches shards on first access and can reuse them in later epochs if the cache retains them. Pure streaming avoids a full-dataset staging phase but repeatedly uses the remote path and remains exposed to its tail latency. Neither on-demand staging nor streaming eliminates first-batch latency, and local staging does not eliminate unrelated network traffic such as checkpoint writes.

    Learning Objective: Compare the trade-offs of prestaging, on-demand staging, and pure streaming data delivery patterns
